# NorthCUA Technical Report

NorthCUA Team, Shanghai Qiji Zhifeng Co., Ltd.

###### Abstract

Scaling computer-use agents from individual actions to complex software
work requires experience that connects decisions to complete outcomes.
We present NorthCUA, an integrated system for turbocharging computer-use
agents through experience at scale. Its Experience Flywheel supplies
diverse executable tasks to the NorthCUA Harness, which sustains long
interactions, records decisions, and checks task outcomes. The Flywheel
uses these records to curate verified trajectories. For unsolved tasks,
recursive self-improvement evolves reusable skills and preserves
verified milestones so later attempts build on earlier progress. Staged
Learning combines this experience with grounding data from the Flywheel
to develop GUI competence through mid-training and long-horizon
supervised fine-tuning. The policy then learns from fresh attempts in
the same Harness through joint desktop–web reinforcement learning,
closing the loop between experience production and policy improvement.
The resulting Nano, Mini, and Pro family achieves state-of-the-art
results among open-source models across GUI grounding, desktop
execution, and Web interaction under the reported evaluation settings
(Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")). For more
responsive Web workflows, NorthCUA-Ultrafast pairs direct action
selection with open-ended generation on pre-RL Nano, offering a measured
quality–latency tradeoff. Together, these advances establish NorthCUA as
a high-performing model family and a reusable foundation for agents that
carry complex software work from intent to completion.

## 1Introduction

Computer-use agents (CUAs) extend the capabilities of large language
models from generating responses to operating computers and automating
software tasks ([Zhang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib102); [Wang et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib81)). They can invoke software functions through
application programming interfaces (APIs) and command-line interfaces
(CLIs). Graphical user interfaces (GUIs) extend this access to functions
that applications do not expose programmatically, providing a critical
last mile for software automation ([Zhang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib101);
[Zhang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib103)). Through GUI interaction, agents
interpret the screen and translate natural-language goals into mouse and
keyboard actions. Advances in visual grounding and action generation
have expanded the range of operations these agents can perform ([Qin
et al., 2025](#bib.bib59); [Wang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib83); [Zhou
et al., 2025](#bib.bib108)). As agents combine these operations into
longer workflows, earlier actions shape the software state on which
later decisions depend. Reliable completion requires agents to preserve
intermediate progress and the user’s constraints, adapt to changing
interfaces, and recover from errors while keeping the overall goal in
view ([Zhang et al., 2026](#bib.bib105); [Lu et al., 2026](#bib.bib41)).

Training agents to coordinate such workflows therefore requires
experience that develops precise interface skills and connects
successive decisions to complete task outcomes. Obtaining this
experience at scale begins with constructing varied tasks, each of which
needs a request grounded in available software and content, a usable
initial environment, and a reliable way to judge success ([Ge et al.,
2026](#bib.bib23); [Zhou et al., 2026b](#bib.bib110)). Once these tasks
are available, collecting experience from them requires an agent
framework that sustains long interactions and records decisions and
outcomes across desktop and web environments. The resulting
demonstrations can teach the policy how to complete workflows, but
independent execution may lead it into states absent from those
demonstrations. Continued improvement therefore requires fresh
interaction and outcome feedback from the policy’s own attempts ([Wang
et al., 2025c](#bib.bib83); [Jung et al., 2026](#bib.bib34); [Lu et al.,
2026](#bib.bib41)). Meeting these linked needs calls for co-designing
how tasks are constructed, how agents execute them, and how the
resulting experience is used for learning.

We present NorthCUA, an integrated system for _turbocharging
computer-use agents through experience at scale_. Our central idea is to
co-design the production and use of experience, making software
interaction a continuing source of learning. We connect executable tasks
to policy updates through three components
(Figure [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1Introduction ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")),
focusing on GUI-based software operation. The Experience Flywheel
constructs executable tasks for the NorthCUA Harness to run and assess.
The recorded trajectories and outcome evidence guide curation, supplying
Staged Learning with demonstrations that complement the Flywheel’s
separately constructed grounding examples. This mixture develops
interface understanding and workflow completion. Shared execution and
outcome interfaces also let the learner collect fresh attempts from the
policy itself, extending the same system from demonstration collection
to continued policy improvement.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/00_experience_loop.png)

Figure 2: The NorthCUA experience loop. 01 The harness executes desktop
and web tasks, records their trajectories, and retains workspaces
through required live inspection. 02 The Experience Flywheel expands
intents, environments, and verifiers into executable tasks, then curates
assessed trajectories. The hard-task panel denotes skill-guided and
checkpoint-assisted collection through the harness
(Section [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
03 Staged Learning converts complementary experience into an updated
policy for subsequent execution. GUI states, cursor cues, and rewards
are schematic. Trajectory rows omit intermediate actions, and GRPO
retains valid failures alongside successes.

##### NorthCUA Harness: full-lifecycle infrastructure.

We design the NorthCUA Harness as _full-lifecycle infrastructure_ that
executes software tasks and turns the resulting attempts into
inspectable experience for learning. This requires keeping the agent’s
decisions connected to its workspace and outcomes, from environment
preparation through execution and evaluation to record preservation.
Within the shared runtime, visual context and task memory help agents
build on progress across long workflows ([Zhang et al.,
2026](#bib.bib105); [Zhang et al., 2025d](#bib.bib104)). An evaluator
uses the execution record and live workspace to check task completion
and execution quality, supplementing task-specific verifiers where their
coverage is incomplete ([Zhuge et al., 2025](#bib.bib114)).
To scale this process, the Batch Rollout Service coordinates parallel
workers and overlaps lifecycle stages, supporting efficient,
high-concurrency experience collection
(Section [3](#S3 "3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

##### Experience Flywheel: scaling grounding and agentic experience.

With a runtime for collecting and checking software experience, the next
challenge is to supply enough varied, useful work for the agent to
learn. The Experience Flywheel addresses two levels of supervision:
single-step grounding examples for locating controls and assessed agent
trajectories for completing tasks. Grounding pairs screenshots with
queries and target locations. Visual and structural annotations provide
broad coverage ([Lu et al., 2024](#bib.bib42); [Li et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib38)), while application documentation helps explain a
control’s function when appearance alone is insufficient. At the
trajectory level, three complementary construction routes supply tasks
to the harness. _Scaling Intents_ derives requests from available
software and content, while _Scaling Environments_ builds the workspaces
needed by sourced requests or reconstructs reference websites. For tasks
with executable checks, _Scaling Verifiers_ creates further tasks by
adapting requests, initial states, and verifiers together ([Ge et al.,
2026](#bib.bib23); [Lu et al., 2026](#bib.bib41); [Lv et al.,
2026](#bib.bib43)). When a valid task remains unsolved in ordinary
collection, we use recursive self-improvement (RSI) for hard-task
collection. Skill-guided exploration retains tested procedures for later
attempts, while milestone-guided search preserves software states and
agent context at verified milestones to explore alternative ways of
completing the remaining work. Task outcomes and execution quality
determine which trajectories become demonstrations for learning
(Section [4](#S4 "4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

##### Staged Learning: from experience to capability.

A model’s ability to coordinate a long workflow builds on familiarity
with the interfaces and operations it uses. Staged Learning follows this
progression, starting with _Broad GUI mid-training_ on grounding
examples and desktop and web trajectories judged to complete their tasks
([Wang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib83); [Jung et al., 2026](#bib.bib34)). The
broad trajectory corpus spans over four million GUI interaction steps
across dozens of applications. Building on this operational foundation,
_Verified long-horizon supervised fine-tuning (SFT)_ concentrates on
carefully selected, complete workflows whose outcomes pass executable
task verifiers and whose intermediate results support later decisions.
Independent execution can still lead the policy into states absent from
its demonstrations. _Joint desktop–web agentic reinforcement learning_
therefore lets one policy practice in both domains and learn from fresh
successes and failures. Task verifiers score these attempts, and
group-relative policy optimization (GRPO, [Shao et al.,
2024](#bib.bib70)) uses their outcomes to update the policy
(Section [5](#S5 "5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

Shared task, execution, and assessment interfaces connect these learning
stages to experience collection
(Figure [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1Introduction ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
An updated policy can return to the same Harness, where its new attempts
are evaluated and can be curated into subsequent training experience.
This return path lets advances in model capability support further
collection, giving NorthCUA a reusable foundation for continued learning
across desktop and web environments.

The resulting NorthCUA family comprises Nano (9B), Mini (35B), and Pro
(397B), all built on Qwen3.5 ([Qwen Team, 2026a](#bib.bib60)). We
evaluate this family on six benchmarks spanning GUI grounding, desktop
execution, and Web interaction. The suite follows the agent from
locating controls to completing long workflows and checking whether Web
applications behave as intended. NorthCUA establishes state-of-the-art
results among open-source models in all three domains
(Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")). With 397B
parameters, Pro outperforms trillion-parameter Qwen3.8-Max and Kimi-K3
on both grounding benchmarks, OSWorld-Verified, and WebArena-Verified
([Qwen Team, 2026c](#bib.bib62); [Kimi Team, 2026](#bib.bib35)). The 35B
Mini also surpasses Kimi-K3 on OSWorld-Verified and Qwen3.8-Max on
WebArena-Verified. Stage comparisons show desktop and Web gains across
all three model sizes after long-horizon SFT and joint RL
(Section [6](#S6 "6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

Long workflows also accumulate the latency of successive model
decisions. To reduce this cost, NorthCUA-Ultrafast adapts pre-RL Nano
with a Jev-inspired choice head, selecting finite actions directly while
retaining generation for open-ended outputs ([Almeida,
2026](#bib.bib1)). Its Head + LoRA variant completes matched successful
WebArena-Verified tasks $`2.3\times`$ faster on average than full
generation
(Section [6.8](#S6.SS8 "6.8 NorthCUA-Ultrafast ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
We release all three models, the full NorthCUA Harness code, and this
technical report, providing an open foundation for developing agents
that carry software work from user intent to completed outcomes.

## 2Scope and System Overview

### 2.1  Task Scope and Characteristics

We study two capability targets in desktop and web environments
(Figure [3](#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Single-step UI grounding. ‣ 2.1 Task Scope and Characteristics ‣ 2Scope and System Overview ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")):
locating a requested control in a screenshot and completing a task
through software interaction. We evaluate these targets separately.

##### Single-step UI grounding.

Given a screenshot and a natural-language query, the model predicts the
coordinates $`(x,y)`$ of the target UI element in the original image
([Cheng et al., 2024](#bib.bib13); [Li et al., 2025b](#bib.bib39)). The
model completes the task with one prediction over a fixed screenshot.
The query can describe the element’s appearance or function, such as
locating the zoom tool in
Figure [3](#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Single-step UI grounding. ‣ 2.1 Task Scope and Characteristics ‣ 2Scope and System Overview ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(a).

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/03_task_scope.png)

Figure 3: Task scope and interaction. (a) Single-step grounding predicts
a target location in a fixed screenshot. The enlarged output view marks
the zoom tool, with absolute pixel coordinates in the original image.
(b) Desktop and web agents change software state through successive
actions, with completion assessed from the resulting artifacts or state.
Web observations also include DOM information. Generated GUI scenes,
action overlays, and outcomes are illustrative. The displayed
transitions omit intermediate interactions.

##### End-to-end computer use.

An end-to-end task combines a user request, an initial software
environment, and conditions for judging completion. The environment
provides the applications, files, and website records needed for the
request. The agent observes the current state, takes an action, and uses
the resulting feedback to decide what to do next. Later decisions can
use information and results from earlier steps. The request and
completion conditions stay fixed throughout the attempt, so the final
files or software state can be checked against the original goal ([Zhou
et al., 2024](#bib.bib111); [Xie et al., 2024](#bib.bib86)).
Figure [3](#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Single-step UI grounding. ‣ 2.1 Task Scope and Characteristics ‣ 2Scope and System Overview ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(b)
illustrates the two interaction settings:

- •
  Desktop agents observe desktop screenshots and operate applications
  through mouse and keyboard actions, including clicking, typing,
  scrolling, and dragging. Tasks may span multiple applications and
  files.
- •
  Web agents observe webpage screenshots and document object model (DOM)
  information, and interact through browser tools for navigation,
  element-based or coordinate-based actions, text input, and tab
  management.

Both settings follow the same observation, action, and feedback loop,
using their respective desktop or browser interfaces. The acting policy
uses these interfaces to perform the task. Preparation, annotation, and
evaluation can use additional programmatic tools with separate
permissions.

### 2.2  System Organization and Experience Lifecycle

The experience loop in
Figure [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1Introduction ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
connects task construction to execution and learning. Its handoffs
specify how an executable task becomes an assessed record and then a
training sample.

The Experience Flywheel packages each executable task as a CaseSpec , a
task specification containing the request, input materials, environment
configuration, and setup instructions, together with requirements for
assessing the result. For the invoice task in
Figure [3](#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Single-step UI grounding. ‣ 2.1 Task Scope and Characteristics ‣ 2Scope and System Overview ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(b),
this means providing the workbook and spreadsheet application and
specifying that the exported PDF should highlight overdue invoices.

Using this specification, the NorthCUA Harness prepares the environment,
uploads input files, and runs setup and launch commands before starting
the agent on the request. Observations, actions, and feedback are
recorded throughout the attempt so that its outcome can be assessed in
the context of the work that produced it. Task-specific checks, an
evaluator, or both inspect the result against the declared requirements.
For the invoice task, this means checking the saved PDF and its
highlighted contents. These outcome and execution-quality assessments
give the Experience Flywheel the evidence needed to select
demonstrations. Valid tasks that remain unsolved can instead enter the
hard-task collection branch, where skill-guided exploration or
milestone-guided search produces further attempts for the same
assessment process
(Section [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

Staged Learning turns this collected experience into capability in three
steps. Broad GUI mid-training combines diverse task trajectories with
the Flywheel’s separate screenshot–query–target examples, building a
foundation in interface understanding and software operation. Grounding
supervision is used only in this stage. Verified long-horizon SFT then
focuses on high-quality, complete workflows with checked outcomes,
teaching the policy to carry information and intermediate results across
dependent steps. This prepares the model to learn further from its own
interaction.

For joint desktop–web agentic reinforcement learning, we first select
tasks on which the SFT policy’s trial attempts have different outcomes.
This focuses initial collection on tasks with useful outcome differences
for comparing executions. The Harness collects fresh groups of attempts,
executable verifiers score their outcomes, and GRPO uses comparisons
within each task group to update one shared policy
(Section [5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
The updated policy returns to the Harness for subsequent attempts under
the same task and outcome interfaces.

The following sections explain these components in detail.
Section [3](#S3 "3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
presents the execution and assessment framework,
Section [4](#S4 "4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
describes task construction and experience curation, and
Section [5](#S5 "5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
develops the staged learning recipe.

## 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use

The NorthCUA Harness provides _full-lifecycle infrastructure_ for
executing desktop and web tasks and turning each attempt into
inspectable experience for learning. During execution, a persistent
workspace and retained task context let the agent build on earlier
progress. We retain its execution record and resulting artifacts for
outcome assessment, keeping the original environment available when live
checks need to inspect the state the agent produced. Resources are
released after the required inspection and record preservation are
complete. By carrying each attempt through assessment and preservation,
this lifecycle keeps decisions, outputs, and judgments connected to the
same task attempt
(Section [2](#S2 "2Scope and System Overview ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

Figure [4](#S3.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
organizes this lifecycle at two levels. Within one task attempt, the
unified agent runtime (01) supplies the shared interaction loop and
platform tool interfaces. Long-horizon support (02) operates within this
execution, keeping the next decision informed while retaining the
complete trajectory. After acting ends, a tool-using evaluator (03)
checks the declared requirements using that trajectory and the live
workspace. The Batch Rollout Service coordinates the parallel task
lifecycles shown below. Each isolated environment proceeds through
preparation, execution, and assessment. Centralized control and
recording manage these attempts, keeping each workspace available
through inspection and output capture. This distinction separates the
mechanisms that make one attempt inspectable from the service that turns
them into sustained experience production. Online RL reuses the
execution and outcome interfaces under training-side lifecycle
management
(Section [5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")),
extending the same foundation from standalone collection to policy
learning.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/04_harness_lifecycle.png)

Figure 4: NorthCUA Harness lifecycle. Within the expanded attempt, the
shared runtime and long-horizon support sustain GUI interaction while
retaining the full trajectory. The evaluator inspects recorded behavior
and the live environment. Trajectory, task artifacts, evaluation
results, and task metadata are saved in an experience record before the
workspace is released. The lower lanes show parallel instances of this
lifecycle, coordinated by the batch collection service. GUI scenes,
agent imagery, and trajectories are illustrative.

### 3.1  Unified Execution Across Desktop and Web

Desktop and web agents follow the same basic loop: observe the software,
choose an action, execute it, and use the feedback to continue. What
differs is how each platform exposes its state and controls. Building on
shared-runtime approaches to computer use ([Zhang et al.,
2026](#bib.bib105); [Yan et al., 2025](#bib.bib94)), we design _Agent
Kit_ to separate this common loop from platform-specific observation and
action tools. Long-horizon support and evaluation reuse this same
execution foundation.

#### 3.1.1  Shared Agent Runtime

We use a shared runtime to organize interaction as a ReAct-style loop
with visual feedback ([Yao et al., 2023](#bib.bib97)). It assembles the
request, observation, and relevant history into the next model input,
invokes the policy, and executes the resulting tool calls. Returned
observations and action results then inform the next decision. Common
runtime services manage progress, budgets, and termination, while
environment-specific implementations supply the observations and
operations. To build an agent for another platform, developers supply
its perception functions, action tools, and observation processing
hooks. The shared runtime then carries these components through the
complete decision and recording loop, without rebuilding the agent flow.
Section [3.1.3](#S3.SS1.SSS3 "3.1.3 Desktop and Web Instantiations ‣ 3.1 Unified Execution Across Desktop and Web ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
details the desktop and web instances.

CUA execution also involves several timescales. Application and browser
sessions persist across actions, a user request can require many
decisions, and each decision produces feedback needed by the next. We
represent these lifetimes through three nested scopes:

- •
  Session maintains the agent’s execution state and associated software
  resources, such as a browser session, across a sequence of user
  requests.
- •
  Round tracks the processing of one user request, including its
  multi-step execution, budget, and termination status.
- •
  Step records one policy decision and the resulting tool execution and
  feedback. Each step can contain multiple tool calls.

These runtime scopes organize interaction, while the task attempt
supplies the collection-level identity used to associate actions,
artifacts, and assessment. Each recorded action retains its request and
session context, supporting task-level inspection and collection
recovery. Session resources are managed within the broader environment
lifecycle coordinated by the collection or training system.
Appendix [H.1](#A8.SS1.SSS0.Px1 "Execution scopes and tool interfaces. ‣ H.1 Runtime Configuration ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives the execution-scope details.

#### 3.1.2  Tool Integration and Dispatch

Desktop input, browser interaction, and workspace inspection expose
different operations, whose implementations may run beside the agent or
near a remotely hosted application. To make these capabilities available
to the shared decision loop, we use a common tool interface that allows
their implementations to evolve independently.

Tool definitions describe operations and their arguments, while dispatch
connects each call to its implementation and returns the result to the
requesting step. The Agent Kit integrates local functions and tools
exposed through Model Context Protocol (MCP), whose standard tool
descriptions and invocation messages support capability discovery and
execution ([Model Context Protocol, 2025](#bib.bib47)). Both paths feed
the same interaction history and recording services. Platform-specific
controls can therefore be added through tool bindings while reusing task
progression and feedback handling.

To reduce the repeated inference overhead of long GUI workflows, we
allow the model to issue multiple action calls in a single response. The
runtime executes these calls serially in the specified order, allowing
actions that can be planned together to share one model invocation.

For computer use, tool feedback also needs to describe the state after
an operation. Post-action observation hooks attach fresh screenshots or
page observations to state-changing tool results, giving the next
decision a current view of the interface.
Section [3.2.1](#S3.SS2.SSS1 "3.2.1 Action-Aware Visual Context ‣ 3.2 Sustaining Long-Horizon Interaction ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
develops this action-aware feedback design. The same tool separation
supports centralized agents operating remote devices, whose collection
lifecycle is described in
Section [3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Reliable Batch Rollout at Scale ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

#### 3.1.3  Desktop and Web Instantiations

We build desktop and web agents on this runtime by supplying the
observations, tools, and execution context appropriate to each platform.
Table [1](#S3.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 3.1.3 Desktop and Web Instantiations ‣ 3.1 Unified Execution Across Desktop and Web ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
compares the two instances along these interfaces:

- •
  Desktop agent. The agent uses desktop screenshots to observe
  applications and mouse and keyboard tools to click, type, scroll, and
  drag. Its platform adapter manages the active application, foreground
  window, and input focus so that an action reaches the intended
  control. This supports workflows across application windows and files,
  with screenshots, action results, and output artifacts retained for
  later assessment.
  Appendix [H.2.1](#A8.SS2.SSS1 "H.2.1 Desktop Agent ‣ H.2 Action Spaces and Parameter Schemas ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
  lists its actions and parameter schemas.
  Appendix [J.2](#A10.SS2 "J.2 Using a Terminal Through the GUI ‣ Appendix J OSWorld 2.0 Execution Cases ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
  follows a recorded animation task in which these same desktop actions
  open a terminal, enter scripts and return to the presentation UI for
  playback checks.
- •
  Web agent. The agent observes page screenshots together with
  DOM-derived information. Browser tools support navigation,
  element-reference or coordinate-based actions, form entry, and tab
  operations. Its adapter maintains the active tab, page state, and
  element references as navigation changes the interface. The resulting
  record includes page observations, action results, and browser
  artifacts.
  Appendix [H.2.2](#A8.SS2.SSS2 "H.2.2 Web Agent ‣ H.2 Action Spaces and Parameter Schemas ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
  specifies the corresponding browser tools and argument constraints.

The adapters handle these platform differences, while both agents reuse
model invocation, request progression, context management, and
trajectory recording.

|                            |                                                        |                                                                                                |
| -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Interface                  | Desktop agent                                          | Web agent                                                                                      |
| Interaction surface        | Applications, windows, and system interfaces           | Pages, tabs, and browser state                                                                 |
| Policy observations        | Desktop screenshots                                    | Page screenshots and DOM-derived information                                                   |
| Environment-facing actions | Mouse-and-keyboard GUI operations                      | Navigation, element-reference or coordinate actions, keyboard input, forms, and tab operations |
| Execution context          | Active application, foreground window, and input focus | Active tab, page state, and element references                                                 |
| Retained evidence          | Screenshots, action results, and application artifacts | Screenshots, page snapshots, action results, and browser artifacts                             |

Table 1: Desktop and web interfaces used in this report. Platform
adapters provide observations and action execution, while the Agent Kit
supplies the shared runtime and trajectory-recording services.

The same separation provides a path to additional platforms. Mobile
extensions, for example, would supply screen observations, touch
operations, and application/session handling around the shared runtime.
Across the desktop and web instantiations, this reusable execution
foundation also exposes a common challenge: carrying earlier constraints
and intermediate results into decisions made much later in a workflow.

### 3.2  Sustaining Long-Horizon Interaction

Long-horizon execution is a central challenge in practical computer use.
Real software work can require many fine-grained GUI operations across
applications, files, and intermediate artifacts, with later actions
depending on earlier discoveries and decisions ([Yuan et al.,
2026](#bib.bib99)). Editing a document may still require a value read
from a spreadsheet and an output requirement encountered much earlier.
Meanwhile, repeated visual observations and tool feedback enlarge the
history supplied to each decision ([Lu et al., 2026](#bib.bib41); [Lv
et al., 2026](#bib.bib43)). Sustaining these workflows requires
controlling context growth while keeping current state, earlier
progress, and task-relevant knowledge available for continued action.

We separate the _decision context_ presented to the model from the
_complete execution record_ retained for inspection and learning. To
keep later decisions informed within a limited context budget, we design
three complementary mechanisms: _action-aware visual context_ for recent
states and operations, _task-preserving compaction_ for earlier progress
and remaining work, and _structured working memory_ for facts and
decisions the agent chooses to retain.
Figure [5](#S3.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 3.2 Sustaining Long-Horizon Interaction ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
follows one invoice workflow across these mechanisms. The visual window
shows the steps leading to export, the summary keeps export marked as
pending, and an agent-written note carries the overdue total into the
report. Each view supplies information needed for continued action
without replacing the full execution record.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/05_long_horizon.png)

Figure 5: Context for long-horizon execution. The complete trajectory is
retained separately from the next-action context. The visual window
contains historical pre-action overlays and the latest clean tool-result
image. Textual action and result history can outlast image eviction
until compaction. Compaction summarizes earlier actions and results,
omits old GUI locators, and preserves recent complete exchanges and the
current GUI. An active agent-written note carries the overdue total from
workbook to report. PDF export remains unconfirmed. GUI scenes and the
displayed visual-window size are illustrative, with intermediate
interactions omitted.

#### 3.2.1  Action-Aware Visual Context

Repeated GUI interaction produces an image-heavy history whose size
grows with the workflow ([Lu et al., 2026](#bib.bib41)). Its visual
content serves two related purposes: the current screen guides the next
action, and recent states help the agent interpret what it just did. The
resulting screen alone can leave the location of a preceding click or
the path of a drag unclear, particularly after a window or page changes.
Visual context therefore needs to preserve both current state and the
spatial meaning of recent operations within a limited image budget.

We use a sliding window to provide a bounded visual history of up to
$`k`$ recent frames, including the latest clean screenshot. Textual
action and tool-result history survives image eviction, retaining a
longer account of the interaction until the text-history policy calls
for compaction. To keep the newest frame current, post-action
observation hooks supply fresh post-action feedback alongside
state-changing tool results. The next policy invocation receives this
screenshot directly, avoiding a dedicated model turn solely to request
the resulting screen.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/06_action_overlays_compact.png)

Figure 6: Action-aware visual feedback. Matched crops show the
historical pre-action view with its NorthCUA overlay (left) and the
clean resulting state (right). 01 A click opens the chart dialog. 02 A
drag moves a video clip earlier on its track. 03 Scrolling reveals route
details below. 04 A text badge records the payload entered into the
focused field. Screenshots come from interactive GUI mockups with
generated scenery. Overlays use the unmodified runtime renderer. The
input badge is enlarged below its historical view.

To help the agent connect its previous operation with the change now
visible on screen, we design a complementary action view in the
historical frames. Visual prompting work shows how spatial marks can
make image regions easier to refer to ([Yang et al., 2023](#bib.bib95)).
Our historical action overlays mark the corresponding _pre-action_
image, using different cues for different operations. As shown in
Figure [6](#S3.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 3.2.1 Action-Aware Visual Context ‣ 3.2 Sustaining Long-Horizon Interaction ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
a click marker locates the control that opens a dialog, a drag path
links the clip’s old and new positions, and a scroll cue identifies the
direction in which the page moved. Text and key badges record operations
that are less clear from a spatial marker alone.
Figure [6](#S3.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 3.2.1 Action-Aware Visual Context ‣ 3.2 Sustaining Long-Horizon Interaction ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
pairs each annotated pre-action view with its clean resulting state to
make the operation and observed change easy to compare. In the runtime’s
decision context, historical overlays identify recent operations, while
the latest clean screenshot provides the current interface for the next
decision. Replacing an earlier clean frame with an overlay changes the
image history. The training-segment rule in
Section [5.3.3](#S5.SS3.SSS3 "5.3.3 Preserving Executed Decisions for Learning ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
preserves the input seen before that change.

This bounded visual history preserves both the spatial meaning of recent
actions and the current interface state. The textual record continues to
accumulate across these visual windows, creating the next
context-management challenge.

#### 3.2.2  Task-Preserving Context Compaction

Bounding images leaves another source of context growth: assistant
messages, reasoning, tool feedback, and intermediate task information
accumulate throughout the workflow. Earlier constraints and discoveries
may still be needed when this text approaches the model’s usable input
budget. Context-management methods consolidate long histories to retain
useful information within finite prompts ([Ye et al.,
2026](#bib.bib98)). We adapt this principle to computer use by designing
task-preserving compaction around three priorities. When older history
needs to be summarized, we preserve the immediate action–feedback
context, retain task progress, and avoid carrying stale GUI locators
into later decisions:

- •
  Protect current interactions. The task request, latest GUI
  observation, and latest few steps remain protected. Assistant actions
  stay grouped with their tool results, and open exchanges remain intact
  until their responses arrive. This preserves the immediate
  action–feedback context, including the current GUI observation whether
  it appears in a user message or a tool result.
- •
  Summarize task progress. The summary organizes goals, constraints,
  supported facts, decisions, completed and failed work, current state,
  open items, and artifact references around task continuation. Desktop
  and web profiles instruct the summarizer to retain semantic software
  state and observed effects while condensing routine navigation. For
  example, an extracted spreadsheet value, its source file, and a
  pending export requirement can therefore remain available after the
  navigation history is compressed. The instructions also distinguish
  issued actions, observed effects, and unresolved completion claims: an
  export command and an observed saved file represent different evidence
  about progress.
- •
  Handle transient GUI locators. Coordinates and element references can
  become stale after a page update or application switch. The
  summary-input projection filters these volatile fields, and the
  summary instructions exclude old locators from current executable
  state. The retained current observation remains available for
  grounding subsequent actions.

Later compaction updates the existing summary with newly eligible
history, with source references available for inspection. This gives
continued work a compact account of its dependencies while keeping the
full execution record available for outcome assessment. Alongside this
budget-triggered consolidation, the agent can actively preserve
important information as it is discovered.

#### 3.2.3  Structured Working Memory

During a long task, an agent encounters information with clear future
value: a figure read from another file, a requirement for the final
deliverable, an unsuccessful approach, or a plan for remaining work.
Assistant messages can mention these details in the chronological
history, where their later visibility depends on history retention and
summarization. Giving selected information an explicit working-memory
representation lets the agent decide what to retain and revise during
execution, following the broader principle of agent-controlled memory
management ([Packer et al., 2024](#bib.bib56)).

To let the agent preserve useful information when it discovers it, we
design a dedicated text-only note tool. The agent can call this tool
proactively to record a spreadsheet value before switching to a
document, or to preserve a correction while revising an unsuccessful
approach. The right-hand panel of
Figure [5](#S3.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 3.2 Sustaining Long-Horizon Interaction ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
shows this transfer: note N1 records the overdue total from the workbook
for later use in the report. Notes are stored independently of the
history summary and can be presented in subsequent decision contexts.
This complements compaction: a summary consolidates accumulated work
under budget pressure, while a note explicitly preserves information the
agent expects to use again.

Notes serve three purposes: (i) retaining reusable knowledge and choices
through _facts_, _constraints_, and _decisions_, (ii) recording lessons
and risks through _corrections_ and _precautions_, and (iii) tracking
progress and unfinished work through _checkpoints_. This organization
encourages concise entries with a clear role in later decisions.

As work progresses, replacement notes can supersede outdated entries.
The memory service maintains them within the authorized task and
session, then supplies a budgeted view of active entries to prompt
construction. A corrected value or revised plan can therefore replace
the corresponding obsolete item in working context, keeping remembered
information aligned with the agent’s evolving understanding of the task.

Together, these mechanisms keep the current screen, accumulated
progress, and reusable knowledge available for continued action. The
complete execution record connects these decisions to the resulting
workspace, providing the evidence for outcome evaluation and subsequent
training.
Appendix [H.1](#A8.SS1 "H.1 Runtime Configuration ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives the runtime settings, and
Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Supervised Representation and Sampling ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
and
Section [5.3.3](#S5.SS3.SSS3 "5.3.3 Preserving Executed Decisions for Learning ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
describe their training interfaces.

Appendix [J.1](#A10.SS1 "J.1 Preserving a Delivery Constraint Across Long Contexts ‣ Appendix J OSWorld 2.0 Execution Cases ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
follows a photo-delivery task in which a payment constraint remains
available from reconciliation through the final email.

### 3.3  Agentic Outcome Evaluation

Selecting GUI trajectories for learning requires accurate outcome
judgments across a growing variety of tasks. Dedicated verifiers become
difficult to write and validate when correctness depends on content,
presentation, or application-specific state ([Rosset et al.,
2026](#bib.bib68)). We therefore use an adaptive evaluator that
interprets the declared task requirements and gathers the evidence
needed to assess the work.
Figure [7](#S3.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ 3.3 Agentic Outcome Evaluation ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
shows how recorded work and active inspection together support an
evidence-linked assessment.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/07_evaluator.png)

Figure 7: Agentic outcome evaluation. Open questions guide selective
retrieval and live inspection in the task environment. E1 records an
issued export, E2 establishes file presence, and E3 exposes PDF contents
for comparison with the source workbook. Unresolved questions prompt
further checks and can remain unresolved in the final report.
Evidence-linked findings distinguish task fulfillment from execution
assessment. GUI views, tool results, actions, and findings are
illustrative. Generated document views are cropped, intermediate actor
actions are omitted, and selected quality fields are shown without
ratings.

We build the evaluator as an _agent-as-a-judge_ ([Zhuge et al.,
2025](#bib.bib114)), reusing Agent Kit tools. After the acting phase
ends, it selectively retrieves saved records and artifacts and can
inspect the original workspace using authorized shell, GUI, and browser
tools. It adapts its inspection to each requirement and seeks further
evidence when the available observations leave the outcome unclear. This
combines reusable inspection capabilities with task-specific judgment,
extending assessment to requirements that lack dedicated executable
checks. Task-grounded rubrics guide this process, and multidimensional
reports connect the findings to experience selection and failure
analysis. In
Figure [7](#S3.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ 3.3 Agentic Outcome Evaluation ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")’s
export example, the evaluator checks that the PDF exists and opens it to
compare its contents with the source. This artifact inspection supplies
the evidence for its final report. We require this report to associate
each criterion-level judgment with its evidence, uncertainty, and
evaluator operations.

#### 3.3.1  Artifact and Live-State Inspection

The evaluator receives the task request, assessment criteria, and a
compact view of the execution records. Requirements may concern an
earlier action, a final file, or a state that is visible only inside an
application. To reach these different forms of evidence, it uses two
complementary tool groups:

- •
  Recorded evidence. Retrieval tools expose selected steps, screenshots,
  tool results, and retained artifacts. They let the evaluator inspect
  relevant parts of a long trajectory as questions arise, while the full
  record remains available outside its current context.
- •
  Live task state. Live tools inspect the original device or sandbox
  through fresh screenshots, read-only shell commands, and GUI or
  browser operations. Web probes can reuse the actor’s browser session,
  including its tabs and page state. This access makes
  environment-resident results available for checking while the
  workspace is still alive.

Appendix [H.2.3](#A8.SS2.SSS3 "H.2.3 Evaluator ‣ H.2 Action Spaces and Parameter Schemas ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
lists the evaluator’s retrieval tools, authorized live interfaces, and
report schema.

The evaluator chooses between these sources according to the requirement
under review, continuing its investigation when an observation leaves an
important question open. This environment-aware approach is also studied
in [Shi et al. (2026)](#bib.bib71). For a document export, for example,
a recorded action can show that export was attempted, a file check can
establish whether an output exists, and an application inspection can
reveal its rendered contents. Comparing these contents with the task
inputs supports judgments about their correctness. Each observation
answers a different part of the acceptance question. Selective retrieval
and live access thus connect the history of an operation to its actual
result.

|                         |                                                                       |                                                                           |
| ----------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Dimension               | Assessment                                                            | Analysis and selection use                                                |
| Task completion         | Criterion fulfillment, overall verdict, and completion rating         | Identify supported completion examples and unresolved requirements        |
| Process quality         | Suitability of actions, tool use, and avoidable mistakes              | Distinguish reliable demonstrations from fragile paths to the same result |
| Efficiency              | Repetition, detours, unnecessary steps, and recovery behavior         | Compare successful paths and apply cohort-specific efficiency criteria    |
| Safety and side effects | Sensitive operations, unsolicited changes, and observed risks         | Review unsafe or undesired behavior separately from completion            |
| Failure diagnosis       | Failure mechanism, controllability, and supported divergence location | Analyze capability gaps and distinguish them from environment blockers    |
| Judgment uncertainty    | Confidence, missing information, and uncertain verdicts               | Route unresolved cases for further checking                               |

Table 2: Evaluator reporting dimensions and their uses in trajectory
curation and failure analysis.

To preserve attribution, the assessment protocol pairs pre-inspection
terminal evidence with a separate record of evaluator operations. Each
judgment is linked to the submitted work and the observations used to
assess it, making the investigation traceable to the original attempt.
The rubric then connects this evidence to task fulfillment and execution
quality.
Appendix [H.1](#A8.SS1.SSS0.Px2 "Evaluator profiles and attribution. ‣ H.1 Runtime Configuration ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
details the inspection and attribution protocol.

#### 3.3.2  Task-Grounded, Multidimensional Rubrics

The value of a GUI trajectory depends on both its result and the
behavior it demonstrates. Successful attempts can contain unnecessary
detours or unintended changes, while an attempt blocked late by the
environment can contain useful completed subtasks. Data curation
therefore needs answers to two complementary questions: (i) *which task
requirements were fulfilled*, to establish what the trajectory
accomplished, and (ii) *how the agent performed the work*, to assess the
behavior available for learning. We therefore design the rubric to
combine task-grounded acceptance criteria with multidimensional
execution assessment ([Rosset et al., 2026](#bib.bib68)).

For task fulfillment, the request and declared acceptance conditions
define the intended outcome. The rubric expresses these obligations as
specific, individually checkable questions. Export tasks may require the
right contents, destination, and format. Explicitly required procedures
also form part of acceptance. Request-derived criteria are generated
before scoring the trajectory, with the original task requirements
governing their interpretation. Assessing these obligations individually
identifies complete, partial, and unresolved fulfillment, including
which requested subtasks have supporting evidence.

For execution assessment, the report records process quality,
efficiency, side effects, and failure diagnosis alongside judgment
uncertainty. These dimensions explain the path taken to the result:
repeated ineffective actions may reduce its value as a demonstration,
while a successful recovery can provide useful behavior to study.
Failure diagnosis distinguishes agent errors from environment blockers,
and uncertainty identifies judgments that need further checking.
Findings are linked to the relevant requirements and recorded steps so
that their scope can be inspected.
Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.3.1 Artifact and Live-State Inspection ‣ 3.3 Agentic Outcome Evaluation ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
summarizes the dimensions and their uses.

Together, the rubric and execution report describe both the result
achieved and the behavior that produced it. Their links to specific
requirements and recorded steps make the judgments useful for selecting
complete demonstrations, reviewing local segments, and analyzing
recurring errors.

#### 3.3.3  From Evaluation to Experience Selection

We use the report’s separate rubric scores to select data for different
learning goals. Task fulfillment identifies completed work, while
process quality, efficiency, and side-effect assessments let us adjust
selection thresholds for each data cohort. For example, a cohort of
concise successful demonstrations can apply stricter efficiency
criteria, while a cohort meant to teach recovery can retain useful
corrections and exploration within successful attempts.

At the trajectory level, these criteria select complete demonstrations.
At the segment level, requirement-linked evidence can guide review of
completed subtasks or recovery sequences, with the original decision
context and local outcome kept alongside the behavior.
Figure [7](#S3.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ 3.3 Agentic Outcome Evaluation ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")’s
report separates completion from execution quality so that these
dimensions can be used together in selection.
Section [5.1](#S5.SS1.SSS0.Px2 "Outcome-based trajectory curation. ‣ 5.1 Broad GUI Mid-Training ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
applies the reports to trajectory curation, and
Section [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Verified Long-Horizon SFT ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
retains complete episodes for long-horizon SFT.

### 3.4  Reliable Batch Rollout at Scale

The preceding mechanisms turn one task attempt into assessed experience.
Producing a large training corpus requires many such attempts to run in
parallel, each with its own software state. Their preparation, GUI
execution, evaluation, and recording take different amounts of time and
consume different resources ([Xie et al., 2024](#bib.bib86); [Yuan
et al., 2026](#bib.bib99)). Simply launching more agents can therefore
create a backlog at another stage, while an interruption after execution
can leave useful work without a complete record.

To support efficient large-scale collection, we design a Batch Rollout
Service that coordinates the entire task lifecycle. Following the
environment-lifecycle perspective also explored in [Peng et al.
(2026)](#bib.bib57), we combine centralized agent control with isolated
workspaces, prepare upcoming tasks alongside ongoing execution, and
manage load and recovery through record delivery.
Figure [8](#S3.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ 3.4 Reliable Batch Rollout at Scale ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
shows the two relationships behind this design. Panel (a) connects one
controller to independent device workers. Panel (b) shows how their
lifecycles overlap: the same-time marker crosses evaluation, execution,
and preparation in three different attempts. The service coordinates
this concurrent work so that completed attempts produce usable
experience records.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/08_batch_rollout.png)

Figure 8: Batch rollout at scale. (a) A centralized controller manages
agent runners connected to device workers in independent E2B sandboxes
over long-lived WebSocket connections. Each worker shares its sandbox’s
lifecycle. (b) Staggered lifecycles overlap preparation, execution, and
evaluation. Ready intervals mark prepared environments awaiting
dispatch. Sandboxes remain available through live checks and output
capture. Record icons mark captured outputs that persist after release.
The vertical time slice marks concurrent stages. Timings and concurrency
are schematic, and GUI scenes are generated illustrations.

#### 3.4.1  Remote Execution with Centralized Agent Control

As collection grows, prompts, model settings, and agent logic continue
to change. Placing that logic inside every task environment would
require updating those environments whenever the agent changes. We
therefore separate centralized agent control from device execution, as
shown in
Figure [8](#S3.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ 3.4 Reliable Batch Rollout at Scale ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(a):

- •
  Centralized controller. One service manages multiple agent runners and
  their device workers. It maintains prompts and agent configuration,
  assembles context, invokes the model, and dispatches actions.
- •
  Device worker. Each worker runs inside one task’s isolated sandbox. It
  executes tool calls and captures observations from that environment.
  The worker starts with its sandbox and ends when the sandbox is
  released.

The controller maintains a long-lived WebSocket connection to each
worker, sending actions and receiving observations over the same
connection. Related distributed device orchestration appears in [Zhang
et al. (2025d)](#bib.bib104). Here, each runner communicates with the
worker assigned to its task, keeping the concurrent attempts separate.

This division lets us update compatible prompt and model configurations
centrally for later batches while reusing the same device-worker
implementation and software templates. The device layer remains focused
on observation and tool execution, so routine agent changes do not
require changing that layer. We save each batch’s configuration for
resumed collection and retain its trajectories on the controller side,
where they survive sandbox release. With control and recording shared,
scaling execution then depends on preparing enough independent task
environments.

#### 3.4.2  Isolated Environments with Pipelined Preparation

A new attempt needs the files and software state specified by its task.
Reusing a workspace changed by an earlier attempt could alter those
starting conditions ([Xie et al., 2024](#bib.bib86)). We therefore give
each attempt its own initialized sandbox in our fresh-start collection
mode. For Linux desktop and browser tasks, we choose E2B to create and
manage these isolated environments ([E2B, 2026b](#bib.bib20)), with its
desktop extension providing the graphical workspace ([E2B,
2026a](#bib.bib19)). Custom templates supply the operating system and
shared applications, while per-task setup uploads files, launches
applications, and prepares the required state. This lets tasks reuse a
software template without inheriting another attempt’s changes.

Creating a fresh environment takes time, so preparing tasks only when an
agent is ready would leave collection waiting on setup. We address this
with a task-bound preparation queue: upcoming environments are prepared
while earlier attempts are executing or being evaluated.
Figure [8](#S3.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ 3.4 Reliable Batch Rollout at Scale ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(b)
makes this overlap visible in the offset Prepare blocks. Each ready
workspace is assigned once to its matching task, provided enough time
remains for execution and cleanup. It stays available through the
required outcome checks and output capture, then is released.
Preparation can thus run ahead without sacrificing fresh starting
conditions or losing the evidence needed for assessment.

#### 3.4.3  Completion-Oriented Scheduling and Recovery

Preparation, execution, and evaluation progress at different rates.
Independent admission can leave prepared environments waiting for
execution or completed attempts waiting for evaluation. We coordinate
admission with two controls. Stage-specific concurrency limits bound the
number of attempts admitted to each stage. Because resource demand can
vary within these limits, controller-pressure feedback slows new
admissions when the shared controller remains busy. The first control
limits local demand, while the second responds to load across the
concurrent attempts.

When a connection is lost or a downstream process fails, we first check
whether the affected attempt is still running or has completed.
Completed execution records can support retries of record-based
evaluation or artifact processing, preserving the work already done. If
the task itself needs to be rerun, we start it in a fresh environment.

Before delivery, we check that the required trajectory, observations,
and assessments are complete and linked to the same attempt.
Appendix [H.3](#A8.SS3 "H.3 Batch Collection and Resource Ownership ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives the configuration and recovery details.

Together, these designs carry the same runtime from individual task
execution to sustained delivery of complete, inspectable experience. The
Experience Flywheel in
Section [4](#S4 "4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
builds on this foundation to construct tasks and curate the resulting
records for model training.

## 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience

The NorthCUA Harness provides the execution infrastructure, but training
a computer-use model also requires a large and varied supply of
experience. The model needs examples of which control a request refers
to, as well as trajectories that show how successive decisions achieve a
complete goal. We design the Experience Flywheel to construct and curate
these two levels of supervision. Single-step grounding pairs a
screenshot and a query with the location of one visible control.
End-to-end task construction supplies user goals, prepared workspaces,
and outcome checks from which the harness can collect multi-step
trajectories.

These levels require different construction methods. For grounding, we
start from visible controls and generate language that identifies them.
For complete tasks, we connect available software and content, sourced
requests, or verified seed tasks to the environments and assessments
they need. This gives three complementary routes for scaling intents,
environments, and verifiers. Executing the resulting tasks supplies
experience for learning, although successful trajectories can remain
scarce for the hardest workflows. We address that remaining gap through
recursive exploration that retains useful skills and verified progress
across attempts. Together, these methods supply localization examples
and curated trajectories for the staged learning in
Section [5](#S5 "5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

### 4.1  Single-Step UI Grounding

Single-step grounding takes a screenshot and a natural-language query,
then predicts the location of the requested control ([Cheng et al.,
2024](#bib.bib13)). Each training example contains a screenshot, a query
that identifies a visible control such as a button or toolbar icon, and
the control’s coordinate in the original image. The main construction
challenge is to write queries that identify the same control across
different visual states and ways of describing an operation, especially
in specialized applications ([Lu et al., 2024](#bib.bib42); [Li et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib38)).

We construct these examples through two complementary routes:

1.  1.  Broad visual and structural coverage. We use screenshot evidence and
        available interface metadata to describe visible controls across
        diverse applications and states
        (Figure [9](#S4.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ 4.1.1 Broad Visual and Structural Coverage ‣ 4.1 Single-Step UI Grounding ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
2.  2.  Documentation-grounded functional localization. We retrieve
        application knowledge when a control’s appearance leaves its
        function unclear, then use the matched function to generate queries
        (Figure [10](#S4.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ 4.1.2 Documentation-Grounded Functional Localization ‣ 4.1 Single-Step UI Grounding ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

The first route expands interface and language coverage. The second
fills gaps that visual evidence alone leaves unresolved. Both preserve
the target’s original location and produce the same
screenshot–query–coordinate format, so their candidates can pass through
shared quality and coverage checks.

#### 4.1.1  Broad Visual and Structural Coverage

To obtain broad grounding coverage, we first need screenshots of varied
interfaces, then a way to turn their visible controls into language
targets. We use the three-step procedure in
Figure [9](#S4.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ 4.1.1 Broad Visual and Structural Coverage ‣ 4.1 Single-Step UI Grounding ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
Panel (a) collects interface states, including states encountered during
agent work. Panel (b) associates one visible control with its appearance
and original location. This target record gives the query generator in
(c) a fixed referent: it can vary how the control is described while
keeping the location label unchanged.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/09_grounding_broad.png)

Figure 9: Constructing GUI grounding examples. (a) Screenshots cover
public UI datasets, web-collected interfaces, and states encountered in
agent trajectories. (b) Visual annotations and available accessibility
metadata describe one target in its original screenshot. (c) Name-,
position-, and operation-based queries each form a separate candidate
example with the same clean screenshot and absolute target point. The
enlarged Day–Week–Month control group supplies the local evidence for
these descriptions. GUI assets are illustrative. Target outlines explain
construction, while training uses the unmarked $`1536\times 1024`$
source image.

To collect these states, we combine public UI datasets, web-collected
GUI screenshots, and screenshots from agent trajectories, including real
Linux desktop interaction. Trajectories contribute intermediate states
such as open dialogs, expanded menus, and partially completed forms. We
then use OmniParser, a screen-parsing system that detects interactive
regions and describes icons, to obtain candidate control boxes and
descriptions ([Lu et al., 2024](#bib.bib42)). Accessibility annotations,
when available for the same state, add control names and roles. These
sources provide complementary evidence for identifying a target. Visual
parsing supports screenshots collected without accessibility metadata,
while names and roles help connect visible controls to the language used
in requests.

For each target, we consolidate this evidence into one control record
that keeps the crop and original image position together with the
available name, role, color, appearance, and surrounding context ([Zhang
et al., 2026](#bib.bib105)). For the calendar example in
Figure [9](#S4.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ 4.1.1 Broad Visual and Structural Coverage ‣ 4.1 Single-Step UI Grounding ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(b),
the record identifies the blue _Week_ tab between _Day_ and _Month_,
together with a point inside its clickable region. The full screenshot
preserves the neighboring controls needed to make a description
specific.

With this record in place, we use an LLM to generate several queries
from the record and full screenshot. The descriptions refer to the
control’s name, appearance, position, or an operation supported by the
available evidence. In the example, _Select the Week tab_ names the
control directly, while _Select week view_ expresses its purpose. Each
query becomes a separate candidate example paired with the clean
original screenshot and the same coordinate. This variation broadens the
language supervision without changing which control the model learns to
locate ([Chen et al., 2026a](#bib.bib11)).

This route supplies broad coverage of interfaces and visual states. When
a control’s operation depends on application-specific knowledge, the
documentation route provides the missing functional description.

#### 4.1.2  Documentation-Grounded Functional Localization

Some controls belong to specialized applications whose functions are
hard to infer from a screenshot alone. Parsing may locate a toolbar icon
while a general-purpose LLM remains unsure what the icon does. This
distinction matters when several visually similar controls perform
different operations, as in a layout editor with alignment controls for
different parts of a document ([Wang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib83)).

We design a data-construction agent to supply the missing application
knowledge before query generation.
Figure [10](#S4.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ 4.1.2 Documentation-Grounded Functional Localization ‣ 4.1 Single-Step UI Grounding ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
illustrates three steps. The agent identifies the application and
target, then searches online documentation for candidate functions. It
uses the surrounding GUI to select the function that fits the visible
context.

In the alignment example, the same-looking alignment symbol could refer
to moving objects or formatting text. Matching its documented function
resolves what a query can reliably ask the agent to do with the already
located control.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/10_grounding_documentation.png)

Figure 10: Documentation-guided control disambiguation. (a) The
screenshot supplies the application, version, and located target. (b)
Online manual search retrieves control images and descriptions for
visual top-$`k`$ candidates. One aligns the left edges of separate
objects, while the other aligns paragraph text within a fixed text
object. (c) Panel and selection context determine the function match,
which guides query generation. Each query retains the original
screenshot and target point. The GUI, documentation cards, and
before/after operation diagrams are illustrative. Documentation supports
sample construction.

##### Identify the application and target.

This route begins with the same OmniParser-based screen parsing, which
provides candidate control boxes and crops while preserving their
locations in the original screenshot ([Lu et al., 2024](#bib.bib42)).
Given the screenshot and a target crop, the construction agent
identifies the application and any visible version information, such as
Inkscape 1.3 in panel (a). These details constrain the documentation
search to the software in which the control appears, while the crop
provides the visual target to match.

##### Retrieve documented functions.

Using that application identity, the agent searches online user guides
and manuals and extracts illustrated controls with their function
descriptions. We keep each illustration with its function description
and page context, so that a visually similar icon retains the
information needed to interpret its operation. Image-embedding
similarity then narrows these references to top-$`k`$ candidates
([Radford et al., 2021](#bib.bib67)). Panel (b) shows both parts of this
step: the online manual search and the candidate operations retrieved
for the target icon.

##### Match with GUI context and generate queries.

Visual similarity supplies candidates but can leave the operation
ambiguous. The agent therefore compares their descriptions with the full
screenshot, neighboring controls, and active tool group. This contextual
check matters because similar icons can denote different operations.

In
Figure [10](#S4.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ 4.1.2 Documentation-Grounded Functional Localization ‣ 4.1 Single-Step UI Grounding ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
object alignment and paragraph alignment provide plausible visual
candidates. Object alignment moves selected canvas objects to align
their left edges, whereas paragraph alignment left-aligns the text
within a text object. The visible _Align and Distribute_ panel
identifies the relevant tool group, and the selected canvas objects
provide complementary evidence for the object-alignment interpretation.
The accepted match therefore links a specific function description to
the control already located in the screenshot.

Once the function is resolved, we give the accepted description, target
crop, and full screenshot to an LLM to generate query variants. One can
request the documented operation, such as aligning selected objects
along their left edges. Another can identify the same control through
its tool group and appearance. All variants inherit the original target
coordinate. Thus, application knowledge enriches the query while
preserving the common grounding format. The documentation and retrieval
candidates remain construction-side evidence, and the resulting samples
enter the shared quality checks below.

#### 4.1.3  Quality Control and Coverage

Both routes produce the same candidate format: a clean screenshot, a
query, and a target coordinate. We use two complementary checks before
adding a sample to the corpus:

- •
  Correctness check. Geometry checks confirm that the point lies inside
  the annotated control. Multimodal review then checks that the control
  supports the described operation, the query refers to it, and the
  description is unambiguous among nearby controls.
  Documentation-derived examples also carry their accepted function
  match into this review.
- •
  Coverage check. Deduplication removes repeated screenshots and
  near-identical descriptions. Selection retains useful variation across
  applications, UI states, control types, and query styles, so that
  different ways of referring to a control remain represented.

These checks address different reasons a larger candidate pool may fail
to provide useful supervision: incorrect target labels and repetitive
coverage. Geometry or identity failures return to construction when they
can be repaired, while unresolved cases are excluded. Selection then
retains accepted examples across interfaces and language styles.
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Grounding Data Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
records the annotation, matching, and curation settings.

The accepted examples enter mid-training as direct localization
supervision. Screenshots acquired during multi-step collection can also
enrich this pool with states encountered in actual software work. The
next subsection extends the same construction logic from identifying one
control to carrying out a complete user goal.

### 4.2  Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers

Grounding examples teach the model to locate a requested control.
Learning to plan and act across a complete workflow also requires
interaction trajectories ([Wang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib81); [Jung
et al., 2026](#bib.bib34); [Wang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib78)). To collect
them, we must turn each user goal into an executable task by providing
the workspace it needs and a reliable assessment of its outcome.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/11_three_axes_overview.png)

Figure 11: Three task-construction routes. Scaling Intents binds
functions to file or website content before writing requests. Scaling
Environments prepares files and applications for sourced requests or
reconstructs website interfaces and backing state from references.
Scaling Verifiers jointly adapts requests, setup, and executable checks,
then tests tasks and verifiers. Each alternative route produces a
complete task binding a request, environment setup, and outcome
assessment. GUI trials test completion from the initial state, while
result probes test acceptance of correct outcomes and rejection of
incorrect ones. GUI assets and task packages are illustrative.

Figure [11](#S4.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
compares three construction routes by what they start with and what they
generate:

- •
  Scaling Intents starts from applications, files, or website content
  and generates requests supported by their functions and facts.
- •
  Scaling Environments starts from sourced requests or reference
  websites and builds the files, applications, or local website needed
  to execute tasks in that setting.
- •
  Scaling Verifiers starts from tasks whose outcomes can be checked by
  reliable scripts. It generates related tasks by adapting the request,
  setup, and executable verifier together.

Each column ends with a complete task: a request, workspace
initialization, and outcome assessment. The routes thus expand task
supply from different resources while retaining the same requirements
for execution and checking. Depending on the task, assessment uses an
executable verifier or evaluator inspection of the declared acceptance
conditions.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/11_casespec_contract.png)

Figure 12: A concrete executable task contract. The saved
commercial-review CaseSpec binds a request, input files, startup, and
output collection. The initial-workspace illustration redraws
representative source records in Calc. Requested deliverables comprise a
refreshable dashboard, an editable two-page memo with PDF export, and an
editable four-slide review deck. Inspection requirements summarize the
query, while post-run collection gathers available files for assessment.
GUI views, requested output layouts, abbreviated filenames, and the
launch command are illustrative.

We package the output of each route in the same CaseSpec contract
introduced in
Section [2](#S2 "2Scope and System Overview ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
Figure [12](#S4.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
shows one saved commercial-review task: its request asks for a quarterly
comparison, its workbook and startup instructions establish the initial
Calc workspace, and its requirements specify the deliverables and how
they should agree.

The dashboard, memo, and review deck in
Figure [12](#S4.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
are outputs the agent is asked to create. The post-run hook gathers
available files for inspection against those requirements. This example
shows what all three routes need to establish, even though they start
from different resources.

#### 4.2.1  Scaling Intents

Applications and working materials are readily available from software
templates, public file collections, and websites. However, these
resources do not come with a varied set of natural user requests
grounded in their contents and capabilities. We design Scaling Intents
to generate such requests from the operations the software supports and
the content already present. This grounding gives the requested work
concrete subjects and feasible actions, while variation in goals and
phrasing broadens the collection. We first describe desktop base tasks,
then extend the construction to dependent long-horizon workflows and web
content.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/12_intent_construction.png)

Figure 13: Constructing a desktop base task. The selected Calc function
narrows compatible workbooks. The orders.xlsx summary grounds the Status
= Pending condition, while the persona guides request wording. CaseSpec
