binds this request and file to startup instructions and the required
saved filter. The required view retains A17 and A58 and hides A42
without deleting source rows. Software and GUI assets, records, and
setup are illustrative. The right-hand view specifies a requirement, not
an observed rollout.

##### Desktop base tasks.

For a desktop base task, we need to connect a supported software
operation to material on which it makes sense.
Figure [13](#S4.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ 4.2.1 Scaling Intents ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
illustrates the three steps used to generate the request. We first
choose an application and one of its supported functions. That choice
establishes the requirements for selecting a compatible source file. The
selected file’s content then supplies the facts from which an LLM writes
a grounded request. Once the request is defined, we prepare its starting
workspace and assessment to form the complete task shown in the lower
panel.

Select a function. We begin with E2B templates that provide installed
applications and their dependencies for office work, media, file
management, browsing, and system settings. Each application has a
profile of supported operations and readable file formats. We choose the
target application and an eligible operation according to the desired
coverage of software and functions. Selecting Calc and filtering, for
example, determines both what the eventual request will ask for and what
kind of input it needs.

Match a file. To supply these inputs, we collect tens of thousands of
files from public and open resources and supplement them with
agent-generated materials ([Ge et al., 2026](#bib.bib23)). We index
their formats and extract bounded content summaries, including
spreadsheet fields and sample values or document text excerpts. With the
function selected, we use its format and material requirements to find
usable candidates. A spreadsheet-filtering task needs a readable
workbook whose fields support a meaningful selection condition. Sampling
favors files used less often within the construction run, and the
selected files’ summaries become inputs to request generation. For
settings tasks, the relevant material is the application preference
state instead of a source file.

Write the request. We give an LLM the selected function and file
summaries, together with a persona describing the user’s role and
motivation ([Jung et al., 2026](#bib.bib34)). The function fixes the
operation, and the summaries identify the concrete subjects, fields, and
values to which it can be applied. For example, a workbook summary that
identifies a Status column containing Pending supports a request such as
_“Filter the open spreadsheet to show only the pending records and save
it.”_ The filtering condition comes from the workbook, and the persona
guides how the user expresses the need.

Assemble CaseSpec. The request now describes feasible work, but the
executing agent still needs the corresponding files and application
state. To establish this starting point, we specify where the harness
should place the selected files in the sandbox, which software template
and dependencies to use, and which application or document to open
before the first action. In the example, opening the workbook in Calc
makes “the open spreadsheet” an unambiguous reference. Tasks involving
several files instead identify them by name or workspace-relative path.

The same requested change determines the completion criteria. The
workbook should be saved with the pending-record filter applied and all
source rows retained.
Figure [13](#S4.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ 4.2.1 Scaling Intents ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
uses matched views of the initial workbook and the required saved
filter, which hides nonmatching rows without deleting them. The request,
workbook, startup instructions, and required result form one CaseSpec.
The same selection therefore supplies the content of the request, the
file the agent operates on, and the result to inspect.

##### Desktop long-horizon tasks.

Base-task generation connects a function to suitable content.
Long-horizon construction extends these local matches into a coherent
workflow, especially across applications, by defining how later stages
use information or results produced earlier ([Yuan et al.,
2026](#bib.bib99); [Ge et al., 2026](#bib.bib23)).

We therefore plan the workflow before choosing its concrete materials
and writing the request. The procedure in
Figure [14](#S4.F14 "Figure 14 ‣ Desktop long-horizon tasks. ‣ 4.2.1 Scaling Intents ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
has three steps. First, we assemble work stages into a dependency graph
so that they contribute to a shared goal. We then prepare the files and
records that make each stage executable and its required outcome
specific. Finally, we use this grounded plan to write the request and
package the starting workspace and outcome requirements. The resulting
task asks for a coherent workflow whose operations are connected by a
shared purpose and explicit prerequisites.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/10_long_workflow.png)

Figure 14: Constructing long-horizon tasks from blueprints. (1)
Blueprint selection assembles work stages and prerequisites. Solid edges
carry content dependencies. The dashed C-to-D edge sets workflow order.
A node can require several GUI actions. (2) Materials bind the approved
amount and obsolete wording to inclusion and exclusion conditions for
D’s PDF. C binds an initial record to the required budget-decision
update. (3) The grounded plan guides LLM request writing and supplies
the workspace and outcome requirements in one CaseSpec. Its two outputs
are the PDF and updated record. Discovery facts remain in the prepared
files. The structure follows a saved plan. GUI details, the shortened
request, and prospective outcomes are illustrative.

Plan the workflow. We encode recurring workflows as _scenario
blueprints_, each defining a final deliverable, candidate stages, their
applications and materials, and their prerequisites. For the office
scenario, a source review, a delivery-record update, and a final
document all contribute to preparing the same delivery package.

For each instance, target task length, application coverage, and desired
interaction challenges guide the choice of a blueprint and a compatible
stage combination. We favor less-used scenarios and components, then use
seeded sampling among eligible combinations to vary the work. A
combination is retained when its prerequisites are present and every
selected stage contributes, through its dependencies, to the overall
workflow goal. Operation variants further specify the requested result,
such as requiring the PDF’s text to remain searchable.

We represent the selected workflow as a directed acyclic graph (DAG), a
planning abstraction also used for cross-device orchestration in UFO3
([Zhang et al., 2025d](#bib.bib104)). Here the graph specifies the
task-construction plan. Each node is a work stage, such as inspecting a
document for approved content, and may require several GUI actions.
Edges can express use of earlier information or artifacts, or a required
execution order. We retain the blueprint’s dependencies and, when
constructing longer workflows, add precedence between selected stages
that use shared facts.

Figure [14](#S4.F14 "Figure 14 ‣ Desktop long-horizon tasks. ‣ 4.2.1 Scaling Intents ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(1)
shows an office-delivery example of this planning process. The shared
goal is to produce a current delivery package and reconcile its business
record. We organize that work into four stages. Source inspection (A)
establishes the approved content, which guides the search for obsolete
wording in an older deck (B). Record reconciliation (C) can begin
independently. The final PDF stage (D) uses the findings from A and B.
The blueprint also places the record update before PDF production, shown
by the dashed C-to-D edge. This edge specifies workflow order, while the
updated record remains a separate deliverable with its own check.

Before preparing the materials, we check the assembled plan for cycles,
missing prerequisites, and inconsistent production or use of files. The
checked plan associates each stage with its application, required
inputs, and expected output, so material preparation can follow the
dependencies of the workflow.

Ground the workflow in materials. The DAG specifies what each stage
should accomplish, but its nodes still need concrete information to
operate on. We ground each node by selecting or generating the files and
service records required by that subtask. Existing-file selection
follows the same principles as base-task selection, matching application
support, file format, and content. Long-horizon construction also
considers whether the selected files belong to a shared project or
directory and whether their contents support the relationships between
stages. File summaries supply the names, values, and other details used
to specify stage goals and acceptance conditions.

When the workflow calls for generated materials, we instead choose
scenario facts and place them in the files or records the agent will
inspect. The office sample follows this path in
Figure [14](#S4.F14 "Figure 14 ‣ Desktop long-horizon tasks. ‣ 4.2.1 Scaling Intents ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(2).
The approved document supplies a value that the final PDF should
include, while the older deck contains obsolete wording that the PDF
should exclude. The business record supplies the initial state for a
reconciliation update. Each material therefore has a role in both the
work stage and its outcome condition. The example requires two results:
the PDF and the updated record.

We check that the required information is available through the selected
applications and that generated files contain their assigned content.
Linking each requirement to its source and its later use keeps the
workflow grounded across stages. The output is a task plan with concrete
input files, specified initial record values, stage goals, and
corresponding outcome criteria.

Create the executable task. With the work and its materials fixed, we
use an LLM to write the user request. It receives the user-visible goal,
references to the available materials, and required constraints,
together with a selected persona and expression style. These choices
vary how users describe their work while preserving the constructed
task. Facts designated for discovery remain in the workspace for the
executing agent to find. The request can ask for a reconciled delivery
record and a searchable PDF index without revealing the values or
obsolete terms the agent needs to discover. Query checks compare the
wording with the task plan, return inconsistencies for revision, and
filter semantically duplicate requests.

Figure [14](#S4.F14 "Figure 14 ‣ Desktop long-horizon tasks. ‣ 4.2.1 Scaling Intents ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(3)
brings the request-writing, workspace, and assessment branches of the
same grounded plan together. As in base-task construction,
initialization instructions place the files and open the required
applications. Here they also prepare the business record used by the
workflow. Outcome assessment checks the requested PDF, record changes,
and source preservation. Together with the request, these components
form the CaseSpec executed by the harness. The DAG organizes task
construction, while the executing agent plans its own GUI actions.

The shared task plan keeps the goal, workspace, and assessment aligned
as the collection expands to longer, multi-application workflows.

##### Web tasks.

An existing website supplies records and pages that can be read or
changed through interface controls. We generate requests from this
material in three steps. Examining the content and controls first
establishes what work the site supports. We then select a goal involving
that content and ask an LLM to express it as a user request. The chosen
goal determines the initial records to prepare and the outcome to
assess, completing the web task.

We first identify the information visible on the relevant pages and the
controls used to access or modify it. On an illustrative task-management
site, these include task names, due dates, and status values, together
with controls for filtering the task list, opening a task, and changing
its status. This combination supports requests to find tasks with
particular properties and apply a corresponding update.

Using this account of the site, we select conditions that match records
in the starting data and an operation available through the interface.
We then use an LLM to express the grounded goal as a user request, for
example: _“Mark tasks due before 1 September 2026 that are still In
progress as Needs follow-up.”_ The due dates and current statuses
determine which tasks qualify, and the status control provides the
requested operation. Other requests use different items, conditions, and
available actions to cover information retrieval, comparison, and form
filling.

The generated request determines which initial state to prepare and
which result to assess. We record its entry page, startup instructions
to open that page in the browser, and any setup needed to make the
selected records available. In the example, the starting records
establish which task identifiers satisfy the date and status conditions.
Outcome assessment checks the updated status of those same tasks after
execution. Keeping their identities tied to the starting data lets the
check follow the requested items even after their status changes.

Across desktop and web, these constructions turn available software and
content into user requests with defined starting states and outcomes to
assess. The resulting CaseSpec binds the goal and requirements to the
selected resources and initialization instructions. The next route
starts with an existing request or reference website and builds the
workspace needed to support it.

#### 4.2.2  Scaling Environments

Scaling Intents uses the workspaces already available to generate new
requests. Sourced requests offer a complementary resource: they describe
useful work, but often omit the files, dependencies, or application
state needed to execute it. Reference websites and software issues
likewise describe interactions without necessarily providing an isolated
environment for collection. We design Scaling Environments to build
these missing execution conditions. For desktop requests, this means
preparing the files and applications assumed by the user. For Web tasks,
we collect open-source frontends and reconstruct workflows from public
URLs to create local environments that we can initialize and reuse. We
also develop repository-based frontend testing as a distinct web-agent
application, where a runnable project lets browser interaction test a
candidate code repair.

##### Desktop environments.

Forums contain user-reported software problems, while tutorials and help
resources describe practical workflows and their intended results ([Wang
et al., 2025b](#bib.bib81)). These sources provide goals grounded in
software use, but their descriptions often assume attachments or
workspace state that the collector does not have. We collect tens of
thousands of such requests and task seeds from forums, wikiHow, and help
resources. The construction problem is then to supply the inputs and
startup conditions each seed assumes while preserving the work it asks
the agent to do.

We therefore design an environment-building agent, EnvAgent, to
interpret each request and prepare its input files and startup plan.
Figure [15](#S4.F15 "Figure 15 ‣ Desktop environments. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
illustrates three construction steps. The agent first retrieves and
reads candidate files to establish which requirements are already
supported. Any missing inputs then guide file generation, using the
retrieved material to keep related records consistent. With the inputs
available, the agent specifies the request and startup plan and checks
that they agree. A failed check returns the affected input or
instruction for revision before the package becomes a CaseSpec. The
order-comparison example below shows how the retrieved and generated
materials contribute to the same task.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/13a_desktop_environment.png)

Figure 15: Request-driven desktop environment construction. EnvAgent
identifies the required resources, retrieves an order table, and uses
its identifiers to construct a missing shipment table. An LLM grounds
the sourced request in the prepared filenames and their shared Order ID
field. EnvAgent specifies startup and checks the materials and
preparation plan, revising inputs or instructions when checks fail
before producing a CaseSpec. The GUI agent subsequently performs the
requested comparison. GUI views and records are illustrative.

Prepare the input materials. EnvAgent uses the request and selected
software template to identify the applications and inputs the task
needs. Comparing orders and shipments, for example, requires two tables
that refer to the same orders, with a shared identifier through which
their records can be matched. A request restricted to a particular month
also requires the corresponding date information. We reuse the indexed
file formats and content summaries from Scaling Intents to locate
candidates, using project and date information to find related tables.
EnvAgent then reads their records to confirm that the identifiers refer
to the same orders and that the files contain both shipped and
outstanding orders for the comparison. If the retrieved materials meet
the input requirements, the agent proceeds to request and startup
specification. Otherwise, the unmet requirements guide generation.

Figure [15](#S4.F15 "Figure 15 ‣ Desktop environments. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(2)
shows an example of this step. Given a suitable order table but no
matching shipment table, EnvAgent uses file tools and reusable
construction skills to create a shipment table with matching identifiers
and business context. The resulting input set gives the requested
comparison concrete files to operate on.

Specify and check the task environment. The files establish which names
and fields the request can refer to. We use the original request and
these file details to guide an LLM’s replacement of general references
with concrete ones, preserving the goal and constraints. In the example,
the request now names the two workbooks and their shared Order ID field.
It asks for unmatched orders in a separate file and preserves any
original source-protection or time restriction. The GUI agent still
needs to identify the outstanding orders and create the result file.

The refined request also determines how execution should begin. EnvAgent
specifies that both inputs be placed in a project directory, Calc be
launched, and the order table be opened. The startup plan waits for the
application window to be ready, with the shipment table available
alongside the order table. These instructions give the harness the
sequence needed to present the files and application assumed by the
request.

Before handoff, EnvAgent checks the prepared materials and startup plan.
File tools establish whether the inputs exist and have valid formats,
while the agent reviews whether their contents support the requested
comparison, whether the template provides the required applications, and
whether startup actions place the files before opening them. Findings
such as a missing identifier field or an incomplete startup action lead
to revisions of the affected material or instruction. After a file
changes, EnvAgent submits a new inspection report before finalization,
so the report describes the materials that will actually be delivered.

The checked request, files, environment configuration, and startup plan
form the CaseSpec from which the harness creates the E2B instance and
establishes the starting application state. With the input files in
place, the GUI agent performs the comparison and saves
missing_orders.xlsx, whose contents are then checked against the
required orders alongside preservation of the source files. Construction
checks thus establish the starting conditions, while outcome assessment
evaluates the work performed from those conditions.

##### Web environments.

We collect Web frontends from open-source websites as starting points
for local task environments. To extend this pool, we also reconstruct
selected workflows from public reference URLs ([Chae et al.,
2026](#bib.bib10)). These references provide realistic pages and
interactions, while local control lets us initialize records, repeat
attempts, and construct multiple tasks within the same site.

Our URL-to-environment construction builds on WebAnything ([Xie et al.,
2026](#bib.bib89)) and follows the four steps in
Figure [16](#S4.F16 "Figure 16 ‣ Web environments. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
We first explore the reference to identify the required pages and
behavior. Those observations guide construction of a local interface
connected to backing state. Once it runs, browser tests check the
interactions and return discrepancies for repair. We then restore the
intended starting data and package the checked site for task collection.
These steps turn an external example into an interactive environment
with controlled initial conditions.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/13b_web_environment.png)

Figure 16: Reconstructing interactive websites from references. The
aligned GUI views follow record R17 from the reference site to the local
implementation and a reload test. Observed pages and interactions guide
the interface and shared backing record. The test edits and saves the
title, then reloads the record to check persistence. Discrepancies
return to implementation for repair and retesting. The checked site is
packaged with its declared initial records and runtime configuration,
separately from construction-test edits. GUI states and URLs are
illustrative.

The builder begins at the reference URL and explores the workflows
selected for reconstruction, retrieving relevant pages and accessible
frontend assets. For record editing, this covers the list page, detail
view, editing controls, and navigation between them. An existing request
can further focus the exploration. The resulting observations establish
which pages the local site needs and what each interaction should do.

We use these observations and accessible frontend assets to build the
local pages. Their interactions also specify the backend behavior to
reproduce. When the reference’s backing data or implementation is
unavailable, the builder creates a local schema, initial records, and
update logic for the observed workflow. In the record-editing example,
the list and detail views refer to the same record, and saving an edit
updates that record for subsequent visits. Linking the reconstructed
controls to these state changes makes the workflow available for actual
browser interaction. If construction raises questions about a page or
control, the builder returns to the reference site for targeted
inspection before continuing the implementation.

Once the local workflow is runnable, a browser testing agent checks its
visual correspondence with the reference and the effects of its
controls. For the illustrated check, it opens a record, edits and saves
it, and refreshes the page to inspect the stored value. An unreachable
detail page leads to a navigation repair, while a lost update leads to a
correction of the backend state change. After revision, the affected
interactions are tested again.
Figure [16](#S4.F16 "Figure 16 ‣ Web environments. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(3)
shows a persistence test on record R17. The agent changes _Delivery
plan_ to _Delivery plan v2_, saves, and reloads. Seeing the edited title
after reload provides the evidence for this particular check.

These tests modify the records that future tasks will use. Before
collection, we therefore restore the declared initial records and
entry-page state, keeping construction-test edits separate from task
setup. We package this starting state with the checked site for isolated
E2B execution. Its content and tested functionality also support Scaling
Intents, allowing the same environment to supply multiple user tasks.

##### Repository-based frontend testing.

Frontend testing is an important application of web agents because code
that builds successfully can still behave incorrectly when a user
interacts with it ([Yang et al., 2025](#bib.bib96)). We therefore treat
issue-driven testing as a distinct setting for environment construction
and experience collection. A repository supplies the application, and
its issue report specifies behavior that browser interaction can test.

Figure [17](#S4.F17 "Figure 17 ‣ Repository-based frontend testing. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
shows how browser testing guides successive code revisions. We first
prepare a runnable project and turn the issue into a fixed test
expectation. A coding agent then revises and launches the application,
making a candidate repair available to the web agent. The web agent
replays the reported interactions and compares their effects with the
expectation. Finally, its action trace, screenshots, and verdict return
to the coding agent in a test report. This evidence directs the next
repair while keeping every iteration focused on the same issue.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/13c_repository_environment.png)

Figure 17: Browser interaction guides iterative code repair. (1) The
issue fixes the expected behavior. (2) The Coding Agent inspects and
edits the relevant implementation, then launches a candidate
application. (3) The Web Test Agent edits R17, saves the new title, and
reloads the page. The stacked views show the new title after saving and
the old title after reload, with cursor overlays marking interactions. A
test report returns this discrepancy, recorded actions, and screenshots
to the Coding Agent for repair and retesting under the unchanged
expectation. Generated GUI views, code, and test feedback are
illustrative.

Environment preparation begins with GitHub frontend repositories whose
issues describe browser-reproducible behavior. The builder installs
dependencies, starts the project, and prepares the pages and data
involved in the issue. This makes the reported problem available for
direct interaction and supplies the workspace in which repairs will be
tested.

The issue then defines what to check in this workspace. Its expected
behavior supplies the coding agent’s repair objective and a test list of
browser actions and expected results for the web agent. For example, an
issue may report that an edited page title appears to save but
disappears after reload. The corresponding test changes the title, saves
it, reloads the page, and checks that the new title remains. Both the
code change and its assessment therefore address the same user-reported
problem.

The coding agent inspects the relevant implementation using the issue
and any feedback from the previous browser test. It then changes the
code and launches the revised application. These actions, illustrated by
the editor views in
Figure [17](#S4.F17 "Figure 17 ‣ Repository-based frontend testing. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(2),
supply a runnable candidate whose behavior still needs to be tested.

The web agent performs the test actions against that candidate and
captures the resulting states. A visible save confirmation establishes
what the interface showed immediately after saving. Reloading tests
whether the change persists. In the illustrated failure, the edited
title appears after save but the old title returns after reload.

The browser test report links these states to the actions that produced
them and states the discrepancy directly. The stacked views in
Figure [17](#S4.F17 "Figure 17 ‣ Repository-based frontend testing. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(3)
follow the same R17 record through editing, saving, and reloading.
Because the edited title should persist, the observed old title
identifies a discrepancy for the coding agent to investigate. This
feedback guides its next revision, which the web agent tests again
through the running interface. The issue’s expected behavior remains
fixed across iterations. This loop supplies GUI interaction trajectories
and outcome feedback tied to real development requests, with evidence
qualification and final acceptance detailed in
Appendix [F.1](#A6.SS1 "F.1 Qualifying Browser Evidence ‣ Appendix F Web Repair and Testing Protocol ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

##### Case study of a responsive homepage repair.

Figure [18](#S4.F18 "Figure 18 ‣ Case study of a responsive homepage repair. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
follows a local Mercedes-Benz GLE SUV homepage built from supplied
materials. The request called for a responsive page with usable controls
and navigation. Two defects emerged when the WebTestAgent followed a
button click over time and inspected the page at a narrow width.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/25_webtest_repair_case.png)

Figure 18: Bug-guided repair and targeted retesting. Desktop and
narrow-screen tests produce one report, which guides both code changes
before retesting. Screenshots are later replays of archived code, with
subtitle line breaks cleaned for presentation. Overlays, report
excerpts, and abbreviated patches are explanatory. R2 passed three
selected checks, including homepage appearance, without repeating all
five R1 items.

In the first browser test (R1), the WebTestAgent clicked _Learn more_
and waited. The transient notification disappeared, leaving no stable
confirmation. Resizing the page to 320 px then exposed a second defect,
with text navigation covering the centered logo. The R1 report on the
left links both findings to the actions and screenshots that exposed
them, giving the Coding Agent two specific behaviors to repair.

The central patch excerpts show how this feedback changed the code. For
the first finding, the Coding Agent added an HTML confirmation element
below the button and updated its text through the JavaScript click
handler, so the response could remain visible. For the second, a CSS
rule hid the text links at widths up to 560 px while retaining the
hamburger menu. This freed space around the logo while preserving
navigation access. The matching numbers connect each reported defect to
its corresponding patch.

With the revised page running, the Coding Agent requested a second
browser test (R2). The WebTestAgent clicked the button again, waited
three seconds, and changed the carousel slide. The inline confirmation
remained visible. At 320 px, the links no longer covered the logo, the
menu opened and closed, and the page had no horizontal overflow.
Together with a homepage visual check, these observations supported PASS
on three selected items from R1’s five-item checklist. The two reported
defects were resolved in this targeted retest.

Together, these routes expand environment supply from user requests,
reference websites, and runnable software projects. Their prepared
workspaces support experience collection, and reconstructed sites also
feed new task generation through Scaling Intents. Tasks equipped with
reliable executable verifiers offer a further route to expansion: the
next section uses their goals and outcome checks as seeds for additional
task construction.

#### 4.2.3  Scaling Verifiers

Task datasets and manually authored cases provide user goals already
paired with executable verifiers. A reliable verifier makes the intended
outcome explicit and supplies a repeatable check on collected
trajectories. Creating additional checks is costly, however, because
each needs to match its task and distinguish correct results from
plausible mistakes. We design Scaling Verifiers to expand this resource
by adapting existing task–verifier pairs ([Wang et al.,
2026a](#bib.bib78); [Lv et al., 2026](#bib.bib43)).

Figure [19](#S4.F19 "Figure 19 ‣ Expanding the work and its success conditions. ‣ 4.2.3 Scaling Verifiers ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
illustrates the construction and validation phases. We first use a
seed’s goal and success conditions to construct related tasks, changing
the request, initial state, and verifier together. The new pair then
undergoes a GUI execution trial and tests of the verifier’s judgments.
Their findings guide repairs before the pair enters collection.

This joint treatment matters because changing a request’s selection
criterion also changes which records the verifier should accept as
updated and which it should require to remain intact.

##### Expanding the work and its success conditions.

The construction agent reads the seed request, initialization, and
verifier to identify useful subgoals, their prerequisites, and the
observable changes that establish success. A subgoal can involve several
GUI actions, such as editing a document and exporting the result. We use
these subgoals in three different ways. Decomposition extracts a
meaningful part of the seed task. Variation changes the condition or
operation while retaining its basic structure. Composition connects
subgoals into a longer workflow, whose later work uses earlier results.
The approval example in
Figure [19](#S4.F19 "Figure 19 ‣ Expanding the work and its success conditions. ‣ 4.2.3 Scaling Verifiers ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
shows the effect of each choice on the requested work and the
corresponding outcome checks.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/14_verifier_expansion.png)

Figure 19: Expanding tasks and their verifiers together. The agent
extracts a subtask, changes a selection criterion, or joins approval
with export, adapting completion and preservation checks to the new
task. The lower composition example separates a GUI execution trial from
verifier tests on prepared outcomes. The verdict column gives the
requirement-derived response for each probe. Testing checks whether the
verifier returns it. The task lists, generated GUI views, and
hypothetical judgments illustrate the construction procedure. The
ellipsis omits intervening GUI actions, including export. Initial-state
preparation and repair iterations are not shown.

- •
  Decomposition turns a meaningful part of the workflow into a task of
  its own. Extracting record selection yields a request to identify the
  qualifying records, an initial state containing the required data, and
  a verifier for the returned selection and preservation of all records.
- •
  Variation changes the materials, parameters, or operations. A new
  selection criterion leads to a new target set derived from the
  corresponding input records. The request and verifier are updated to
  express and assess that change.
- •
  Composition joins compatible subgoals through their inputs and
  outputs. Adding a summary export to an approval task makes the
  approved identifiers the contents of the later file. The adapted
  verifier checks the updates, the exported summary, and preservation of
  the records the request protects. The added dependency can produce a
  more demanding multi-stage task.

Across these changes, the agent resolves the actual identifiers and file
references used by the request, environment, and verifier. In the
composition example, the targets established during selection remain the
targets during assessment, even when the update changes a field used for
selection. Desktop compositions carry the same relationship through
documents and intermediate files. Requirements that matter during a
stage are checked there, while final-state requirements are assessed at
completion.

##### Testing the task and its verifier.

Each rewritten task undergoes two complementary checks before
collection. A GUI trial examines whether an agent can carry out the
request from the prepared state. Verifier probes examine whether the
checker judges known outcomes as the task requires. The lower part of
Figure [19](#S4.F19 "Figure 19 ‣ Expanding the work and its success conditions. ‣ 4.2.3 Scaling Verifiers ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
keeps these two sources of evidence separate.

For the execution trial, we run a CUA from the declared initial state
with the rewritten request. Its interaction and resulting state reveal
problems such as missing inputs, unreachable controls, or instructions
inconsistent with the workspace. These findings identify which setup or
request needs repair.

To test the verifier itself, we construct outcome probes whose expected
verdicts follow from the request and starting data, then compare those
verdicts with the verifier’s outputs. In the approval-and-export
example, a complete result should pass, whereas an otherwise correct
update without the required export should fail. Additional probes
isolate incomplete updates, changes to protected records, and allowed
alternative outputs. A disagreement identifies a checker rule to revise,
even when the GUI task itself can be completed.

We collect trial failures and verifier disagreements into a construction
report. Setup and instruction failures return to task construction,
while incorrect verdicts return to the verifier. After the revision, we
rerun the affected tests against the same intended requirements before
admitting the pair to collection. Requirements that need contextual
judgment use the evaluator in
Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Agentic Outcome Evaluation ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
This feedback loop keeps task expansion connected to executable
conditions and meaningful outcome assessment.

The three construction routes supply tasks to the shared collection
service
(Section [3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Reliable Batch Rollout at Scale ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
Executing these tasks produces trajectories and outcome assessments.
Obtaining successful experience from the hardest tasks introduces the
next challenge.

### 4.3  Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection

The routes in
Section [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
supply executable tasks whose rollouts provide experience for learning.
During collection, however, we encounter long and difficult tasks that
remain unsolved within the ordinary budget. Collecting demonstrations of
sustained planning and recovery from these tasks requires addressing two
recurring obstacles. Repeated attempts can lose useful discoveries and
repeat ineffective procedures. A late failure can also force a new
attempt to rebuild substantial earlier work.

We design two recursive self-improvement (RSI) pipelines for collecting
experience from these tasks. Each carries forward the information needed
to address one of these obstacles.

- •
  Knowledge accumulation. Skill-guided recursive exploration turns
  trial-and-error findings into tested procedures that guide subsequent
  attempts
  (Figure [20](#S4.F20 "Figure 20 ‣ 4.3.1 Skill-Guided Recursive Exploration ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
- •
  Progress accumulation. Milestone-guided trajectory search preserves
  completed work and resumes exploration from the corresponding software
  state and agent context
  (Figure [21](#S4.F21 "Figure 21 ‣ 4.3.2 Milestone-Guided Trajectory Search ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

Both use feedback from earlier trials to shape later exploration ([Zhu
et al., 2026](#bib.bib112)). They target tasks that pass construction
checks but remain unsuccessful in ordinary collection. The original
acceptance conditions stay fixed. We check the completed task before
admitting an assisted trajectory as a full-task demonstration.
Milestone-guided collection can also retain verified subtask traces from
attempts that leave the full task unfinished.

#### 4.3.1  Skill-Guided Recursive Exploration

Some GUI tasks require a procedure that the agent has not discovered.
The difficulty may be an unfamiliar application command, a hidden
prerequisite, or a sequence whose useful effect becomes clear only after
trying it and inspecting the result. Repeated attempts can make partial
discoveries, but those discoveries remain scattered across their
histories unless the collector carries them forward. We therefore need a
way to turn trial-and-error experience into reusable operating guidance.

We design a skill-guided loop with four stages, shown in
Figure [20](#S4.F20 "Figure 20 ‣ 4.3.1 Skill-Guided Recursive Exploration ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
Task-directed exploration first produces observations about useful
operations and unresolved obstacles. A skill creator then reviews that
experience and consolidates actionable findings into written guidance. A
separate agent tests the candidate instructions before they are supplied
to a new task attempt. That attempt can use the validated procedure to
make further progress, and its new discoveries feed the next round of
skill refinement. The loop accumulates tested knowledge across attempts
while retaining full-task verification for the final result.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/15a_skill_exploration.png)

Figure 20: Accumulating operating knowledge through exploration. The
source report contains “Quarterly,” but an image-only export makes the
phrase unsearchable in the PDF. Further exploration yields a
text-preserving procedure. An agent follows its fixed instructions in a
separate trial and searches the resulting PDF. The validated skill then
guides a new attempt in the original workspace, where full-task checks
determine trajectory acceptance. The return arrow depicts a failed task
attempt, while failed validation also prompts revision. Accepted
trajectories retain the supplied skill in context. GUI scenes, controls,
actions, and outcomes are illustrative, with intermediate exploratory
trials omitted.

##### Explore the task and identify obstacles.

We use a computer-use agent to attempt the original query in its
prepared workspace. It inspects the relevant applications and files,
tries controls, and adjusts its action sequence as it observes their
effects. An unsuccessful attempt can still reveal an important
prerequisite, an ineffective action to avoid, or a way to reach a
previously inaccessible state. Successful operations likewise show
procedures worth retaining. We examine these findings in the trajectory
and result, using evaluator observations of the live state to help
identify the remaining obstacle
(Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Agentic Outcome Evaluation ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"))
([Shinn et al., 2023](#bib.bib72)).

In the representative report workflow in
Figure [20](#S4.F20 "Figure 20 ‣ 4.3.1 Skill-Guided Recursive Exploration ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
exploration may reveal several export options. One trial produces a PDF
with all the required pages but represents their text as an image. The
searchability check then focuses further trials on which export settings
preserve text. The initial exploration has thus turned a broad
completion goal into a specific procedural question that can be tested
in the application.

##### Consolidate a candidate skill.

The skill creator reviews the accumulated trajectories and their
outcomes. When the useful procedure is still unclear, further
exploration or focused practice tests alternative operations. The
creator turns the resulting findings into textual guidance that states
when the procedure applies, which actions to take, and how to check its
intended effect ([Wang et al., 2024](#bib.bib79); [Wang et al.,
2026c](#bib.bib84)). For the PDF example, this means recording the
text-preserving export settings and a search check, together with the
image-only choice to avoid. The candidate gives later attempts a
concrete procedure and the relevant pitfall.

##### Validate the skill independently.

Guidance inferred from exploratory trials may omit a prerequisite or
misidentify why an operation worked. We therefore freeze the candidate
and give it to a separate agent in an independent validation trial. The
agent applies the instructions and checks their claimed effect in the
environment.

In the PDF example, it exports using the specified settings and searches
for a phrase from the source document. Finding the phrase establishes
the intended effect of this skill, while a failed check returns the
instructions to exploration and revision. The revised candidate is
tested again before admission for reuse.

##### Reuse the skill and continue exploration.

We provide the validated guidance to a new attempt at the original task.
The agent can now use the tested procedure when it reaches the relevant
operation. If it encounters another obstacle, or discovers a useful way
to complete a later stage, that experience feeds the same consolidation
and validation loop. Previously validated skills remain available, so
each round can build on what earlier attempts established.

The process continues until an attempt satisfies the original task’s
full acceptance conditions or the exploration budget is exhausted.
Accepted attempts supply trajectories with the actual guidance retained
in their decision context. The skill library also makes a tested
procedure available to later tasks that meet its applicability
conditions. This accumulation is designed to improve hard-task
collection by carrying useful knowledge between attempts. The TERRA
dashboard case in
Section [4.3.3](#S4.SS3.SSS3 "4.3.3 Case Studies of Knowledge and Progress Accumulation ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
follows how a charting problem becomes revised guidance that a fresh
attempt applies
(Figure [22](#S4.F22 "Figure 22 ‣ 4.3.3 Case Studies of Knowledge and Progress Accumulation ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

For a long workflow, however, even a better-informed attempt can spend
substantial effort repeating earlier work. The next pipeline addresses
that remaining cost by retaining execution progress.

#### 4.3.2  Milestone-Guided Trajectory Search

A long task can be difficult to finish in a single attempt even when its
individual stages are within the agent’s reach. When a late failure ends
an attempt, restarting from the initial state repeats work already
completed. This motivates a second RSI strategy in which collection
focuses on smaller, verifiable goals and retains the work that reaches
them.

We design milestone-guided trajectory search to implement this strategy.
We first decompose the original task into dependent milestones and their
checks. GUI attempts then work toward one milestone at a time, saving
the workspace and agent context when a check passes. Further attempts
restore that checkpoint to explore the next stage, following the
principle of returning to useful states for exploration ([Ecoffet
et al., 2021](#bib.bib21)). After the final milestone, the original
verifier checks the complete result before the selected path becomes a
full-task demonstration.
Figure [21](#S4.F21 "Figure 21 ‣ 4.3.2 Milestone-Guided Trajectory Search ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
illustrates how this process builds successful continuations on retained
progress. It reduces the work each trial needs to repeat while keeping
acceptance tied to the original task. A construction case in
Section [4.3.3](#S4.SS3.SSS3 "4.3.3 Case Studies of Knowledge and Progress Accumulation ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
follows this accumulation of progress from an empty Blender scene to a
complete collected trajectory
(Figure [23](#S4.F23 "Figure 23 ‣ Knowledge accumulation in a dashboard task. ‣ 4.3.3 Case Studies of Knowledge and Progress Accumulation ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/15b_milestone_search.png)

Figure 21: Retaining progress for branching trajectory search.
Checkpoint M2 pairs the prepared report and software state with the
agent context. Independent continuations restore this checkpoint to try
different exports. The example contrasts image-only, text-preserving,
and incomplete single-page exports. The lower panel retains the common
prefix and the chosen continuation as one candidate path. The original
task verifier checks the PDF, earlier record update, and source
preservation before accepting the trajectory. Failed continuations leave
the parent available for further attempts. GUI states and diagnostic
outcomes are illustrative.

##### Define milestones and their checks.

We use a planning agent to read the user request and the original
verifier code, identify dependencies, and propose meaningful
intermediate goals. Each milestone pairs a subtask with a check on its
observable result. The goal should represent useful progress and leave a
state from which later work can continue. Reviewing the pairs against
the original requirements ensures that local checks support the intended
full task. This uses the decomposition principle in
Section [4.2.3](#S4.SS2.SSS3 "4.2.3 Scaling Verifiers ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
here to guide collection within one long task.

For the office workflow in
Figure [21](#S4.F21 "Figure 21 ‣ 4.3.2 Milestone-Guided Trajectory Search ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
the original goal includes updating a business record and producing a
searchable PDF. We organize it into three milestones: complete the
record update (M1), prepare the report (M2), and export the searchable
PDF (M3). Their checks inspect the corresponding records, document
content, and output file. The executing agent receives the permitted
subtask guidance, while private verifier constants remain in the
checking context.

##### Preserve progress and explore continuations.

The harness supplies the first subtask to a computer-use agent in the
initial workspace and runs repeated attempts within the collection
budget. When an attempt passes the milestone check, we save an E2B
workspace snapshot together with the agent context about completed work,
useful findings, and remaining goals. Restoring both gives the next
attempt a consistent starting point for the following milestone
(Section [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Sustaining Long-Horizon Interaction ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
We retain the full interaction prefix alongside the bounded decision
context and can keep several passing states to support different
continuations.

Retained checkpoints form the nodes of a search tree, and GUI action
sequences toward the next milestone form its branches. Each continuation
restores its parent checkpoint in an isolated instance, observes the
interface, and attempts the next stage. Passing results become
checkpoints for the next milestone. If a continuation stalls or fails
its check, its parent remains available so that a new attempt can try a
different sequence without replaying the earlier stages.

We can also retain selected recoverable failed states for further
exploration. These states keep their unresolved milestone labels. A
continuation attempts the necessary correction before advancing to later
goals, allowing the collected path to include genuine recovery from an
earlier mistake. Thus the tree can support both alternative attempts
from verified progress and recovery attempts from a useful but
incomplete state.

In
Figure [21](#S4.F21 "Figure 21 ‣ 4.3.2 Milestone-Guided Trajectory Search ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
branches A and C fail the export requirement, while B produces a
searchable PDF. All three begin at M2, retaining the same earlier record
update and report preparation. A failed export can also trigger the
skill exploration described above, whose validated guidance can be used
by a new continuation. The saved checkpoint preserves the work already
done, and the skill helps the agent choose how to perform the unresolved
operation.

##### Verify outcomes and retain useful traces.

After the final milestone, the original task verifier assesses candidate
completions against all requirements, including those established in
earlier stages. For the office task, it checks the PDF, the required
record update, and source preservation. Search continues until a branch
passes this final check or the budget is exhausted. A passing branch
yields a _search-assisted trajectory_: the actual parent–child path from
the initial state to completion, combining its retained prefix with the
selected continuations. It keeps the path’s observations, actions, and
supplied guidance, including mistakes followed by recovery. Failed
sibling branches remain exploration records and are not spliced into the
accepted trajectory.

If an attempt completes only part of the milestone sequence, we retain
only the trace portions associated with passed milestones as
subtask-level demonstrations, preserving their original decision
context. This recovers useful supervision from unfinished attempts and
makes fuller use of the collection budget.

#### 4.3.3  Case Studies of Knowledge and Progress Accumulation

We use two case studies to explain how the RSI strategies collect
experience on difficult tasks. The first follows skill refinement while
building a live retail dashboard in Calc. The second follows milestone
accumulation while constructing a greenhouse cafe in Blender. In each
case, the recorded attempts show what goes wrong and how retaining
knowledge or progress helps subsequent attempts complete the task.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/26_terra_skill_case.png)

Figure 22: Skill refinement in the TERRA dashboard case. Numbered
annotations track chart orientation (1) and series colors (2) through
exploration, revised guidance, and a fresh attempt. Attempt 1 repairs
the orientation during execution but fails the final palette check.
Attempt 2 applies skill v2 and passes all 26 final checks. Chart crops
and the final PDF come from recorded artifacts. Window frames and
equivalent Python excerpts are composed for presentation.

##### Knowledge accumulation in a dashboard task.

Figure [22](#S4.F22 "Figure 22 ‣ 4.3.3 Case Studies of Knowledge and Progress Accumulation ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
starts with a request to build a quarterly operations dashboard for
TERRA from sales records and store targets. The deliverables are an
editable workbook with live calculations and charts, and a matching
one-page PDF. The request specifies grouped vertical columns and a navy,
teal, and orange palette. The difficulty is translating these visual
requirements into Calc’s orientation and color settings, whose effects
need to be checked in the saved workbook and exported PDF.

In Attempt 1, the agent follows skill v1, which asks for grouped columns
and final document inspection. These instructions leave the relevant API
settings unspecified. The agent sets Vertical=True through the legacy
chart interface, producing the horizontal bars shown in the top row. It
discovers the correct setting and fixes the direction during this
attempt, but the charts retain the default blue and red series colors.
The final palette check therefore rejects the delivery.

Reviewing this trajectory identifies the two changes shown in the middle
of the figure. Skill v2 records Vertical=False as the setting for
vertical columns and adds explicit series-color assignment through
chart2, followed by property readback and visual inspection. Engineering
probes check these procedures before the revised skill is fixed in a new
attempt’s context. Attempt 2 then starts from the original input in a
fresh workspace. It applies the orientation setting and assigns teal to
net sales and orange to the target series before exporting the
dashboard. The bottom row shows the resulting columns, palette, and PDF.
The completed task passes all 26 final checks, including input-change
tests of the live formulas and charts. Earlier trial-and-error has thus
become precise operating guidance that the next attempt can apply to
produce a verified demonstration.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/24_milestone_case.png)

Figure 23: Milestone-guided construction of ORBIT BOTANICA. Three
display milestones group the recorded checks. Teal frames mark one
retained path, and arrows preserve ancestry through omitted intermediate
steps. Branches can complete later work early. The equipped checkpoint
and its failed finishing continuation share the unchanged opaque-glass
render. Both successful finishing branches pass the full-task checks.
Scene crops are unretouched, with frames and diagnostic annotations
added for presentation.

##### Progress accumulation in a 3D modeling task.

Figure [23](#S4.F23 "Figure 23 ‣ Knowledge accumulation in a dashboard task. ‣ 4.3.3 Case Studies of Knowledge and Progress Accumulation ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
follows a request to build ORBIT BOTANICA, a futuristic greenhouse cafe,
from an empty Blender scene and deliver an editable model with its final
render. The task combines structural, equipment, and appearance
requirements, so an error in the final render can arise after much of
the construction is complete.

We apply the milestone-guided procedure in
Section [4.3.2](#S4.SS3.SSS2 "4.3.2 Milestone-Guided Trajectory Search ‣ 4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
to organize construction into checkable intermediate goals. The figure
groups the recorded checks into three display milestones and shows
selected branches. At M1, the agent builds the platform, frame, and
roof. The left branch fails because its upper ring misses the posts,
whereas the other two displayed branches produce connected frames.
Saving these passing scenes with their agent contexts allows exploration
to continue from established structures.

At M2, the continuations add the interior and solar equipment. The right
branch misplaces solar cells and fails the equipment check. The two
continuations from the central M1 checkpoint pass, preserving the
completed structure and equipment for finishing work.

At M3, the displayed continuations all start from the central equipped
checkpoint. One leaves the glass opaque, obscuring the interior and
failing the finishing requirements. The other two produce clear-glass
renders and pass the full-task checks. Because the equipped checkpoint
remains available, these finishing trials can address the remaining
rendering requirements without rebuilding the frame or furnishing the
cafe. The teal frames trace one retained path through all three
milestones. Once its final result passes the original task verifier, the
interaction prefix and successful continuations along that path form a
complete demonstration. Failed siblings remain search records. This case
shows how preserving checked intermediate work lets collection
concentrate further trials on the unresolved part of a long task.

Together, the cases illustrate two ways to carry useful experience
forward. TERRA reuses a procedure learned from earlier execution, while
ORBIT BOTANICA reuses the work already completed in the software
environment. Both routes direct subsequent attempts toward unresolved
requirements and retain verified task trajectories for learning.
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Hard-Task Collection Protocol ‣ Appendix C Task Construction Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
details checkpoint contents, search budgets, skill records, and the
treatment of complete paths and checkpoint continuations.

##### Summary.

The Experience Flywheel supplies the staged learner with both
demonstrations and opportunities for fresh interaction. Curated
grounding examples support control localization, while selected
trajectories demonstrate complete workflows. RSI expands the candidate
pool with attempts at difficult tasks. The same executable tasks can
support new rollouts with task-specific outcome feedback as the policy
develops.
Section [5](#S5 "5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
explains how the learner uses these complementary resources.
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
describes data sources, scale, and source-grouped splits, with
construction details in
Appendix [C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Environment Construction and Binding ‣ Appendix C Task Construction Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

## 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience

The Experience Library assembled by the Experience Flywheel provides
quality-selected demonstrations of software work, alongside grounding
examples and executable tasks for further collection
(Section [4](#S4 "4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
The next step is to turn these resources into a policy that can reason
about interfaces and carry out user goals across desktop and web
applications. We design a three-stage learning pipeline that develops
this capability progressively, as outlined in
Figure [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1Introduction ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

1.  1.  Broad GUI mid-training first establishes familiarity with controls,
        application functions, and their effects through diverse software
        experience.
2.  2.  Verified long-horizon SFT builds on that foundation and concentrates
        learning on high-quality demonstrations that carry dependent work to
        completion.
3.  3.  Joint desktop–web agentic reinforcement learning then lets the
        policy learn from its own decisions and their outcomes in
        interactive environments.

Each stage uses the preceding policy as its starting point while
changing the learning emphasis, from operational breadth to sustained
completion and then outcome-driven improvement.

Table [3](#S5.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
summarizes the resources and priorities of the three stages. The shared
interfaces of the NorthCUA Harness connect demonstration collection with
online execution, so the policy can continue using the same observation
and action conventions as training progresses. We describe each stage
below.
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
defines the supervised prediction units and how recorded RL attempts
become learning samples.

For model training, all spatial coordinates, including grounding targets
and coordinate-based action arguments, use a shared $`[0,1000]`$
representation. For an image of width $`W`$ and height $`H`$, a pixel
location $`(x,y)`$ becomes $`(1000x/W,1000y/H)`$, giving the model a
consistent coordinate convention across screen resolutions.

[TABLE]

Table 3: Learning priorities, data selection, and scale across the three
stages. Supervised scale entries are rounded cohort summaries
(Table [16](#A4.T16 "Table 16 ‣ Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
GUI steps count interactions within trajectories. Grounding examples are
counted separately, as are auxiliary evaluator demonstrations. RL counts
describe the prepared task pool.

### 5.1  Broad GUI Mid-Training

Computer-use agents encounter many applications, interface layouts, and
ways of performing similar operations. To work across these settings, a
policy needs experience recognizing controls, interpreting their
functions, and observing how actions change software state. We use broad
GUI mid-training to build this foundation from diverse demonstrations
([Wang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib83); [Jung et al., 2026](#bib.bib34)).

Through the collection routes in the Experience Flywheel, the reported
broad task corpus contains over 100,000 trajectories and over 4 million
GUI interaction steps, spanning dozens of applications and hundreds of
operation families. This range exposes the policy to different tasks,
materials, and interfaces within and across applications. We complement
the trajectories with approximately 40,000 single-step grounding
examples to train spatial recognition and control localization directly.
The following collection and selection choices determine what experience
enters this mixture.

##### Broad software coverage.

We select commonly used desktop applications and organize their
functions into operation families. These profiles guide the task
requests and CaseSpec environments used for experience collection,
making it possible to vary the operations demonstrated within each
application. Sampling across applications and operations, together with
variation in source files and user goals, broadens this coverage.
Representative coverage in the application profiles and workflow
templates includes:

- •
  _Office work._ Calc tasks cover formulas, auditing and reconciliation,
  filtering, pivot tables, and print layout. Writer adds document
  formatting and annotation, while Impress covers slide composition,
  content insertion, styling, and synthesis of source material into
  presentations.
- •
  _Visual and media work._ GIMP tasks include cropping, resizing,
  annotation, and export. Inkscape adds vector text, color, alignment,
  and grouping operations. Image viewers, PDF viewers, and VLC provide
  content inspection and playback tasks.
- •
  _Workspace and information management._ File-manager tasks cover
  organization and batch processing. Thunderbird, browsers, and VS Code
  add message handling, browser configuration, information lookup, and
  GUI-based file editing.
- •
  _CAD and 3D workflows._ Expanded workflow templates define model
  review and part design in FreeCAD and SolveSpace, together with scene
  organization in Blender.
- •
  _Medical-image and anatomy visualization._ 3D Slicer scenarios cover
  inspecting image volumes, checking orientation, annotating landmarks,
  and saving review scenes.

To extend this coverage beyond individual functions, we also use the
long-workflow construction in
Section [4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1 "4.2.1 Scaling Intents ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
Its dependent tasks provide experience combining operations, carrying
information between applications, and maintaining progress toward a
shared deliverable. Mid-training therefore includes sustained workflows
as part of its broad foundation, before the next stage concentrates on
selected verified examples.

We further enrich web experience with (1) information retrieval, (2)
form filling, (3) human-verification flows, and (4) frontend testing.
Retrieval and form filling represent common everyday uses of websites.
Human-verification flows add visually conditioned interactions
encountered during web use, while frontend testing exercises the effects
of controls and the persistence of state changes. Together, these
settings diversify both what users ask and how an agent must interact,
complementing desktop operation with web-specific workflows. The web
scenario library also provides browser-game and sliding-puzzle tasks.
Grounding examples add direct practice locating the requested control,
including specialized controls handled by the documentation-guided route
in
Section [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Single-Step UI Grounding ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
Table [4](#S5.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Broad software coverage. ‣ 5.1 Broad GUI Mid-Training ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
summarizes the coverage of these sources, while
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives their rounded counts by domain.

|                        |                                                                                                            |
| ---------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Source                 | Representative coverage                                                                                    |
| Desktop base tasks     | Office operations, visual/media work, and workspace/information management                                 |
| Desktop long workflows | Cross-source reasoning, conflicting-information resolution, and dependent work toward a shared deliverable |
| Web tasks              | Information retrieval, form filling, human-verification flows, and frontend testing                        |
| Single-step grounding  | Locating requested controls in desktop and web screenshots                                                 |
| Evaluator trajectories | Tool-assisted inspection and evidence-based assessment of task outcomes                                    |

Table 4: Representative coverage of broad mid-training sources. Dataset
scale and sampling conventions appear in
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
and
Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Supervised Representation and Sampling ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

##### Outcome-based trajectory curation.

Broad coverage is useful for learning only when the collected behavior
provides a worthwhile demonstration. Large-scale attempts can also leave
tasks incomplete or spend many actions without progress. We therefore
use the agent-as-a-judge evaluator in
Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Agentic Outcome Evaluation ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
to inspect each candidate’s requested outcome, trajectory, and resulting
artifacts or state ([Zhuge et al., 2025](#bib.bib114)). Its assessment
selects completed work with useful execution behavior for the training
corpus. This selection limits the inclusion of incomplete or low-quality
traces while preserving examples of successful software use across the
coverage pool.

Among the trajectories that pass review, selection preserves broad
application and operation coverage. Meaningful exploration and recovery
remain part of this experience when they lead to completed work, giving
the model examples of how actions and subsequent observations inform the
next decision. Outcome-based filtering thus makes quality control part
of building a large and varied training corpus.

##### Learning localization, interaction, and assessment.

The selected mixture teaches both where to act and how to continue a
task. For grounding examples, the model predicts the target control’s
coordinates from the request and screenshot ([Cheng et al.,
2024](#bib.bib13); [Lu et al., 2024](#bib.bib42)). Trajectories train
the model to predict each recorded assistant response from the task and
the observations and history available at that decision. Targets include
recorded reasoning, text responses, and action tool calls, while
screenshots and tool results provide context. This response-level
objective connects interpreting the interface, deciding what to do, and
issuing the operation.

We also include evaluator trajectories as a separate judgment-task
source. These examples condition the model on the evaluator’s inspection
context and train its recorded assessment responses, providing
demonstrations of how to check an outcome and support a judgment with
evidence. Actor demonstrations retain their original decision-time
context, separate from these evaluation inputs. The evaluator therefore
serves two distinct purposes in mid-training: its reports select task
demonstrations, and its own trajectories teach assessment.
Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Supervised Representation and Sampling ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
distinguishes the sample types and their response targets.

Through this broad mixture, mid-training develops familiarity with
diverse GUI functions, action effects, and ways of interpreting results.
This provides the operational foundation for learning more demanding
workflows. The next stage sharpens that foundation using a concentrated
set of high-quality, verified long-horizon demonstrations.

### 5.2  Verified Long-Horizon SFT

Mid-training gives the policy broad experience operating software,
including extended workflows. Sustaining correct work over a long and
difficult task requires a more concentrated learning signal: later
decisions need to use earlier findings, preserve constraints, and
complete the final deliverable. We therefore continue from the
mid-trained checkpoint with supervised fine-tuning on selected
long-horizon trajectories whose complete outcomes pass executable task
verifiers ([Yuan et al., 2026](#bib.bib99); [Lu et al.,
2026](#bib.bib41)). The cohort contains approximately 3,000 trajectories
and 310,000 GUI interaction steps.

##### Selecting verified demonstrations.

We start with tasks and executable checks supplied by Scaling Verifiers
(Section [4.2.3](#S4.SS2.SSS3 "4.2.3 Scaling Verifiers ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"))
and collect successful completions. For difficult tasks that ordinary
attempts do not solve, the skill-guided and milestone-guided RSI routes
in
Section [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
provide additional ways to obtain candidate demonstrations. Skills carry
useful operating knowledge between attempts, while retained checkpoints
let exploration build on completed intermediate work. In either route, a
candidate enters this stage only after its final result passes the
original task verifier.
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
lists the source contributions that remain to be reported separately
from this aggregate cohort.

Within this accepted pool, we prioritize difficult workflows with
long-range dependencies while maintaining application diversity. We use
length to identify sustained work, then inspect how later actions use
earlier results to assess its dependencies. We then check whether these
demonstrations provide usable training sequences. Data cleaning rejects
unusable records and traces with malformed or unmatched tool calls or
unproductive loops. This screening retains coherent recovery when the
trace shows an error, its correction, and verified completion. Each
retained trace keeps its original action–observation sequence,
preserving the context needed to learn that recovery. These checks
combine programmatic outcome acceptance with demonstrations of
purposeful work across stages.

##### Learning sustained completion.

With this selected corpus, we fine-tune the mid-trained policy to
predict the recorded assistant responses from the request and
decision-time context. The objective remains response prediction, but
the data now emphasize maintaining task state, using intermediate
results, coordinating work across applications, and recovering without
losing the original goal. For assisted demonstrations, any supplied
skill or milestone guidance remains in the context under which the
recorded response was produced. Representation and sampling requirements
are described in
Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Supervised Representation and Sampling ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

This stage strengthens sustained task execution on top of the broad
operational foundation. The resulting policy is the starting point for
autonomous rollouts, where its own choices can create states absent from
the demonstrations. Reinforcement learning provides the next learning
signal by connecting those choices to their task outcomes.

### 5.3  Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning
