Mid-training and long-horizon SFT provide a policy that can recognize
interfaces and follow demonstrated workflows. To develop its ability
further, we let it learn from autonomous interaction. Its own choices
determine which states it encounters, and task outcomes provide feedback
on whether those choices accomplish the user’s goal. We use agentic
reinforcement learning to connect this fresh experience to policy
improvement ([Qi et al., 2025](#bib.bib58); [Lai et al.,
2026](#bib.bib37)).

We train a single policy jointly on desktop and web tasks. The shared
runtime in the NorthCUA Harness already expresses both as repeated
observation, reasoning, and tool-based action, making it natural to
learn from them through one training loop. Platform-specific
observations and tools retain the differences needed to operate each
interface. Task-specific verifiers then provide outcome feedback for the
work performed in either domain.

Figure [24](#S5.F24 "Figure 24 ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
follows an attempt from task preparation to a policy update. The Task
and Environment Manager selects executable cases and prepares their
isolated workspaces and verifiers, giving NorthCUA Rollout Workers the
conditions needed to act with the current policy. Once those attempts
have been recorded and scored, the Experience Adapter pairs the model
calls with their task outcomes to construct training samples. These
task-grouped samples let the Policy Learner compare alternative attempts
with GRPO and update the shared policy ([Shao et al., 2024](#bib.bib70);
[Zhu et al., 2025](#bib.bib113)). Returning the new weights to the
workers closes the loop, so subsequent interaction uses the updated
model. The following subsections explain how we select useful tasks,
obtain comparable feedback, and preserve executed decisions as that
feedback enters learning.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/17_agentic_rl.png)

Figure 24: Joint desktop–web agentic reinforcement learning. The manager
selects tasks, binds verifiers, and prepares isolated environments.
Workers use a model checkpoint to act through desktop or web interfaces.
Manager-run checks supply outcomes before workspace release. The adapter
binds outcomes to training samples while preserving each recorded
input–response pair and task/attempt identity. The learner uses GRPO to
compare valid attempts within the same task, initial-state, and verifier
group, then returns an updated checkpoint to workers. Worker and learner
icons denote execution and training uses of the same model. GUI scenes
and binary outcomes are illustrative. The example leaves task-group size
unspecified.

#### 5.3.1  Selecting Tasks with Outcome Variation

GRPO obtains its task-level learning signal by comparing the rewards of
multiple attempts at the same task. An attempt’s relative outcome
determines its advantage within that group. If all valid attempts
receive the same terminal reward, their terminal group-relative
advantages are zero. Since GUI rollouts are costly, we first identify
tasks on which the SFT policy produces different outcomes, so initial
collection can provide informative comparisons.

For each model, the Task and Environment Manager starts from
human-reviewed desktop and web tasks associated with its SFT data. For
each candidate case, we collect at least five rollouts under the SFT
checkpoint with fixed task and execution settings. The task verifier
scores each attempt’s outcome, and we select cases with variation among
these valid verifier scores. Valid task outcomes determine this
selection, while environment and verifier errors are recorded
separately. This focuses RL collection on tasks where outcome feedback
distinguishes alternative executions.

The selected cases form the initial RL task pool. Fresh grouped attempts
supply the subsequent learning data.
Appendix [E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Task Groups and Terminal Feedback ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
describes the screening protocol and the fields needed to record valid
repeats and the selection rule for each run.

#### 5.3.2  Joint Rollout and Task-Relative Feedback

With the task pool selected, we generate fresh attempts and turn their
outcomes into the comparisons used by GRPO. For each case, the manager
first establishes equivalent starting conditions. Workers then act in
those environments, and the task verifier assesses each completed
attempt. The recorded decisions and outcome are converted into samples
for policy learning.

The Task and Environment Manager prepares a separate E2B sandbox for
each attempt in a task group. All members receive the same request,
initial files and application state, verifier, and execution budget.
Their sandboxes are isolated, so one attempt’s edits do not change
another’s starting conditions. This makes differences in outcomes
interpretable as alternative executions of the same task.

NorthCUA Rollout Workers use the policy to carry out each multi-turn
attempt. Desktop and web execution follow the same runtime loop, using
their respective screen or browser observations and allowed tools. After
execution, the manager runs the task verifier on the resulting
workspace. Its valid verdict supplies the complete attempt’s terminal
reward. Both successful and unsuccessful attempts contribute to the
comparison, while setup or evaluation errors follow the separate
invalid-attempt path.

The Experience Adapter binds each outcome to the model calls from that
attempt. It reconstructs training samples from the recorded multimodal
inputs and responses, retains behavior-policy likelihoods and versions,
and marks which response tokens are learning targets. Each sample keeps
its task and attempt identity so that the Policy Learner can compute
relative advantages within the correct task group and apply the GRPO
update. Desktop and web groups contribute to the same shared policy
through this procedure.

This completes the path from an executable case to outcome-driven
learning. For long GUI attempts, however, converting the record into
training samples requires additional care. The next subsection explains
the two rules we use to preserve both the original decision conditions
and the attempt’s learning contribution during that conversion.

#### 5.3.3  Preserving Executed Decisions for Learning

The rollout procedure provides complete attempts and their outcomes, but
the learner must also preserve the conditions under which each decision
was made. Our harness maintains a sliding window of recent screenshots
and can compact earlier history. As a task proceeds, an image may leave
the window or a summary may replace earlier messages. Using only the
final history to train every response would therefore change the
evidence available to some decisions. Preserving the sampled input is
also a central concern in harnessed agentic RL ([He et al.,
2026](#bib.bib29)).

To accommodate this context management, we design the Experience Adapter
around two preservation rules. First, each response is learned with its
recorded decision-time input, starting a new training segment when the
history no longer extends consistently. Second, those segments retain
the reward attribution and learning weight of their original complete
attempt. This matters because frequent context changes can split one
attempt into more segments than another.
Figure [25](#S5.F25 "Figure 25 ‣ 5.3.3 Preserving Executed Decisions for Learning ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
illustrates input preservation in panel (a) and attempt-level comparison
and aggregation in panel (b). Together, the two rules let the harness
manage long histories without turning a representation change into a
different learning problem.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/18_rl_context.png)

Figure 25: Context-preserving RL sample construction. (a) The earlier
input contains Overview and Editing. Appending the typing result
preserves history, so Enter title and Save share one segment. After the
successful Save action, Overview leaves the visual window (cross),
starting a new segment. The highlighted label marks the latest
observation. Gray objects supply context, while teal responses and token
spans contribute to policy loss. (b) Task-group outcomes determine one
advantage per attempt, shared across its segments. In the
trajectory-mean configuration, masked policy losses are pooled and
normalized over each attempt’s target tokens before averaging across
attempts
(Eq. [1](#S5.E1 "In Preserve the complete attempt’s learning weight. ‣ 5.3.3 Preserving Executed Decisions for Learning ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
Panel (a) shows one continuous attempt under one behavior-policy
version, with earlier images unchanged until eviction. GUI states,
responses, and outcomes are illustrative. Thumbnails are input excerpts,
strip lengths are schematic, and earlier calls are omitted.

##### Preserve decision-time inputs.

Training associates each response with the context that produced it. To
preserve this association through context changes, we capture each model
call’s input, delivered response, generation log-probabilities, and
behavior-policy version. The adapter joins consecutive calls only when
their text and ordered image histories form a confirmed prefix extension
under the same version. Image identity includes its content digest, so
replacing a clean frame with the historical action overlay from
Section [3.2.1](#S3.SS2.SSS1 "3.2.1 Action-Aware Visual Context ‣ 3.2 Sustaining Long-Horizon Interaction ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
also breaks this prefix. The adapter starts a new segment from the
captured input whenever images are replaced or evicted, a summary
rewrites history, or the behavior version changes.

Earlier responses can still appear as history in a later segment, but
they serve only as context there, following the history-masking
principle used in ScaleCUA ([Lv et al., 2026](#bib.bib43)). Response
masks assign each learning target to its original occurrence. Save is
supervised when first generated and serves as context for Finish. This
construction lets the learner use the harness’s actual decision
conditions while keeping only new responses as targets.
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
specifies processor alignment and masks.

##### Preserve the complete attempt’s learning weight.

Preserving inputs can produce different numbers and lengths of segments
across attempts. If these segments were treated as separate
reward-bearing attempts, more frequent context changes would alter the
group comparison. Averaging segment losses independently would also give
an attempt more weight simply because it was split more often. Neither
change reflects additional task experience.

We therefore retain the complete attempt as the comparison unit. GRPO
computes its relative advantage once, and all of its segments receive
that same value. In
Figure [25](#S5.F25 "Figure 25 ‣ 5.3.3 Preserving Executed Decisions for Learning ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")b,
the two segments of attempt 1 share $`A_{1}`$, while attempt 2 receives
$`A_{2}`$. Loss aggregation then preserves the same unit. In the
supported trajectory-mean configuration, we use the trajectory-level
normalization also adopted in Qwen-CUA ([Lu et al., 2026](#bib.bib41)).
We sum the masked policy losses over all target tokens of an attempt,
divide by its total target-token count, and average these per-attempt
means:

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{policy}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{s\in S_{i}}\sum_{t}m_{ist}\ell_{ist}}{\displaystyle\max\!\left(1,\sum_{s\in S_{i}}\sum_{t}m_{ist}\right)},
``` |  | (1) |

where $`N`$ is the number of complete attempts, $`S_{i}`$ contains the
segments of attempt $`i`$, $`m_{ist}`$ selects target tokens, and
$`\ell_{ist}`$ is the token-level policy loss. All segments of an
attempt enter the same optimizer step, so the denominator covers the
complete attempt across microbatches.
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
defines the reduction and the run-specific optimization fields. This
normalization prevents segmentation alone from increasing an attempt’s
contribution when its target tokens and losses are unchanged. Combined
with decision-time input preservation, it allows one continuous
execution to yield several training segments without creating extra
reward comparisons or increasing the attempt’s weight in the policy
loss.

#### 5.3.4  Continuous Rollout and Policy Updates

Training from long GUI rollouts is costly even after the samples are
correctly constructed. Overlapping screenshot windows repeat visual
preprocessing and carry large tensors to the learner. Meanwhile, task
durations vary, so waiting for every active task before updating the
policy can delay the next learning cycle. We address these two costs
separately: visual-delivery mechanisms reduce redundant input
preparation and transfer, while asynchronous coordination lets policy
updates proceed without restarting ongoing tasks.

##### Deliver visual experience efficiently.

Adjacent decision windows often contain unchanged screenshots.
Processing every window from scratch repeats work on those images. For a
supported per-image processor, the Experience Adapter therefore reuses
the preprocessing tensors of unchanged images within an attempt and
processes only new images. It reconstructs the requested image order
under the same processor settings. Changed images or settings require
fresh processing. The reuse saves CPU preprocessing while retaining
every image in the model input. The neural visual encoder still
processes the complete visual input.

Large image tensors also add delivery cost after preprocessing. The
object-store path supports lossless tensor compression, retaining a
compressed payload only when it is smaller. The learner decodes the
microbatch it is about to consume, recovering the same values, shape,
and type. Bounded CPU workers prepare samples, and group admission
counts completed work awaiting delivery so that preparation does not
create an unaccounted queue. These mechanisms target redundant
processing and payload transfer while preserving the sample’s contents.
Appendix [E.4](#A5.SS4 "E.4 Visual Experience Delivery ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
describes the implemented reuse and transport paths, their
configuration, and the checks used to assess sample equivalence and
delivery cost.

##### Coordinate policy updates asynchronously.

In asynchronous mode, completed groups can supply the learner while
other workspaces remain active. To publish an update, the system pauses
new model calls, waits for calls already in progress, commits the new
weights, and resumes calls. Ongoing tasks keep their workspace and
completed work, so later decisions can use the updated policy.

The captured behavior version and generation probabilities identify the
policy that produced each response. A version change starts a new
segment, so each policy ratio uses the corresponding behavior
probabilities. For attempts spanning several versions, the clipped
objective is an off-policy approximation. Preserving the recorded inputs
keeps the sampled decisions identifiable, while policy lag affects the
resulting update.
Appendix [E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Task Groups and Terminal Feedback ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
defines policy lag and mixed-version attempts and distinguishes
asynchronous coordination from synchronous collection. This coordination
lets learning continue while ongoing tasks retain their environments and
completed work.

Together, these mechanisms address the cost of supplying visual
experience and coordinating updates, while the preceding preservation
rules keep learning tied to the executed decisions and complete task
outcomes.
Section [6.5](#S6.SS5 "6.5 Progress Across Training Stages ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
reports the Mid-to-SFT checkpoint changes.
Section [6.6](#S6.SS6 "6.6 Joint Desktop–Web Reinforcement Learning ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
then examines joint RL through reported checkpoint comparisons,
experience-delivery measurements, and a concrete example of
task-relative feedback.

## 6Experiments and Analysis

Our evaluation follows the progression from identifying a control to
completing a software task. We first compare grounding, desktop, and Web
performance, then examine how results change through long-horizon SFT
and joint desktop–Web RL. Trace analyses and selected cases connect
these scores to concrete decisions, while an engineering profile
measures the cost of delivering visual experience to the learner.
Finally, we study NorthCUA-Ultrafast to assess how direct action
selection changes the balance between task success and execution
latency.

### 6.1  Evaluation Setup

Computer use requires both finding the right control and maintaining the
user’s objective through successive state changes. We select the six
benchmarks in Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
to cover these complementary requirements. ScreenSpot-Pro tests dense
professional interfaces, while OSWorld-G adds functional and spatial
instructions. Desktop evaluation extends these decisions into
application workflows, and Web evaluation covers both user tasks and
defect detection.
Table [5](#S6.T5 "Table 5 ‣ 6.1 Evaluation Setup ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
summarizes what each benchmark measures.

[TABLE]

Table 5: The six-benchmark suite and the capabilities and metrics
covered by each evaluation.

##### Models and checkpoints.

We evaluate the NorthCUA family at three sizes, Nano (9B), Mini (35B),
and Pro (397B), built on Qwen3.5 ([Qwen Team, 2026a](#bib.bib60)). We
call the checkpoint after broad GUI mid-training *Mid* and the
checkpoint after verified long-horizon supervision *SFT*. Thus, Mid
already contains NorthCUA training. Mini and Pro use nominal 35B/3B and
397B/17B total/active language-model parameter counts, while Nano is
dense. We keep the publicly released Qwen3.5 checkpoints separate as
backbone-family references. The main grounding results use the
long-horizon SFT checkpoints, while the main desktop, WebArena, and
WebTestBench results use the final RL checkpoints. Ultrafast starts from
the pre-RL Nano model. Dashes indicate unreported metrics. WebTestBench
includes models with at least one measured metric. We report result
metrics to one decimal, computing differences before rounding.

##### Grounding protocol.

We evaluate SSP by direct inference on the full screenshot, without
iterative zoom or tool-assisted refinement, using all 1,581 examples.
OSWorld-G uses 564 Refined instructions and normalized 0–1000 click
coordinates. In addition to the main accuracy comparison, repeated
predictions characterize consistency and target coverage. NorthCUA’s
OSWorld-G entries use best-complete-run accuracy.
Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
details the sampling settings and reports three-run means and standard
deviations.

##### Interactive evaluation.

We use a maximum of 100 steps per task for OSWorld-Verified,
WebArena-Verified, and WebTestBench, and 500 steps for OSWorld 2.0.
OSWorld-Verified uses the officially supported 361-task setting,
excluding eight Google Drive tasks that require separate account
configuration.¹¹ 1
[https://timothyxxx.github.io/OSWorld/user_guides/run_public_evaluation.html](https://timothyxxx.github.io/OSWorld/user_guides/run_public_evaluation.html)
We evaluate OSWorld 2.0 on the full set of 108 tasks and retain binary
completion, requiring a normalized score of 1, and mean partial score
where available. We keep these measures separate because completing some
requirements can still leave a dependent workflow unfinished.
WebArena-Verified uses all 812 tasks and official outcome checks, with
screenshots and structured page references as the agent’s observations.
WebTestBench uses the golden-checklist setting, supplying the reference
test items directly to the agent. This isolates interactive defect
detection from checklist generation. We treat Fail as the positive
class. Precision, recall, and F1 are computed within each task and then
averaged separately across tasks, giving each task equal weight
regardless of its checklist length.

##### Comparison models.

We select representative GUI specialists and general-purpose models from
original papers and official model cards, including explicitly
attributed evaluations of other providers’ models. Source markers
distinguish our evaluations from published scores within each model
group. Our OSWorld-G evaluation includes nine general-purpose baselines.
Model-name citations identify original papers, reports, or official
model pages, while
Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
collects score sources and protocol details, including benchmark
versions and action interfaces ([Tong et al., 2026](#bib.bib74); [Zhou
et al., 2026a](#bib.bib109)).

### 6.2  GUI Grounding Results

NorthCUA achieves state-of-the-art grounding results among the systems
compared in
Table [6](#S6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ 6.2 GUI Grounding Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
with Pro reaching 93.3% on SSP and 91.0% on OSWorld-G. The two
benchmarks test complementary requirements, from locating small controls
in dense professional interfaces to following functional and spatial
instructions. We report the final long-horizon SFT checkpoints, using
direct SSP inference and the Refined OSWorld-G instructions described in
Section [6.1](#S6.SS1 "6.1 Evaluation Setup ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

Claude Opus 5.5 is the strongest reference on both benchmarks in this
comparison, scoring 88.1% on SSP and 89.9% on OSWorld-G in our
evaluation. Pro exceeds these scores by 5.2 and 1.1 percentage points,
respectively. On SSP, Pro also leads Qwen3.8-Max’s reported 84.5% by 8.8
points. On OSWorld-G, it exceeds Nex-N2.5-Pro’s reported 87.4% by 3.6
points.

The smaller tiers retain much of this capability. Mini reaches 92.4% on
SSP and 87.9% on OSWorld-G with 35B total and 3B active language-model
parameters. Its SSP score is within one percentage point of Pro. Nano
reaches 72.9% and 83.0% with 9B parameters, with SSP accuracy close to
GUI-Owl-1.5-32B-Instruct’s reported 72.9%. To understand what these
aggregate scores capture, we next separate geometric precision, control
semantics, and target availability.

##### Broad coverage and fine localization.

Mini and Pro both exceed 90% SSP accuracy in all six application groups.
Pro reaches 95.0% in CAD and 94.5% in scientific software. This broad
coverage also includes controls with little room for a coordinate error.
To measure that difficulty directly, we pair the three models’
predictions on all 1,581 examples and group targets by size, measured as
the smaller of the target box’s width and height in the original
screenshot
(Table [7](#S6.T7 "Table 7 ‣ Broad coverage and fine localization. ‣ 6.2 GUI Grounding Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
A target in the smallest group is less than 16 pixels wide or tall. On
these narrow targets, accuracy rises from Nano’s 53.4% to Mini’s 85.2%
and Pro’s 92.1%. Pro’s 6.8-point gain over Mini is larger than its
1.2-point gain on targets at least 32 pixels wide and tall. Its stronger
aggregate result therefore also extends to the smallest target regions
in this breakdown.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  Benchmark |  Target subset |  Items |  NorthCUA-Nano |  NorthCUA-Mini |  NorthCUA-Pro |
|  SSP |  $`<16`$ px |  88 |  53.4 |  85.2 |  92.1 |
|  |  $`16`$–$`<32`$ px |  1,079 |  72.4 |  92.1 |  92.4 |
|  |  $`\geq 32`$ px |  414 |  78.3 |  94.7 |  95.9 |
|  OSWorld-G |  Visible target |  510 |  84.0 |  88.6 |  91.7 |
|  |  Absent target |  54 |  67.9 |  74.1 |  75.9 |

Table 7: Trace-based grounding accuracy (%). SSP uses one prediction per
item. Target size is the smaller of the annotated box’s width and
height, measured in original-image pixels. OSWorld-G averages three
draws per item. Subgroup definitions and repeated-draw breakdowns appear
in
Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

##### Interpreting specialized controls.

A small target must also be matched to the user’s intended function. Pro
reaches 90.2% on SSP’s icon targets, followed by Mini at 88.6% and Nano
at 55.0%. The difference persists within the most populous size band of
16–$`<32`$ pixels, where Pro and Mini both locate 89.0% of the 418 icon
targets and Nano locates 53.1%. Their corresponding text-target scores
are closer at 94.6%, 94.1%, and 84.6%, respectively. Even after this
coarse size grouping, the gap between Nano and the larger models remains
greater for icon targets than for text targets.

This distinction helps explain why our data construction addresses both
target geometry and control meaning. The broad route retains the image
coordinates, while documentation supplies functional descriptions for
specialized controls.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/evaluation_grounding_cases.png)

Figure 26: Grounding a documented control. Left, an actual
documentation-grounded annotation pairs an Ample Bass P control with its
manual-defined articulation and a generated training query. Right,
NorthCUA localizes this control in an SSP screenshot with a different
surrounding GUI state, following the recorded query *legeto slide*. The
target’s local appearance and position match the construction view. In
the enlarged detail, the teal box marks the reference target and the
orange point is the recorded prediction. Enlargements and annotations
are reader aids. Documentation is used in data construction, not
supplied during direct evaluation.

Figure [26](#S6.F26 "Figure 26 ‣ Interpreting specialized controls. ‣ 6.2 GUI Grounding Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
illustrates this construction with the Legato Slide control in Ample
Bass P, a virtual instrument hosted in FL Studio. Its articulation
buttons encode different playing techniques through small symbols, so
the icon alone gives little indication of the requested function.

The construction record links this control to the manual’s Legato Slide
description ([Ample Sound, n.d.](#bib.bib2)) and generates the
instruction *Activate the Legato Slide articulation*, retaining the
original target location.

This documentation-grounded package was used in Nano and Mini training.
In the SSP example, NorthCUA correctly locates the documented control
from the full screenshot and query alone, without access to the manual
during inference.

##### Target availability and repeated predictions.

Reliable grounding also requires recognizing when a requested target is
absent. OSWorld-G tests this distinction alongside visible-target
localization. Across three draws, Pro averages 91.7% on the 510
visible-target items, compared with Mini’s 88.6%. Its correct-refusal
rate reaches 75.9% on the 54 absent-target items, compared with Mini’s
74.1%
(Table [7](#S6.T7 "Table 7 ‣ Broad coverage and fine localization. ‣ 6.2 GUI Grounding Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
The larger gain is therefore in locating visible controls, while
recognizing when to decline a location request remains harder across all
three tiers. This distinction matters when a predicted coordinate
becomes an agent’s next action.

Repeated sampling then reveals whether correct predictions persist
across draws. Pro is correct in all three draws on 489 of 564 items,
compared with 469 for Mini and 445 for Nano. Its pass@3 reaches 94.0%,
covering 530 items, while its three complete runs range from 89.4% to
91.0%. Pro also succeeds at least once on 36 of Mini’s 53 persistently
missed items, including 26 that Pro gets right in every draw. Thus, its
stronger aggregate result accompanies both more stable successes and
coverage of additional difficult items.

### 6.3  Desktop Agent Results

Grounding accuracy is useful only if the policy can sustain correct
decisions until the requested software state is reached.
Tables [8](#S6.T8 "Table 8 ‣ 6.3 Desktop Agent Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
and [9](#S6.T9 "Table 9 ‣ 6.3 Desktop Agent Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
therefore compare complete application workflows and longer dependent
tasks. NorthCUA-Pro reaches 88.6% on OSWorld-Verified, the highest score
among the compared systems. Mini reaches 85.3% with 35B total
parameters, while the 9B Nano reaches 78.9%. These final RL results
extend the grounding comparison from selecting individual controls to
completing software tasks.

Pro’s score is 2.3 points above the joint-highest reference score of
71.6%, reported for Kimi-K3 and obtained by GPT-6 Astra in our
evaluation. Mini reaches 70.4% with 35B parameters, exceeding
Qwen3.8-Max’s reported 66.8% and Nex-N2.5-Pro’s 67.6%. Nano’s 67.2%
provides a 9B alternative.

##### Coverage across websites.

Figure [27](#S6.F27 "Figure 27 ‣ Coverage across websites. ‣ 6.4 Web Agent Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
examines trajectories across six website groups, using Nano and Mini
after SFT and Pro after RL. Pro exceeds 70% success in each of the five
single-site groups, reaching 84.0% on forums and 83.0% in store
administration. This coverage spans consumer-facing websites and
operational interfaces with editable records.

Cross-site tasks remain more demanding. Pro completes 17 of 48 tasks
(35.4%), compared with 583 of 764 single-site tasks (76.3%). These
workflows require carrying information or requirements between websites,
making them a useful test of the sustained execution targeted by our
long-horizon experience pipeline. The gap identifies cross-site
coordination as a continuing challenge despite broad single-site
coverage.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/evaluation_web_breakdown.png)

Figure 27: Web success by website scope. The six groups partition all
812 WebArena-Verified tasks using retained attempts. Nano and Mini use
SFT checkpoints, while Pro uses RL. Pro exceeds 70% in every single-site
group and completes 17 of 48 cross-site tasks.
Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives the selection and group details.

A complementary breakdown by requested outcome shows that Pro completes
274 of 374 state-changing tasks (73.3%), alongside 245 retrieval tasks
and 81 navigation tasks. Thus, the result includes workflows that must
leave a correct persistent site state, beyond locating or reading a
page. Mini completes 253 state-changing and 223 retrieval tasks,
compared with Nano’s 240 and 204. Its additional completions are
concentrated in these two categories, while navigation counts are
similar across the two models.
Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives the group counts and the decision-length distributions for all
three checkpoints.

##### Execution length and local setbacks.

The Pro export contains 14,604 model decisions across its 812 retained
attempts. Successful attempts have a median of ten decisions. Of these,
24 contain at least 50 decisions, and the longest reaches 93. This range
shows completion across both short interactions and extended workflows
within the same benchmark.

For Pro, unsuccessful attempts have a median of 15.5 decisions and
contribute 38.4% of the exported decisions despite accounting for 26.1%
of tasks. Mini shows a similar concentration of work in failed tasks,
whose median is 18 decisions compared with 10 for successes. Its 260
failures account for 50.6% of the 15,224 recorded decisions. This
difference between successful and unsuccessful execution motivates our
emphasis on feedback-guided correction during long workflows. Counts
include terminal responses, with the record boundaries defined in
Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

Section [6.7](#S6.SS7 "6.7 An Evaluated Browser Recovery Case ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
illustrates such a correction in a Pro trajectory. The agent uses
interface feedback to recover from an unavailable option while
preserving the user’s date constraint.

##### Web testing.

Completing a user request and detecting defects require different
judgments. We therefore evaluate defect detection on WebTestBench with
the gold checklist supplied to each agent ([Kong et al.,
2026](#bib.bib36)). Given these test items, the agent must exercise the
application and distinguish genuine defects from correctly implemented
behavior.
Table [11](#S6.T11 "Table 11 ‣ Web testing. ‣ 6.4 Web Agent Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
reports task-averaged precision, recall, and F1. NorthCUA-Pro achieves
the highest F1 at 60.3%, exceeding the strongest comparison model, GPT-6
Astra at 58.2%, by 2.1 percentage points. At 35B, NorthCUA-Mini reaches
54.4% F1, improving on the same-size Nex-N2.5-mini by 5.8 points. Its
precision and recall both increase by 6.3 points in this comparison,
indicating gains in both the correctness of reported defects and the
fraction of defects found.

The separate metrics clarify these detection profiles. NorthCUA-Pro also
achieves the highest precision at 67.8%, alongside 63.7% recall. GPT-6
Astra combines the highest recall of 74.6% with 55.1% precision. GPT-5.6
Sol shows a similar emphasis on recall, at 70.3% against 54.8%
precision, with an F1 of 56.1%. These differences distinguish finding a
larger fraction of defects from making more accurate defect reports. We
compute F1 separately for each task and report the mean of those
per-task scores. The development case in
Figure [18](#S4.F18 "Figure 18 ‣ Case study of a responsive homepage repair. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
separate from the scored WebTestBench evaluation, illustrates how such
findings can guide a test–repair–retest cycle.
Appendix [F](#A6 "Appendix F Web Repair and Testing Protocol ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
describes the browser-evidence qualification protocol.

### 6.5  Progress Across Training Stages

The final-checkpoint comparisons summarize benchmark performance. We
next examine how the reported scores develop through training, beginning
with the transition from broad mid-training to verified long-horizon SFT
(Table [12](#S6.T12 "Table 12 ‣ 6.5 Progress Across Training Stages ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
Desktop success increases across all three tiers, by 7.4 points for
Nano, 15.0 for Mini, and 10.0 for Pro. Pro reaches 85.9% after this
transition, while Mini shows the largest absolute desktop gain.

The Web results show a different distribution of gains. Nano improves by
9.2 points and Mini by 2.6, narrowing their gap from 10.5 to 3.8 points.
Pro improves from 69.1% to 69.6%, a smaller gain of 0.5 points. The
largest gains therefore depend on both model size and task domain. Mini
gains most on desktop tasks, while Nano gains most on Web tasks. These
checkpoint comparisons measure the complete training transition,
including data selection and additional optimization.
Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
records the available evaluation settings and aggregation rules for the
stage comparisons.

The longer OSWorld 2.0 tasks expose the gap between partial progress and
complete workflows at the Mid stage. Across the three tiers, partial
scores range from 24.2% to 30.6%, while binary completion ranges from
0.9% to 9.3%
(Table [12](#S6.T12 "Table 12 ‣ 6.5 Progress Across Training Stages ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
Long-horizon SFT increases partial scores by 16.7, 20.7, and 33.0 points
for Nano, Mini, and Pro, respectively. Binary completion also increases
in every tier, with Pro rising from 9.3% to 27.3%. The gains therefore
include more fully completed tasks as well as higher earned credit.
These SFT checkpoints provide the starting points for the joint-RL
comparison in
Table [13](#S6.T13 "Table 13 ‣ Task completion across platforms. ‣ 6.6 Joint Desktop–Web Reinforcement Learning ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
allowing the two training transitions to be examined separately.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/osworld2_sft_case.png)

Figure 28: Source-paper selection before and after long-horizon SFT. A

dataset revisions and OCR settings.

##### From similar questions to verified source papers.

Figure [28](#S6.F28 "Figure 28 ‣ 6.5 Progress Across Training Stages ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
compares the same OSWorld 2.0 request in two NorthCUA-Mini runs before
and after long-horizon SFT, focusing on how the agent selects and
verifies source documents. A student has collected five question images
and asks the agent to find their source exam papers. Similar statistical
topics recur across sessions, so recognizing the topic alone can lead to
a convincing but incorrect match. The third question, shown at the top
of the figure, asks for an exponential-model test with specific null and
alternative parameters.

The earlier run reads the question images and searches the candidate
papers. It treats related exponential-test wording as confirmation and
assigns Q3 to May 2025. Its final folder contains all five named PDFs,
but the complete attempt receives 10% partial credit. The later run
matches Q3’s specific test to December 2020 and checks that the copied
file agrees with its source. This more discriminating search also
appears elsewhere in the task. For Q5, it rejects a 15-mark candidate
that differs from the requested 22-mark question, continues into Trash,
and recovers the matching original paper. The resulting attempt receives
full credit after 124 decisions, compared with 243 in the earlier run.

The contrast concerns what counts as completion. The earlier run
assembles all five named PDFs while accepting an incorrect source match.
The later run carries the question-specific criteria through search,
rejection and final file checking. These observed behaviors illustrate
the sustained verification targeted by long-horizon training.
Appendix [J](#A10 "Appendix J OSWorld 2.0 Execution Cases ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives the execution settings.

### 6.6  Joint Desktop–Web Reinforcement Learning

Long-horizon SFT teaches the policy to follow verified demonstrations.
RL adds feedback on the states reached by the policy’s own actions,
allowing successful and unsuccessful attempts at the same task to inform
the next update. We examine this transition across desktop and Web
execution under the shared training interface in
Section [5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
The prepared RL task pool contains approximately 1,500 desktop tasks and
500 Web tasks. Each model starts from its long-horizon SFT checkpoint
and learns from fresh attempts on these tasks.

##### Task completion across platforms.

Table [13](#S6.T13 "Table 13 ‣ Task completion across platforms. ‣ 6.6 Joint Desktop–Web Reinforcement Learning ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
compares each long-horizon SFT checkpoint (Before RL) with its
subsequent joint-training checkpoint (After RL). Within each tier, the
task set, tools, observation settings, and execution budget stay fixed,
allowing the comparison to track performance through the transition from
supervised learning to joint RL. OSWorld 2.0 retains Binary and Partial
separately to distinguish complete workflows from progress on individual
requirements.

After joint RL, reported success increases on both platforms at all
three model sizes. OSWorld-Verified success rises by 2.9 points for
Nano, 2.0 for Mini, and 2.8 for Pro. The corresponding WebArena-Verified
gains are 3.0, 2.5, and 4.3 points. Pro reaches 88.6% and 73.9% on the
two benchmarks, respectively. The desktop gains are similar across
tiers, whereas Pro has the largest reported Web increase.

OSWorld 2.0 distinguishes gains in earned credit from gains in full-task
completion. Partial scores increase by 4.7, 3.8, and 3.2 points for
Nano, Mini, and Pro, respectively. Nano and Pro also improve binary
completion by 0.9 and 3.3 points, with Pro reaching 30.6% binary
completion and 66.8% partial credit. Mini’s binary score stays at 18.5%
as its partial score rises from 46.3% to 50.1%. Together, the two
metrics show where the final checkpoints improve requirement
satisfaction and where that improvement also appears in complete
workflows.

The remaining analyses address two different parts of the learning
process. We first measure how the runtime delivers long visual records
without changing their contents, then examine how task rewards
distinguish two superficially similar interaction paths.

|  |  After RL |  78.9 |  13.0 |  45.6 |  67.2 |

Table 13: Before- and after-RL results on three interactive benchmarks
(%). OSWorld-Verified uses 361 tasks, OSWorld 2.0 uses 108 tasks, and
WebArena-Verified uses 812 tasks. Scores retain their reported
aggregation rules
(Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).

##### Delivering long visual experience efficiently.

The learning interface preserves the screenshots seen at each decision.
As a result, long attempts can contribute repeated image windows across
training segments. We use a separate archived 9B desktop run for the
engineering profile and trajectory example below. To evaluate the
lossless delivery path in
Section [5.3.4](#S5.SS3.SSS4 "5.3.4 Continuous Rollout and Policy Updates ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
we examine 28 batches. Pairing the raw and encoded counters for each
batch holds the underlying image tensors fixed while measuring their
representation size.

Figure 29: Desktop visual-experience delivery profiling. The profile
uses a separate desktop workload. (a) Encoded image-tensor bytes as a
percentage of raw bytes, calculated from the summed paired counters
across 28 batches. The dashed reference is the original representation
size. (b) Median and interquartile range of component timings for the
same update range. Policy synchronization includes the in-flight wait,
so the displayed timings are not additive. Payload sizes exclude
non-image fields and transport overhead.

Across this workload, encoding reduces the summed image-tensor payload
by 97.2%, leaving 2.8% of the original representation size. The median
raw and encoded batch sizes are 110.9 and 2.7 GiB, respectively.
Encoding preserves all screenshots and their values. The counters cover
eligible image tensors, including repeated segment occurrences, and
exclude non-image fields and transport overhead.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/rl_reward_case.png)

Figure 30: Executed trajectories with contrasting task rewards. Both
Thunderbird attempts reach Tags view. A hides the entire header, while B
disables Get Messages specifically. Native screenshot crops show
executed click positions in the middle panels and the state before
application closure on the right. A’s first two panels crop one
observation. B opens the header menu between those panels. The archived
configuration verifier returns rewards 0 and 1. Both attempts belong to
one eight-member GRPO group containing five successes and three
failures, which determines their advantage signs. They span an
asynchronous policy update. This is a within-group reward comparison,
not a before/after checkpoint evaluation.

Figure [29](#S6.F29 "Figure 29 ‣ Delivering long visual experience efficiently. ‣ 6.6 Joint Desktop–Web Reinforcement Learning ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")(b)
reports delivery and policy-synchronization timings. Image encoding
takes a median of 21.0 seconds, while object-store put calls take 1.9
seconds. The recorded policy-sync lifecycle takes 87.7 seconds and
includes the 5.0-second in-flight call wait, weight export,
rollout-engine reload, and receipt work. The in-flight wait is nested
within the synchronization lifecycle, so these component timings are
non-additive. They characterize delivery and synchronization costs,
while the payload comparison measures the reduction in representation
size for the same visual experience.

##### From interface choices to learning feedback.

Figure [30](#S6.F30 "Figure 30 ‣ Delivering long visual experience efficiently. ‣ 6.6 Joint Desktop–Web Reinforcement Learning ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
examines what the terminal reward distinguishes in an actual task. The
user asks the agent to switch Thunderbird to Tags view, hide the Get
Messages button, and close the application to save the settings. This
requires selecting the intended control and preserving its configuration
after the application exits.

The two attempts follow the same first five actions and reach Tags view.
Their next choices differ in scope. Attempt A disables the entire Folder
Pane Header, removing all its controls from view. Attempt B opens the
header’s own menu and unchecks Show Get Messages, leaving the remaining
header controls visible. Both then close Thunderbird and report
completion. The task verifier checks the saved Tags mode and the
specific button’s hidden setting, returning reward 0 for A and reward 1
for B. Thus, the reward distinguishes the saved button configuration
from hiding its entire parent header.

The complete group contains five successful and three unsuccessful
attempts. Under the group-relative rule in
Eq. [3](#A5.E3 "In E.2 Task Groups and Terminal Feedback ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
the successful attempts receive positive advantages and the unsuccessful
attempts receive negative advantages, although their task reward is
zero. These advantages carry the verifier’s distinction between saved
configurations into the policy update. The recorded attempts also cross
a policy-publication boundary. The learning interface in
Section [5.3.3](#S5.SS3.SSS3 "5.3.3 Preserving Executed Decisions for Learning ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
preserves each segment’s input and behavior version while keeping its
attempt reward and group identity. Checkpoint-level improvement is
evaluated separately in
Table [13](#S6.T13 "Table 13 ‣ Task completion across platforms. ‣ 6.6 Joint Desktop–Web Reinforcement Learning ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

### 6.7  An Evaluated Browser Recovery Case

Aggregate scores summarize completion, but a recorded interaction can
show how the policy handles a mismatch between its plan and the
interface. In the Pro episode in
Figure [31](#S6.F31 "Figure 31 ‣ 6.7 An Evaluated Browser Recovery Case ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
NorthCUA is asked to identify the best-selling brand in the first
quarter of 2022. Reaching the answer requires navigating to the
appropriate report, applying the correct time interval, and reading the
returned product rows.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/evaluation_web_case.png)

Figure 31: Recovering from an unavailable browser action. In a retained
Pro evaluation attempt, NorthCUA first attempts to select an unavailable
Quarter period. It inspects the dropdown, switches to Month with the
exact Q1 date range, and reads the resulting table before answering
Sprite. The saved evaluator marks the task successful. Numbered panels
show observations before decisions 6, 10, and 12 of a 13-decision
attempt, with intermediate actions omitted. Detail views enlarge
original control crops, with field labels arranged for readability. The
answer at decision 13 and the external evaluator’s success marker are
annotations outside the webpage.

The first attempted period selection requests an unavailable Quarter
option and times out. To identify the mismatch, the agent opens the
dropdown. At decision 6, it observes that only Day, Month, and Year are
available. It then uses Month for aggregation and enters January 1 and
March 31 as the date bounds, preserving the requested quarter through
the two date fields. By decision 10, the visible form contains the
intended interval and is ready to run.

After running the report, the agent scrolls through its rows. The state
at decision 12 shows two Sprite products among the highest-quantity
entries. The agent checks the remaining rows before returning Sprite,
and the saved task evaluator records success. The episode contains 13
model decisions, including the terminal response. Using the date fields
with the available Month aggregation mode lets the agent correct its
execution plan while preserving the requested quarter.

### 6.8  NorthCUA-Ultrafast

Long computer-use tasks accumulate the latency of many successive model
decisions. Some decisions require open-ended content, but others reduce
to choosing an operation and a control already present on the page.
Predicting these finite choices directly could remove the decoding cost
of routine actions. Jev provides a relevant interface for this purpose
by answering structured choice questions without generating their
answers token by token ([Almeida, 2026](#bib.bib1)). We adapt this idea
to NorthCUA-Nano through NorthCUA-Ultrafast, which combines a choice
head for finite decisions with the original model’s generation path for
open-ended outputs.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/26_web_choice.png)

Figure 32: Choice-based Web execution. The input shows the title before
editing. Generated GUI views illustrate filling the new title (right)
and a subsequent Save click (left). At each decision, current controls
support operation and target selection. A direct choice executes without
decoding, while a generation route triggers a second full-model call
with the original prompt and a fresh cache. Optional LoRA adapters are
active only for classification. Route and target checks are omitted.

Figure [32](#S6.F32 "Figure 32 ‣ 6.8 NorthCUA-Ultrafast ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
illustrates the resulting division of work. The classifier receives the
task and recorded interaction history, including page observations,
screenshots, and action overlays. Candidate controls are bound to the
latest valid page observation for execution. One backbone forward pass
supplies the features used to score the operation and its applicable
targets. A selected click, hover, or checkbox change can then be
converted directly into a tool call.

Filling a field additionally requires text, so it triggers a new call
using the original model prompt and generation head. Other tool actions
and final responses use the same generation path. The executor validates
the returned tool and arguments, and uncertain finite choices can fall
back to native generation. This preserves the original output interface
while allowing routine finite actions to bypass decoding.
Appendix [E.7.4](#A5.SS7.SSS4 "E.7.4 Online Task Completion and Timing ‣ E.7 NorthCUA-Ultrafast Adaptation and Evaluation ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
specifies the routing and validation profiles.

We study how much adaptation this choice interface requires, starting
from the long-horizon-SFT NorthCUA-Nano checkpoint before joint RL.
Following Valen’s head-training approach ([Liuziyu77,
2026](#bib.bib40)), *Head-only* freezes all original weights and trains
a 4.21M-parameter choice head, about 0.045% of the complete multimodal
checkpoint. *Head + LoRA* also trains low-rank updates in the language
layers ([Hu et al., 2022](#bib.bib32)), bringing the trainable total to
47.49M parameters. These adapters are enabled for classification and
disabled for native generation, which keeps the original generator
common to both variants. Both use about 226,000 training decision
states, with fixed validation and test sets of 5,203 and 6,087 states.
Supervision covers the recorded operation and its required target.
Appendix [E.7](#A5.SS7 "E.7 NorthCUA-Ultrafast Adaptation and Evaluation ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives the readout, training objective, and recipe.

We first evaluate whether the choice interface retains the recorded
decisions. Head-only and Head + LoRA reach validation route-and-target
accuracies of 86.1% and 88.2%, respectively. On the held-out test set,
Head-only reaches 85.4% route-and-target accuracy against Full
generation’s 85.0%, while their full-action exact-match scores are 64.0%
and 64.6%. Generated parameters account for much of the remaining
full-action error. The LoRA result is a validation comparison.
Appendix [E.7](#A5.SS7 "E.7 NorthCUA-Ultrafast Adaptation and Evaluation ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives the separate metrics and checkpoint-selection details.

To assess complete task execution, we compare the three policies on all
812 WebArena-Verified tasks, deploying each inference replica on a
single GPU.
Table [14](#S6.T14 "Table 14 ‣ 6.8 NorthCUA-Ultrafast ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
reports success and measured execution cost under the definitions in
Appendix [E.7.4](#A5.SS7.SSS4 "E.7.4 Online Task Completion and Timing ‣ E.7 NorthCUA-Ultrafast Adaptation and Evaluation ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
Success uses the full benchmark, while timing and step counts use a
fixed 320-task subset selected from tasks jointly successful in the
timing snapshot. Decoding bypass measures the fraction of dispatched
actions produced directly by the choice head.

|                          |           |             |         |            |
|--------------------------|-----------|-------------|---------|------------|
|  Method                  |  Task     |  Mean task  |  Mean   |  Decoding  |
|                          |  success  |  time       |  steps  |  bypass    |
|                          |  (%)      |  (s)        |         |  (%)       |
|  Full generation         |  64.2     |  616.8      |  9.5    |  0.0       |
|  Ultrafast, Head-only    |  59.2     |  288.8      |  10.2   |  40.2      |
|  Ultrafast, Head + LoRA  |  60.6     |  266.8      |  10.6   |  31.1      |

Table 14: Online quality and execution cost of NorthCUA-Ultrafast.
Success uses all 812 tasks. Mean task time and steps use the fixed
320-task success-selected subset. Decoding bypass uses dispatched
actions.
Appendix [E.7.4](#A5.SS7.SSS4 "E.7.4 Online Task Completion and Timing ‣ E.7 NorthCUA-Ultrafast Adaptation and Evaluation ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
defines the populations and deployment settings.

Head-only completes 481 tasks using only the choice head for adaptation.
Head + LoRA completes 492 (60.6%), gaining 11 tasks or 1.4 percentage
points. With about 0.5% trainable parameters, LoRA retains 94.4% of Full
generation’s 64.2% success rate, leaving a gap of 3.6 percentage points.

The direct-action path is used frequently during execution. Head-only
and Head + LoRA bypass generation for 40.2% and 31.1% of recorded
actions, respectively. We next examine the task-level cost of these
execution policies on the fixed success-selected subset.

Mean task time falls from 616.8 s to 288.8 s for Head-only and 266.8 s
for Head + LoRA, despite slightly longer trajectories of 10.2 and 10.6
steps versus 9.45. On the fixed timing subset, this is a
factor-of-$`2.3`$ reduction in mean task time for Head + LoRA. Its
shorter task time despite a lower bypass rate also shows why
direct-action frequency alone is insufficient to characterize execution
efficiency. Each replica uses one GPU.
Appendix [E.7.4](#A5.SS7.SSS4 "E.7.4 Online Task Completion and Timing ‣ E.7 NorthCUA-Ultrafast Adaptation and Evaluation ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives the decoding and serving settings for these measured execution
costs. The following case illustrates how fast selection and flexible
generation cooperate within one successful task.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/ultrafast_online_case.png)

Figure 33: Ultrafast product-update case. Selected Head + LoRA decisions
from one WebArena-Verified attempt. Lightning and text-entry icons
distinguish direct choice from native generation. Numbers retain the
original decision indices, with intermediate steps omitted. Insets
enlarge recorded details, with post-action states labeled.

##### From search recovery to a saved product update.

Figure [33](#S6.F33 "Figure 33 ‣ 6.8 NorthCUA-Ultrafast ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
follows a Head + LoRA attempt at a WebArena-Verified task that asks the
agent to disable Karmen yoga pants because of quality issues. The
request requires finding the correct catalog entry and saving its
disabled state. Searching for the full phrase returns no records. The
native generator responds to this observation by shortening the query to
*Karmen*, after which the search exposes the configurable *Karmen Yoga
Pant* and its individual variants.

The choice path opens the configurable product, switches *Enable
Product* off, and selects *Save*, all without decoding action text. The
final page shows the saved disabled state. The independent evaluator
verifies the corresponding product-1819 save request and awards full
credit.

Across the complete attempt, six of twelve executed browser actions
bypass generation. The other actions handle navigation, query text, and
waiting, and a thirteenth decision generates the final response. This
execution illustrates the intended division of work. Generation remains
available when the search needs revision, while discrete selections
carry the recovered task through to a verified update. The aggregate
bypass rates in
Table [14](#S6.T14 "Table 14 ‣ 6.8 NorthCUA-Ultrafast ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
measure how often this direct execution path is used across the
benchmark.

## 7Related Work

NorthCUA connects computer-use learning with the tasks and execution
systems that supply its experience. We review these foundations in three
parts. We first discuss policies and learning methods, then the
construction of tasks and experience, and finally the harnesses and
infrastructure that support their collection and use in training.

##### Computer-use policies and learning.

Accurate GUI actions depend on linking instructions to screen elements.
SeeClick studies grounding pre-training ([Cheng et al.,
2024](#bib.bib13)). GUI-Actor develops attention-based pointing, while
GUI-Cursor refines cursor movements through visual feedback ([Wu et al.,
2025](#bib.bib85); [Zhao et al., 2026](#bib.bib106)). End-to-end
policies build on this capability to coordinate actions over time.
UI-TARS integrates perception, reasoning, and action prediction, and
OpenCUA develops reflective supervision from human demonstrations ([Qin
et al., 2025](#bib.bib59); [Wang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib83)). Learning
from outcomes offers a further source of improvement. ComputerRL scales
live-desktop RL ([Lai et al., 2026](#bib.bib37)), while VEM learns
offline value estimates to guide environment-free policy exploration
([Chen et al., 2026b](#bib.bib12)). UI-TARS-2 and Qwen-CUA connect
staged training with interactive learning ([Wang et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib80); [Lu et al., 2026](#bib.bib41)). For long
trajectories, learning methods also specify how history and outcome
feedback are carried across training segments. ScaleCUA masks reused
history and shares rollout rewards and advantages across segments ([Lv
et al., 2026](#bib.bib43)). Qwen-CUA preserves episode credit across
slices and normalizes each trajectory’s objective over active policy
tokens ([Lu et al., 2026](#bib.bib41)). NorthCUA combines broad GUI
mid-training, verified long-horizon SFT, and joint desktop–web RL. Our
emphasis is on integrating decision-time input preservation and
complete-attempt weighting with the Harness and experience-production
pipeline.

##### Scalable tasks and experience.

Scaling workflow learning creates a corresponding demand for executable
tasks and reliable outcomes. OS-Genesis derives tasks from exploratory
interaction ([Sun et al., 2025](#bib.bib73)), while GUI-360 combines
sourced requests, environment templates, execution, and filtering for
Windows data collection ([Mu et al., 2025](#bib.bib49)). ProCUA-SFT
grounds tasks in real materials and checks preconditions before rollout
([Jung et al., 2026](#bib.bib34)). For outcome-driven learning, CUA-Gym
and Qwen-UI-Agent synthesize compatible tasks, initial states, and
verifiers ([Wang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib78); [Zhou et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib110)). WEFT extends this whole-system view to tool-use
post-training, using execution feedback to revise interacting components
and fresh rollouts to evaluate each revision ([Mao et al.,
2026](#bib.bib44)). NorthCUA organizes construction around available
software and content, sourced requests, or verified seed tasks. Each
route produces an executable CaseSpec . A separate grounding route uses
application documentation to identify control functions and construct
screenshot–query–location supervision.

Hard tasks also motivate retaining procedural knowledge across
executions. Work2Skill turns synchronized human GUI demonstrations into
reusable skills through on-demand inspection of action, visual, and
available accessibility evidence ([Huang et al., 2026](#bib.bib33)).
WebXSkill extracts executable procedures with language guidance from
trajectories ([Wang et al., 2026c](#bib.bib84)). Search complements
procedural guidance by considering alternative decisions. Computer-Using
World Model simulates UI transitions for action selection ([Guan et al.,
2026](#bib.bib28)), and Everything of Thoughts combines learned guidance
with tree search for reasoning ([Ding et al., 2024](#bib.bib17)). Our
RSI collection retains validated textual skills and real workspace
checkpoints, so later attempts can reuse procedures or resume partial
progress before full-task verification.

##### Agent harnesses and learning infrastructure.

Such collection and learning require an execution layer that preserves
both software state and training evidence. UFO introduces hierarchical
Windows interaction ([Zhang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib103)). UFO2 adds
application-specialized agents and GUI–API integration ([Zhang et al.,
2026](#bib.bib105)), and UFO3 extends orchestration to heterogeneous
devices through a task DAG ([Zhang et al., 2025d](#bib.bib104)). For
reuse across training systems, Agent Lightning v1.0 separates harness
execution from RL optimization ([He et al., 2026](#bib.bib29)). Orchard
provides shared sandbox lifecycle services ([Peng et al.,
2026](#bib.bib57)), while Polar reconstructs token-faithful training
trajectories from recorded model calls ([Xu et al., 2026a](#bib.bib92)).
NorthCUA focuses on integrating these system responsibilities for
stateful visual interaction. Its shared desktop/web runtime, live
outcome inspection, and parallel lifecycle service support experience
collection. The Experience Adapter then preserves the recorded decisions
when converting online attempts into learning samples, connecting
execution and assessment to policy training through the same task and
outcome interfaces.

## 8Limitations and Responsible Use

##### Generalization and coverage.

NorthCUA operates across diverse applications, while unfamiliar software
and changing interface conventions remain challenging.
Documentation-guided grounding expands the functional knowledge
available during data construction, and skill- and milestone-guided
collection revisit workflows that ordinary attempts leave unfinished.
Extending this coverage to new software and maintaining reliable
execution as applications evolve remain important directions for the
experience pipeline.

##### Feedback and optimization.

Executable verifiers and live-workspace inspection tie learning feedback
to the work actually produced. Feedback quality depends on predicate
coverage and the calibration of model judgments for open-ended content
([Zheng et al., 2023](#bib.bib107)). Controlled verifier probes and
evidence-linked judgments help check both. Long workflows also
concentrate many decisions into a terminal outcome. More informative
intermediate feedback could improve credit assignment and help agents
reach correct results with fewer unnecessary actions.

##### Responsible deployment.

Isolated workspaces, explicit tool profiles, and retained execution
records provide controls for deployment. Operating real software also
requires authorized access, least-privilege credentials, and
confirmation for consequential actions. External pages and documents can
carry indirect prompt injections ([Debenedetti et al.,
2024](#bib.bib14)), motivating adversarial testing appropriate to each
application. Released materials should undergo licensing, privacy, and
credential review alongside the operational checks.

## 9Conclusion

We presented NorthCUA, an integrated system for developing computer-use
agents through experience at scale. By connecting the NorthCUA Harness,
Experience Flywheel, and Staged Learning through shared tasks and
outcome checks, the system supports learning from both verified
demonstrations and fresh policy interaction. Improved policies can
return to the same infrastructure to collect further experience.
Recursive self-improvement extends collection to difficult workflows by
preserving useful procedures and verified milestones, allowing later
attempts to build on earlier progress.

Across six benchmarks, the Nano, Mini, and Pro family achieves
state-of-the-art results among open-source models in GUI grounding,
desktop execution, and Web interaction. At 397B parameters, Pro
outperforms the trillion-parameter general-purpose baselines on both
grounding benchmarks, OSWorld-Verified, and WebArena-Verified. Stage
comparisons further show improvements in desktop and Web execution
across all three model sizes following long-horizon SFT and joint RL.
Alongside these gains in task completion, NorthCUA-Ultrafast explores a
complementary route to faster decision-making.

We release the three models, the full Harness code, and this technical
report so that the work can be extended at both the policy and system
levels. Together, they provide an open foundation for adapting agents to
new software and developing better ways to collect and learn from
experience. The broader goal is to make reliable software execution a
general AI capability, allowing users to delegate complete workflows
from intent to verifiable outcome.
````
