````math
## Appendix A Implementation and Reproducibility

The executable task, its workspace, and the resulting learning samples
share an attempt identity. CaseSpec binds the request to its materials,
initial state, and execution settings. The environment service manages
the workspace, the NorthCUA Harness records interaction, and the
configured verifier or evaluator establishes the outcome. The Experience
Adapter retains the policy’s decision-time inputs when constructing
samples, and the learner associates those samples with their
attempt-level feedback.

### A.1  Information and Permission Boundaries

Construction, execution, evaluation, and learning use different
information. The generator can inspect construction metadata, while the
acting policy receives the request and permitted task inputs. Host-only
acceptance criteria remain with evaluation. Tool capability checks
control permitted operations, while input construction and sample
extraction separately control which information each role receives.

Actor and evaluator records remain separate, preserving the actor’s
submitted result and decision-time inputs during inspection. Actor
imitation excludes privileged evaluation calls. Evaluator demonstrations
used in mid-training are separate judgment tasks with their own
inspection context
(Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Supervised Representation and Sampling ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
During ordinary execution, the actor receives the task request and
permitted inputs, while construction dependency graphs remain
construction metadata. Runtime profiles, live-inspection rules, and
resource ownership are detailed in
Appendices [H.1](#A8.SS1 "H.1 Runtime Configuration ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
[H.1](#A8.SS1.SSS0.Px2 "Evaluator profiles and attribution. ‣ H.1 Runtime Configuration ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
and [H.3](#A8.SS3 "H.3 Batch Collection and Resource Ownership ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

## Appendix B Grounding Data Details

Section [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Single-Step UI Grounding ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
describes visual, structural, and documentation-grounded labeling. The
common annotation unit links an unannotated screenshot, a visible
control, its target geometry, and one or more queries. Documentation
supplies functional evidence, while the GUI image supplies the target
location.

|  |  |
|----|----|
|  Record |  Contents and alignment rule |
|  GUI target |  Original image dimensions, target point or region, and any crop/resize transform. Predictions and labels refer to the same coordinate system. |
|  Documentation match |  Application and available version evidence, manual source, candidate illustrations and function descriptions, and the context supporting the selected match. |
|  Query variants |  Function-, name-, appearance-, or position-based queries linked to the same visible control. Variants remain grouped with their source target. |
|  Candidate disposition |  Accepted, rejected, or unresolved matches, keeping semantic ambiguity separate from geometric invalidity. |

Table 15: Annotation records for tracing a grounding label from source
evidence to the original screenshot.

Crop and resize transforms preserve correspondence with the original
screen. A point target lies inside the actionable region, without an
occluding foreground element. A menu-item target requires the item to be
visible in the captured menu state. Queries requiring unprovided hidden
state are excluded. Diagnostic boxes and action annotations are kept
separate from the unannotated grounding input.

Documentation matching checks the control’s appearance, nearby controls,
visible application state, and the manual’s applicability. Ambiguous or
version-incompatible matches remain outside the accepted set. Label
checks distinguish a wrong function from a spatial miss, even when both
refer to similar-looking controls. We count source images, target
controls, and query variants separately, grouping paraphrases with their
original target. Source grouping and split rules are shared with
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

## Appendix C Task Construction Details

The construction routes in
Section [4](#S4 "4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
bind a request, usable materials, an initial workspace, and acceptance
conditions into an executable task. A material record combines file
format, bounded content summaries, and provenance. After selection, the
workspace builder copies the inputs and records their content hashes and
initialization procedure. The task distinguishes read-only inputs,
distractors, mutable targets, and required outputs.

For dependent work, the plan connects producers to the artifacts or
services consumed by later stages. Structural checks reject unknown
predecessors, cycles, disconnected stages, incompatible references, and
required outputs without production stages. Host-only evaluation
artifacts remain with the evaluator. These constraints define valid
dependencies while leaving the actor free to choose GUI actions within
the task’s prerequisites. The following details cover environment
preparation, assisted collection, and verifier test states.

### C.1  Environment Construction and Binding

The request-conditioned desktop builder first filters candidate
materials by format and application requirements, then ranks them by
content. Request refinement uses the selected inputs to make references
concrete while preserving the source goal and constraints. A mismatch
between the request and available materials sends the task back to
construction. Content summaries support selection without replacing
inspection of the files being packaged.

EnvAgent finalizes the workspace against a current report of file
evidence and query alignment. Material changes require renewed
inspection before finalization. The compiler retains packaged paths,
checksums, environment configuration, and startup actions, with
ownership and permissions that make the initialized files accessible to
the desktop user. The specified live-validation procedure additionally
checks GUI opening and required operations in an isolated attempt.
Collection begins from a fresh initialization, preserving the specified
starting state for the actor.

Dependency checks distinguish data flow from required workflow order.
For example, the office plan in
Figure [14](#S4.F14 "Figure 14 ‣ Desktop long-horizon tasks. ‣ 4.2.1 Scaling Intents ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
uses content from A and B in its final PDF, while the record update in C
has an independent acceptance condition. The C-to-D edge imposes order
without making the record a source of PDF content. A designated terminal
artifact therefore need not be the task’s only required result.

Reference-based web construction specifies both the visible interface
and the state supporting its interactions. Its environment description
records the reference source, supported pages and operations, observed
versus generated content, backend schema and seed state, reset
procedure, and external dependencies. Rendering, navigation,
persistence, initialization, and task feasibility are checked separately
against this description. It defines the reconstructed pages and
behaviors used for task collection.
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
summarizes the resulting data sources and scale.

### C.2  Hard-Task Collection Protocol

The two collection routes in
Section [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Recursive Self-Improvement for Hard-Task Collection ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
retain different products between attempts: validated operating guidance
and recoverable execution progress. Selection starts in the training
partition after ordinary attempts expose a useful obstacle. Invalid
environments, ambiguous requirements, and unreliable verifiers are
repaired or excluded before further exploration. The task’s final
acceptance conditions remain fixed as candidate skills and intermediate
milestones evolve.

A skill record identifies its task family, applicability, guidance,
version, source attempts, validation cases, and known failures.
Validation uses a frozen candidate, separate from the attempts that
produced it. Task-specific assistance, reuse within a family, and
transfer to a new application remain distinct uses of the record.

For milestone search, a planning agent may inspect the request and
verifier to define dependent subtask–check pairs. Actor-visible guidance
uses only permitted task information. Hidden predicate constants and
expected answers stay in evaluation context. A checkpoint binds the
environment snapshot to its parent attempt and action prefix,
prompt-visible history, summary, notes, skill and milestone guidance,
model/tool configuration, and remaining budget. Restoration
re-establishes device bindings and obtains fresh observations. External
service state needs its own isolation and restoration mechanism.

A candidate actor state is retained before state-changing inspection.
Such inspection uses an evaluation copy or separate branch and receives
its own action record. Actor continuations resume from the retained
pre-inspection state. The checkpoint record retains enough ancestry to
reconstruct the selected path and the information available at each
decision.

Search configuration specifies candidate counts, state selection,
diversity and retention limits, uncertainty handling, and stopping
rules. Its budget includes failed attempts, skill validation,
snapshot/branch operations, inspection, and live-resource costs. A
selected recoverable failure retains its unresolved milestone label
until its continuation repairs that requirement. Local checks guide
expansion, while the original task verifier determines full-task
acceptance.

Training extraction follows the selected actor path. Search-controller
decisions, snapshot operations, and failed sibling branches are excluded
from actor targets. Supplied skills and milestone guidance remain in the
context that conditioned those targets. Cross-branch knowledge transfers
are recorded at their point of use. Deleting this guidance would change
the decision inputs, so an unguided demonstration requires recollection.

A complete assisted path, a checkpoint-conditioned continuation, and a
verified subtask from an incomplete attempt are different sample types.
Unassisted completion is evaluated in a fresh-start run using the
original task inputs. Their counting units and source-family separation
follow
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

### C.3  Controlled Verifier States

Verifier tests hold the intended obligations fixed while varying the
submitted state. An incomplete initial state is a negative control, a
correct result is a positive control, a near miss isolates one
violation, and a valid alternative tests sensitivity to presentation.
Each test retains the initial and final state, protected inputs,
acceptance conditions, and the expected verdict. Testing score
composition with stipulated predicate values is separate from executing
the predicates on actual artifacts.

Figure [34](#A3.F34 "Figure 34 ‣ C.3 Controlled Verifier States ‣ Appendix C Task Construction Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives a concrete regional-report example. Expected gross sales are North
420 and South 380, totaling 800. Acceptance also requires complete
regional content, template compliance, and unchanged protected source
files. The two accepted presentations satisfy all of those conditions,
including the regional content and protected-file requirements.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/10_verification.png)

Figure 34: Four states of the same report task. Missing output and
missing regional detail should be rejected. A complete table and an
equivalent chart should be accepted. Both accepted examples are
stipulated to satisfy the full contract, including regional discussion,
template compliance, and unchanged source files. The GUI excerpts and
inspection asset are illustrative. The dashed red region annotates
missing content. Accept/Reject labels show the expected verdicts for
these illustrative states.

## Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale

The dataset records distinguish source materials, constructed tasks,
execution attempts, accepted trajectories, extracted samples, and
training consumption. A task can produce several attempts, and a
trajectory can produce several prediction units. Grounding images,
target controls, and query variants use their own units.
Table [16](#A4.T16 "Table 16 ‣ Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
summarizes the supervised data by stage and domain using rounded counts.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  Source |  Queries |  Steps |  Mean steps/query |
| Broad GUI mid-training |  |  |  |
|  Desktop |  $`\approx 100{,}000`$ |  $`\approx 4{,}200{,}000`$ |  $`\approx 40`$ |
|  Web |  $`\approx 10{,}000`$ |  $`\approx 280{,}000`$ |  $`\approx 30`$ |
|  Grounding |  $`\approx 40{,}000`$ |  $`\approx 40{,}000`$ |  $`1`$ |
| Verified long-horizon SFT |  |  |  |
|  Desktop |  $`\approx 2{,}000`$ |  $`\approx 240{,}000`$ |  $`\approx 120`$ |
|  Web |  $`\approx 1{,}000`$ |  $`\approx 70{,}000`$ |  $`\approx 70`$ |

Table 16: Approximate supervised data scale. Counts are rounded
independently. SFT domain step totals and the web mean are estimated
from the rounded query counts, total steps, and desktop mean. Grounding
steps denote coordinate targets and are separate from trajectory
interactions.

Together, the desktop and web sources provide over 100,000 trajectories
and over 4 million GUI interactions for mid-training. The long-horizon
SFT cohort contains approximately 3,000 queries and 310,000 interaction
steps.

These rounded counts summarize source cohorts by stage and domain before
model-specific sampling and repeated consumption. Auxiliary evaluator
demonstrations are accounted for separately from task-execution
trajectories.

A sample’s lineage connects source and task family, environment and
fixture identity, collection policy, permitted tools, outcome check, and
extraction version. A consumed training sample additionally identifies
its training shard and stage. Actor episodes and evaluator judgment
episodes retain separate roles and permissions. Complete assisted paths,
checkpoint continuations, and verified subtask demonstrations also
retain distinct sample types.

Related screenshots, documents, material sets, task seeds, source sites,
repository/issue families, and derived skills are grouped before
splitting. Source-policy disclosure, licensing, and access permissions
apply to each supervised source.

## Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details

The Nano, Mini, and Pro tiers use the Qwen3.5-9B, Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, and
Qwen3.5-397B-A17B backbone variants, respectively ([Qwen Team,
2026a](#bib.bib60)).
Table [17](#A5.T17 "Table 17 ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
lists the backbone identities and their language-model parameter counts.
The mixture-of-experts variants have distinct total and activated
counts.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  Field |  NorthCUA-Nano |  NorthCUA-Mini |  NorthCUA-Pro |
|  Backbone variant |  Qwen3.5-9B |      Qwen3.5-  35B-A3B |      Qwen3.5-  397B-A17B |
|  Language-model parameters |  9B, dense |      35B total  3B activated |      397B total  17B activated |

Table 17: Model-family identities and language-model parameter counts
following Qwen3.5 ([Qwen Team, 2026a](#bib.bib60)).

The results use *Mid* for broad GUI mid-training and *SFT* for verified
long-horizon supervision. Mid is already a NorthCUA checkpoint, distinct
from an upstream pre-trained release. The supervised stages differ in
their source mixture and selection criteria. We first describe the
supervised prediction units, then explain how task-group feedback and
recorded RL attempts become learning samples.

### E.1  Supervised Representation and Sampling

The supervised stages share response prediction but use different
sources and selection rules. An extraction map $`\Phi`$ converts a
grounding example, task trajectory, or evaluator episode into prediction
units $`x\sim\mathcal{D}`$. A unit can be a decision or a
context-bounded slice. For a binary target mask $`m_{t}`$ with
$`\sum_{t}m_{t}>0`$, the conditional objective is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\rm sup}=-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\frac{1}{\sum_{t}m_{t}}\sum_{t}m_{t}\log p_{\theta}(x_{t}\mid x_{<t})\right].
``` |  | (2) |

Here $`x_{<t}`$ includes the recorded multimodal input at that decision.
The expectation is over extracted units after source sampling, so an
episode’s weight depends jointly on extraction, sampling, and the
within-unit mean.

Grounding examples target coordinates. Actor trajectories target
recorded assistant responses, including reasoning, text, and tool calls
according to the target mask. Evaluator episodes are separate judgment
tasks targeting inspection and assessment responses from their own
permitted context. Grounding, actor trajectories, and evaluator examples
enter mid-training. Long-horizon SFT uses task trajectories. The three
sources have separate counts and sampling weights.

An actor input contains the request and the observations, tool results,
summaries, notes, and guidance available at that decision.
Post-execution judgments and hidden acceptance values belong to the
evaluation context. A post-action screenshot or action overlay is
available only to later decisions. Actor-issued note calls remain in the
response stream with the mask specified by the recipe.
Construction-agent and privileged evaluator calls are outside actor
targets.

Sampling determines how source and episode lengths influence learning.
Uniform decision sampling, uniform trajectory sampling, and
token-weighted sampling give different weights to long episodes. The
recipe therefore distinguishes extraction, source/domain weighting,
packing, target deduplication, and loss reduction. A response repeated
as context has one target occurrence. Assisted traces retain the skill
or milestone guidance that conditioned them. These supervised choices
are specified separately from online RL assembly.

Selection precedes sampling. Broad trajectories come from judge-selected
completed tasks. Long-horizon trajectories require original full-task
script acceptance followed by execution-quality and dependency checks.
Skill- and milestone-assisted trajectories undergo the same checks.
Cleaning rejects unusable records. Retained episodes preserve
action–observation alignment, including coherent recoveries. Source
attribution and cohort overlap follow
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

### E.2  Task Groups and Terminal Feedback

The initial RL pool follows
Section [5.3.1](#S5.SS3.SSS1 "5.3.1 Selecting Tasks with Outcome Variation ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"):
human-reviewed desktop and web tasks are screened with at least five
attempts under each model’s SFT checkpoint and fixed execution settings.
Selection uses variation among valid verifier scores, with environment
and verifier errors recorded separately from task outcomes. Fresh
grouped attempts on the selected pool provide the learning data.

Members of a group attempt the same case package from equivalent initial
states under the same verifier and budget. A task-script result can be a
finite numeric reward or an interpretable Boolean/status verdict, with
Boolean fallback mapped to zero or one. Reward ranges and preprocessing
remain task-specific. Script errors, timeouts, nonzero exits, and
uninterpretable results follow the invalid-evaluation path. Valid
negative verdicts remain learning outcomes.

Retries retain logical member identity under the configured retry
policy. Complete-group checks prevent missing members from silently
changing the comparison. Domain sampling weights specify scheduled work,
while completed, admitted, and consumed members describe successive
stages of actual use. The logical attempt remains the unit of group
membership, independently of infrastructure retries or the number of
extracted training segments.

For $`G>1`$ valid members, terminal advantages are

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A_{i}=\frac{R_{i}-\bar{R}}{s_{R}+\epsilon},\qquad\bar{R}=\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i=1}^{G}R_{i},\qquad s_{R}^{2}=\frac{1}{G-1}\sum_{i=1}^{G}(R_{i}-\bar{R})^{2},
``` |  | (3) |

where $`R_{i}`$ is the attempt’s terminal reward and
$`\epsilon=10^{-6}`$. The baseline is computed within the task group,
independently of other tasks or domains. Identical rewards produce zero
terminal advantages. Each attempt contributes one advantage shared by
its segments. The trajectory-mean configuration uses the full attempt’s
target-token denominator in
Eq. [1](#S5.E1 "In Preserve the complete attempt’s learning weight. ‣ 5.3.3 Preserving Executed Decisions for Learning ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

Synchronous and asynchronous execution differ in when work can overlap
policy updates. For asynchronous runs, policy lag is the learner version
at consumption minus the behavior version of each call. Lag and the
fraction of mixed-version attempts describe the executed configuration.

### E.3  Clipped Policy Objective

Let $`b(s)`$ denote segment $`s`$’s captured behavior version, and let
$`h_{ist}`$ contain its multimodal context and generated prefix before
token $`x_{ist}`$. The learner uses the PPO-style clipped surrogate
([Schulman et al., 2017](#bib.bib69)) with the member advantage from
Eq. [3](#A5.E3 "In E.2 Task Groups and Terminal Feedback ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"):

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\rho_{ist}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\exp\!\left(\log\pi_{\theta}(x_{ist}\mid h_{ist})-\log\pi_{b(s)}(x_{ist}\mid h_{ist})\right),`$ |  | (4) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\ell_{ist}^{\rm clip}`$ | $`\displaystyle=-\min\{\rho_{ist}A_{i},\operatorname{clip}(\rho_{ist},1-\delta_{-},1+\delta_{+})A_{i}\}.`$ |  |  |

The likelihoods use the temperature and recorded-support convention in
Appendix [E.6](#A5.SS6 "E.6 Sampling Likelihood Convention ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
The parameters $`\delta_{-}`$ and $`\delta_{+}`$ define the lower and
upper clipping widths, respectively. In the trajectory-mean
configuration,
Eq. [1](#S5.E1 "In Preserve the complete attempt’s learning weight. ‣ 5.3.3 Preserving Executed Decisions for Learning ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
aggregates token losses within each complete attempt before averaging
attempts.

A behavior version is constant within a segment, but an asynchronous
attempt can span several versions. Captured likelihoods identify the
denominator for each response. For attempts spanning policy versions,
the clipped update is an off-policy approximation under policy lag and
changing state distributions. Terminal feedback assigns one
attempt-level advantage across the episode. Local process rewards and
distillation are separate extensions to this terminal-reward recipe.

### E.4  Visual Experience Delivery

The delivery mechanisms in
Section [5.3.4](#S5.SS3.SSS4 "5.3.4 Continuous Rollout and Policy Updates ‣ 5.3 Joint Desktop–Web Agentic Reinforcement Learning ‣ 5Staged Learning from Scalable GUI Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
address repeated CPU image preparation and visual-tensor transfer. They
preserve the learner’s inputs, unlike semantic compaction, which changes
those inputs. Lossless tensor encoding reduces transfer volume. CPU
preprocessing reuse is a separate mechanism whose enablement depends on
processor compatibility.

The preprocessing-reuse path supports the Hugging Face Qwen2-VL
processor’s independent per-image outputs. Within a member, it retains
pixel_values and image_grid_thw under fixed processor settings,
reconstructs the requested image order, and processes new content. A
clean-to-overlay replacement or a configuration change requires fresh
processing. Unsupported processors or incompatible output structures use
ordinary processing. The cache stores CPU tensors, while neural visual
encoding remains part of the model forward pass.

Owned per-image storage avoids retaining an obsolete batch through a
view. The member’s window is cleared on exit, and state publication
remains scoped to that member during cancellation. Tensors already
staged for prompt projection can be reused during training
materialization under the same content and processor identity.

The selected transport path uses Zstandard level 1 on contiguous CPU
image tensor bytes after data-parallel partitioning. An encoding
replaces the original only when smaller. A failed encoding leaves the
original available. The consumer checks the expected byte count, decodes
its selected microbatch, and transfers tensors to the accelerator
without retaining a decoded full-batch cache. Dtype, shape, resolution,
and floating-point values remain unchanged.

Payload accounting includes delivery-pending groups as well as reserved,
active, and ready work. Admission limits are expressed as numbers of
groups. Successful compact object-store transfer allows the producer to
release visual references, while consumption records keep sample
identities without those payloads. The data-parallel delivery barrier
remains in place.

Equivalence checks compare prompt tokens, image order, preprocessing
tensors, dtype, shape, masks, rewards, and behavior likelihoods on the
same captured calls. Full-update comparison additionally fixes model,
optimizer, random state, and partition schedule. Component measurements
cover preprocessing, codec, transfer, and memory costs. The complete
optimizer interval is a separate measurement boundary.

### E.5  Sequence Integrity and Update Boundaries

Before assembling an actor RL segment, the adapter checks the
delivered-response receipt, policy/tokenizer/template/processor
identities, call order, and response digests. It admits main-agent calls
only. Terminal Controller evidence confirms delivery by linking ordered
execution steps and their artifact digests to the captured responses.
Task success is established separately by the outcome check.

Calls can be appended when prompt tokens and ordered media bindings
confirm a prefix extension under the same behavior version. Media
identity includes a content digest. Replacing a clean frame with an
action overlay, evicting an image, rewriting history, or changing
behavior version therefore starts a new segment from the newly captured
input. Responses repeated as history provide context and are masked out
of the new segment’s targets. Their original target occurrences remain
available for learning. Processor-specific visual-position projection
preserves captured image content and order.

Segments from the same logical attempt share its identity and reward
provenance. The adapter checks group completeness before computing
advantages. In the trajectory-mean configuration, the learner sums
target token counts over the full attempt before partitioning. Every
segment uses this denominator within the same optimizer step. Partition
invariance holds when conditioning, masks, and token losses stay fixed.

Weight publication pauses new model calls, drains calls already in
progress, commits the new weights, and resumes calls with the new
behavior version. The tasks themselves can remain active in their
workspaces. After abnormal recovery, execution restarts from committed
checkpoint and cursor state, rebuilding warm queues and unfinished
rollouts from that committed state.

### E.6  Sampling Likelihood Convention

The policy ratio requires compatible learner and behavior likelihoods.
For temperature $`T>0`$ and the recorded behavior support $`S_{b}(h)`$,
the likelihood convention is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
p_{\theta}^{(T,S_{b})}(x\mid h)=\frac{\mathbf{1}\{x\in S_{b}(h)\}\exp(z_{\theta}(x\mid h)/T)}{\sum_{u\in S_{b}(h)}\exp(z_{\theta}(u\mid h)/T)}.
``` |  |

Here $`h`$ contains the captured context and generated prefix, and
$`z_{\theta}`$ denotes the learner’s logits. With top-p equal to one and
no other vocabulary restriction, $`S_{b}(h)`$ is the full vocabulary.
For nucleus sampling ([Holtzman et al., 2020](#bib.bib31)), replay uses
the recorded token identities and offsets at each target position rather
than constructing a new nucleus from current learner logits.
Temperature, support capture, and replay settings belong to the run
configuration. Entropy regularization has a separately configured
support mask.

### E.7  NorthCUA-Ultrafast Adaptation and Evaluation

This section details the choice-based execution mode introduced in
Section [6.8](#S6.SS8 "6.8 NorthCUA-Ultrafast ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
Its training unit is a decision state extracted from a WebAgent
trajectory. The original causal prefix supplies the input, and the
following recorded action supplies the label. The classifier and native
generator use this same underlying evidence through different prompts.

#### E.7.1  Multimodal Inputs and Action Routing

The classification prompt retains the task, preceding actions, tool
results, screenshots, and action overlays. It replaces the original
system instruction with a finite-choice instruction and appends the
current operation and target candidates. Native generation instead uses
the original messages, tool schemas, and chat template, keeping the
original action prompt separate from classification-specific
instructions and selections.

Targets come from the latest valid set-of-mark observation. We exclude
explicitly disabled elements and restrict filling to input-like roles
and checkbox changes to checkbox roles. At most 150 elements are
retained per observation, with the number omitted recorded. A finite
operation is offered when its tool and eligible targets are available.
Selection operates over this retained candidate set.
Table [18](#A5.T18 "Table 18 ‣ E.7.1 Multimodal Inputs and Action Routing ‣ E.7 NorthCUA-Ultrafast Adaptation and Evaluation ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
gives the seven routes. The operation and each available target type
form separate variable-size choice questions. Only the chosen
operation’s target is used for execution.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  Route |  Target choice |  Completion |
|  CLICK |  Element |  Construct a click call directly. |
|  HOVER |  Element |  Construct a hover call directly. |
|  CHECK |  Checkbox |  Construct a check call directly. |
|  UNCHECK |  Checkbox |  Construct an uncheck call directly. |
|  FILL |  Input field |  Generate the original fill action and require its field to agree with the selected target. |
|  NATIVE |  None |  Generate a complete offered tool call and its arguments. |
|  RESPOND |  None |  Generate the original structured final response. |

Table 18: Ultrafast routing and completion. Current references are
resolved through the latest observation. Completion describes strict
routing. The original runner performs tool execution and checks the
final response format.

In the offline full-action evaluation below, generation starts with a
fresh input encoding and cache, with any LoRA adapters disabled.
Decoding is greedy with thinking disabled and at most 4,096 new tokens.
A valid result requires model termination and the relevant tool schema
or response structure. Strict routing rejects FILL target disagreements.
NATIVE accepts a valid offered tool, including a finite-action tool if
the original generator selects one. This matters when comparing route
labels with complete actions below.

#### E.7.2  Readout and Training

The last hidden layer supplies four vectors for each candidate. These
are the mean context representation before the appended choice block,
the mean question-instruction representation, the mean candidate
representation, and the final decision-marker representation. Each
vector has dimension 4,096. A shared head normalizes and projects these
roles to width 512, adds learned role embeddings, and applies two Mixer
blocks. Each block mixes the four roles through a
$`4\!\rightarrow\!16\!\rightarrow\!4`$ MLP and the channels through a
$`512\!\rightarrow\!1024\!\rightarrow\!512`$ MLP. A scalar readout from
the updated candidate role supplies its score. Softmax is applied within
each question, and argmax selects its answer. The Mixer operates within
each candidate’s four roles, while the backbone has already encoded the
full candidate list.

For state $`i`$, let $`Q_{i}`$ contain the supervised operation question
and, when required, the corresponding target question. With correct
candidate $`y_{iq}`$, score $`z_{iqk}`$, and candidate set $`C_{iq}`$,
the objective for a minibatch of decision states $`\mathcal{B}`$ is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{choice}}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}}\frac{1}{|Q_{i}|}\sum_{q\in Q_{i}}-\log\frac{\exp z_{iqy_{iq}}}{\sum_{k\in C_{iq}}\exp z_{iqk}}.
``` |  | (5) |

Averaging within each state keeps states with an operation and target
equally weighted with operation-only states. Other target questions
receive no label, and the original output tokens receive no generation
loss.

The dataset contains 225,710 training, 5,203 validation, and 6,087 test
states from 25 trajectory collections. Normalized task goals define
groups, and the fixed holdouts remain unchanged when training sources
are expanded. Filtering removes stale or missing targets, conflicting
labels, invalid actions, and known target-tool errors. The native
multimodal prefixes are rejoined to their source records before
training. Both input paths are checked against a 73,728-token budget
after image-token expansion, without truncation. Grouping removes
identified request duplicates. Semantically related paraphrases and task
templates may still occur across groups.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  Setting |  Head-only |  Head + LoRA |
|  Trainable parameters |  4.214M (0.0448%) |  47.492M (0.5047%) |
|  Choice head |  Width 512, two blocks |  Same |
|  LoRA |  None |  Rank 16, scale 2, dropout 0 |
|  Adapter scope |  None |  All 248 language-layer linear modules |
|  Optimizer |  AdamW |  AdamW |
|  Learning rate |  $`10^{-4}`$, constant |  Same |
|  Global batch |  128 states |  Same |
|  Precision |  BF16 backbone, FP32 head |  BF16 backbone, FP32 head/adapters |
|  Gradient clipping |  Global norm 1.0 |  Same |
|  Seed |  42 |  42 |
|  Completed updates |  63,504 (36 epochs) |  24,521 (13 epochs plus part of 14) |
|  Selected checkpoint |  Epoch 22 |  Epoch 1 |

Table 19: Ultrafast adaptation settings. Original model weights remain
frozen in both variants. Parameter percentages use the complete
9.410B-parameter multimodal checkpoint as the denominator.

AdamW uses $`\beta=(0.9,0.999)`$, $`\epsilon=10^{-8}`$, and weight decay
0.01, with no learning-rate warmup or schedule. Candidates retain
deterministic ID order, without shuffling or class reweighting. Head +
LoRA adds 43.278M adapter parameters to the same 4.214M head. The
adapters remain unmerged so that generation can disable them. The two
variants share data, logical batch size, and seed, but use different
completed budgets. Checkpoints are selected by full-validation joint
accuracy, then loss and earlier epoch.

#### E.7.3  Offline Decision and Action Results

Route-and-target joint accuracy requires all labeled questions for a
state to be correct. It excludes generated text and other open-ended
parameters. Full-action exact matching instead compares the tool name
and complete arguments, or the final response object, with the reference
action. It normalizes reference IDs to strings, then compares tool
names, parameter values, and response text exactly. A NATIVE route can
still generate the correct finite-action tool call, so full-action
matching can credit a decision whose route label differs from the
reference.

|                   |                    |              |                     |
|-------------------|--------------------|--------------|---------------------|
|  Policy           |  Validation joint  |  Test joint  |  Test action exact  |
|  Full generation  |  –                 |  85.0        |  64.6               |
|  Head-only        |  86.1              |  85.4        |  64.0               |
|  Head + LoRA      |  88.2              |  –           |  –                  |

Table 20: Offline Ultrafast results in percent. Validation and test
contain 5,203 and 6,087 states. Original generated actions are projected
into the same route labels. Missing results are left as dashes.

Head-only correctly selects the route and target in 5,196 test states,
compared with 5,172 for projected full generation. Complete-action
matching is lower for the hybrid policy, at 3,897 versus 3,933 states.
In the paired comparison, 3,757 states match under both policies, 140
only under Head-only, and 176 only under full generation.

The hybrid gains 46 exact matches among 2,576 CLICK states, while losing
25 among 906 FILL states and 30 among 2,087 NATIVE states. Losses on
generation-dependent FILL and NATIVE actions exceed the gains on CLICK.

The diagnosis is clearest among the 1,403 states with a correct
Head-only route but an incorrect hybrid action. Of these, 935 have
mismatched parameters for the same tool, 359 have a tool/final-output
kind mismatch, 18 differ in final JSON fields, and 91 are rejected by
route or target consistency checks. These are reference-matching
outcomes, distinct from environment success. The paired test reuses one
saved native generation per state for both policies, including states
whose hybrid action bypasses generation. Its execution time covers this
shared-generation offline procedure.

Validation selects epoch 1 for Head + LoRA and epoch 22 for Head-only.
At the same completed epoch 13, their joint accuracies are 86.9% and
85.5%, respectively. This provides an equal-epoch comparison, alongside
the selected checkpoints in
Table [20](#A5.T20 "Table 20 ‣ E.7.3 Offline Decision and Action Results ‣ E.7 NorthCUA-Ultrafast Adaptation and Evaluation ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
All results come from one training seed, and states can share a task.
Earlier text-only experiments use a different input protocol and are
excluded from this multimodal comparison.

#### E.7.4  Online Task Completion and Timing

The online comparison adapts the long-horizon-SFT NorthCUA-Nano
checkpoint before joint RL and retains the original WebAgent runner.
Each inference replica occupies one GPU. Full generation sends every
decision through the native generation path. Both Ultrafast variants and
the case in
Figure [33](#S6.F33 "Figure 33 ‣ 6.8 NorthCUA-Ultrafast ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
use the generation-preferred profile with a finite-choice confidence
threshold of 0.8. A direct action requires both its operation and target
probabilities to meet this threshold. Lower-confidence selections,
missing or stale current references, and unsupported observation
surfaces defer to native generation. When generation is used, its
complete valid action is accepted without replacing its arguments with
the classifier’s selection. The gateway records any disagreement. This
differs from the strict agreement checks used in the offline comparison
above.

##### Task outcomes and matched execution records.

The evaluator assigns success to 521, 481, and 492 of the 812 tasks for
Full generation, Head-only, and Head + LoRA, respectively. Failures,
timeouts, and evaluation errors remain in the denominator. The latest
Head-only and LoRA scores come from rescoring existing trajectories.
Their efficiency statistics retain the same execution records.

Task time and trajectory length use a fixed matched subset of 320 tasks,
selected from 343 tasks jointly successful in the timing snapshot. The
remaining 23 lack complete timing or action-dispatch evidence for at
least one policy. This fixed set supports comparisons on the same tasks
and defines the population for the conditional means. Rescoring updates
the success totals while leaving this timing population unchanged.

##### Step and action boundaries.

A step is one decision round, including a final response. Classification
followed by generation or a length retry remains one step. An action
counts a tool call dispatched to its handler, including calls that
subsequently fail during execution. A step may issue multiple actions or
none, and a final response is not a tool action. Only the final selected
attempt is counted. The 320-task set contains 3,025, 3,260, and 3,386
steps, respectively, yielding the means in
Table [14](#S6.T14 "Table 14 ‣ 6.8 NorthCUA-Ultrafast ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
Its dispatched-action totals are 2,683, 2,940, and 3,066, with means of
8.4, 9.2, and 9.6 actions.

Decoding bypass counts recorded dispatched actions produced directly by
the choice head without invoking native generation. The fractions are
6,182/15,385 for Head-only and 4,248/13,652 for Head + LoRA, or 40.2%
and 31.1%. These denominators cover available actions from successful
and failed tasks, separately from the 320-task timing set. Incomplete
action records affect 12 and eight tasks, respectively. Missing actions
are excluded from both counts. Full generation has zero bypass by
definition.

##### Measured time and deployment.

Task time spans the agent execution loop through terminal observation
and result persistence. It includes decisions, browser operations, and
observations, while excluding environment provisioning, browser
teardown, and evaluator scoring. The measured mean for Full generation
is 616.8 s on the matched task set.

Full generation uses temperature 0.7, top-$`p`$ 0.95, top-$`k`$ 40, high
reasoning effort, and up to 16,384 output tokens. The hybrid runs
combine temperature-zero attempts with temperature-one, top-$`p`$-one
length retries. Each replica occupies one GPU, with run-specific replica
counts, concurrency, and browser placement. The reported task times
characterize these evaluation deployments.

## Appendix F Web Repair and Testing Protocol

### F.1  Qualifying Browser Evidence

The main-text case in
Figure [18](#S4.F18 "Figure 18 ‣ Case study of a responsive homepage repair. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
records browser testing and code repair during local-page development.
To decide whether an observation justifies a product change, the
proposed evaluation protocol first checks that the relevant test actions
completed. A mismatch may otherwise originate in the test specification,
the agent’s actions, or the environment ([Yang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib96); [Wan et al., 2026](#bib.bib77)).

Figure [35](#A6.F35 "Figure 35 ‣ F.1 Qualifying Browser Evidence ‣ Appendix F Web Repair and Testing Protocol ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
uses a title-persistence requirement to make this decision concrete.
Record R17 should retain *Delivery plan v2* after reload. The Web Agent
completes the edit and save, observes the new title, then reloads the
record and finds the old title. Together, these observations support a
candidate persistence defect. The report pairs the expected and observed
values with the completed actions, tested build, reset state, and
supporting artifacts, giving the Coding Agent a specific discrepancy to
investigate.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/06_webloop.png)

Figure 35: A persistence example for evidence qualification. The new
title appears after saving but reverts after a completed reload. The
report preserves this mismatch for repair. An interrupted save or reload
requires diagnosis and retesting before assigning a product defect.
Final acceptance uses fixed checks in a fresh state outside the repair
loop. GUI states and inspection materials illustrate the proposed
protocol.

The same old title has a different interpretation if Save could not be
activated or reload did not complete. In that case, the test has not
established that the product lost a saved value. The proposed
adjudication rule therefore sends unresolved execution failures to
diagnosis or bounded retesting. Test steps may be repaired and
exploratory checks added while the issue’s acceptance goals and
regression requirements stay fixed.

Repair feedback and final acceptance also serve different purposes.
Feedback helps the agents revise a candidate. Final acceptance evaluates
the submitted patch from a protected clean state, using checks the
repair agents cannot change. Its verdict is withheld from further
revision, so acceptance tests whether the fix survives beyond the
session used to develop it.

Appendix [I.4](#A9.SS4 "I.4 Web Repair Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
details the common assignments, resets, and denominators needed to
compare feedback conditions.

These controls support the proposed comparison of browser-feedback
conditions ([Wang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib82)). Inclusion of an episode
in model training follows the separate source and consumption accounting
in
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Training Data Sources and Scale ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

## Appendix G NorthCUA Case Studies

The following cases trace complex software tasks from an initial
workspace or source records to editable deliverables and native task
outcomes. They illustrate sustained GUI interaction across geometry,
data analysis, visual composition, circuit simulation, and gameplay. The
GeoGebra, office, and TIDAL workflows use NorthCUA’s desktop MCP tools.
The Virtuoso workflow uses native CUA Driver MCP tools in a prepared
engineering environment. All use an external agent controller. The Zuma
case extends this setting to screenshot-and-mouse gameplay. These
demonstrations exercise the tool interface and are separate from the
NorthCUA checkpoint evaluations in
Section [6](#S6 "6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
The recorded step counts include GUI actions, observations, preparation,
checks, and retries. The release packages preserve the full traces,
infrastructure repairs, native outputs, and acceptance records.

### G.1  Ruby Heart

The task is to turn supplied equations into a GeoGebra heart that can
pulse, change depth and color, and reveal a moving cross-section. The
difficulty is keeping the cutting plane and section linked to the
changing surface.
Figure [36](#A7.F36 "Figure 36 ‣ G.1 Ruby Heart ‣ Appendix G NorthCUA Case Studies ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
begins with an empty workspace. The agent enters native expressions to
build a first construction, then arranges the geometry and four controls
into an interactive exhibit. Construction view reveals the auxiliary
geometry linked to the surface for inspection.

The agent then exercises these dependencies through playback and direct
manipulation. Holding pulse phase and slice position fixed, it checks
two depth settings that visibly change the surface and its section. It
also reopens the saved file and later drags the slice to another
position. The trace covers 18 combinations of depth, slice position, and
pulse phase, connecting the visible shape to its underlying parameters.
The 440-step record ends with the final saved .ggb and three separate
view images. The editable construction supports continued interaction
with the geometry established during modeling.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/nexcua_case_ruby_heart.png)

Figure 36: NorthCUA case study in GeoGebra. The trajectory connects
expression entry to geometry, playback, and parameter checks. The first
construction is enlarged from state 79. Panel 6 compares depths 0.20 and
0.65 at fixed pulse phase 0 and slice position $`-0.8`$. The section
detail enlarges state 429. Numbers identify captured states in row
order. Editorial overlays summarize nearby actions, with intervening
actions omitted.

### G.2  Silicon Atlas

This task asks for five editable plots from synthetic chip-test records.
The challenge is preserving measurement meaning across spreadsheet
analysis and plotting. Recorded failures take precedence. Otherwise,
incomplete tests remain UNKNOWN, while missing current measurements stay
missing. In
Figure [37](#A7.F37 "Figure 37 ‣ G.2 Silicon Atlas ‣ Appendix G NorthCUA Case Studies ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
the agent imports 2,142 sweep rows into Calc and fills formulas that
classify 1,071 operating groups. A passing group requires two clean
expected repeats, and energy is computed only for passing points. These
rules govern the subsequent plots.

The classified table first feeds a Shmoo map of voltage and frequency in
LabPlot. Matching axes on a second temperature map make the operating
regions directly comparable. The agent next plots the maximum tested
passing frequencies and passing-point energy. A separate 32-device
sample supplies the final count plot, completing the atlas. The agent
then reopens the workbook to check counts and units, repairs crowded
labels, and exports the revised figure. The audit retains 573 passing,
489 failing, and nine unknown groups. After 928 recorded steps, the
workbook and plotting project preserve the calculations and editable
plots alongside the PDF and PNG outputs.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/nexcua_case_silicon_atlas.png)

Figure 37: NorthCUA case study in Calc and LabPlot. Repeat-aware
classification supplies the temperature maps and derived plots. Focused
crops distinguish temperature comparison, passing-frequency maxima, and
32-device counts before the complete atlas. The count inset transcribes
the audit totals at state 877. Numbers identify captured states, and
editorial overlays summarize nearby actions. Intervening actions are
omitted. The data describe a synthetic demonstration.

### G.3  NOVA Presentation

The NOVA task asks for a six-slide presentation from a product brief,
supplied images, and numerical data. The challenge is preserving
accurate, editable scientific content while building a coherent visual
story.
Figure [38](#A7.F38 "Figure 38 ‣ G.3 NOVA Presentation ‣ Appendix G NorthCUA Case Studies ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
starts with an empty presentation and a first cover. To support the
performance slide, the agent computes mean signal-to-noise ratios from
five repeats in Calc. The resulting table and supplied spectra feed
native charts, which it embeds in Impress. Editable shapes and
connectors then form the five-stage signal-path diagram.

The agent reads back the embedded chart data to check that application
transfer preserved the calculation. The figure shows the same mean of
17.6268 at 5 ms in both views, making this check directly inspectable. A
presentation-mode inspection covers all six slides before the 691-step
record ends with an editable .odp, the calculation workbook, and a
matching PDF. The task thus preserves a concrete numerical result
through calculation, chart transfer, and final presentation. NOVA is a
fictional product concept with illustrative imagery and numerical data.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/nexcua_case_nova_presentation.png)

Figure 38: NorthCUA case study in Calc and Impress. Selected states
follow calculation, chart transfer, diagram construction, and data
readback. The chart at state 282 has just been inserted and is later
resized and positioned. The lower-left inset enlarges the native diagram
at state 687, while the large view shows the finished evidence slide at
state 688. Enlarged chart and data-table views connect the mean at state
136 to its embedded value at state 649. Numbers identify captured
states. Editorial overlays summarize nearby actions.

### G.4  CMOS Inverter Analysis

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/nexcua_case_virtuoso_spectre.png)

Figure 39: NorthCUA case study in Virtuoso and Spectre. Panel 2 pairs
device identity and size fields from the same native dialog at state 78.
Panel 6 enlarges the crossing at state 318, estimated by interpolation
between the 0.495 and 0.500 V sweep samples. Current annotations
transcribe the PMOS and NMOS reports at states 335 and 340,
respectively, both at nominal input 0.5 V. Numbers identify captured
states. Editorial overlays summarize nearby actions, with intervening
actions omitted.

The task asks for a 1 V CMOS inverter, its DC transfer curve, and
transistor operating-point checks in Virtuoso and Spectre. The
difficulty is carrying the intended device and analysis settings from
the schematic into the simulator, then connecting numerical readouts to
the conditions that produced them.
Figure [39](#A7.F39 "Figure 39 ‣ G.4 CMOS Inverter Analysis ‣ Appendix G NorthCUA Case Studies ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
begins with an empty schematic. The agent sizes the two transistors,
with the PMOS width and length fields shown in the figure. It then wires
and labels the circuit and checks the completed design before
configuring a 0–1 V input sweep with a 5 mV step. An uncommitted input
edit then exposes a subtle GUI-state mismatch. The first sweep completes
successfully, but the saved nominal input remains at 0 V. The agent
returns to ADE, enters 0.5 V, commits it with Enter, and reruns the
analysis so that the operating-point report uses the intended bias.

With the corrected configuration, the agent inspects the transfer curve
in ViVA and locates the crossing of input and output near 0.498 V. This
is an interpolated estimate from the 5 mV sweep. It separately reads
both device reports at the nominal 0.5 V input, where the drain currents
are $`+5.778\,\mu`$A and $`-5.778\,\mu`$A under the simulator’s sign
convention. The 367-request record ends with the native schematic,
simulator setup, waveform state, and operating-point evidence retained
for inspection. The case shows how a sustained GUI workflow can carry a
circuit through construction, configuration repair, and numerical
interpretation while preserving the engineering artifacts behind the
result. The supplied MOS1 models are constructed teaching models.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/nexcua_case_tidal_model.png)

Figure 40: NorthCUA case study in Inkscape and Blender. Twelve selected
states trace drawing, modeling, material revision, and camera keying.
Panel 2 is the exported sketch. Panels 8 and 12 pair viewport and
settings crops from their respective states. Editorial cues summarize
nearby actions. The compact final row combines native hero,
glass-detail, underside, and iris-detail renders from the same scene.
Intermediate actions are omitted.

### G.5  TIDAL-01 Modeling and Animation

TIDAL-01 asks for a silver-and-glass craft with a thin hull, swept
inlays, and an opening iris beneath the body. The challenge is carrying
this visual design from a two-dimensional drawing into editable geometry
whose separate parts can be animated and inspected from different
angles.
Figure [40](#A7.F40 "Figure 40 ‣ G.4 CMOS Inverter Analysis ‣ Appendix G NorthCUA Case Studies ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
begins with the top and side outlines in Inkscape, then shows the
completed three-view sketch. The agent places this drawing beside a
Blender blockout to match the thin side profile. It then shapes a swept
curve on the hull and fits three inlays to its surface. To animate the
underside opening, it assembles the iris from separately pivoted leaves
and keys their opening poses. The close view of the eight-leaf assembly
makes this change from static shape to articulated geometry visible.

An earlier render still shows pale, opaque inlays. The agent sets glass
transmission to 1.0, then inspects a later render to assess transparency
and contact with the hull. To refine the panel seats, it constructs
three matching cutter volumes by extruding and rotating the inlay
outlines. After further geometry work, an underside render exposes the
optical ring and open iris. With these parts established, it keys a
tracked camera arc that moves from the upper hull to the underside and
returns to a rear-quarter view. The final strip combines whole-object
views with glass and iris close-ups from the same editable scene,
connecting the sketch’s silhouette and underside layout to the rendered
object. Across 1,805 recorded steps, including observations, checks, and
retries, the workflow produces an original SVG, a packed .blend, and a
12-second native animation with 288 frames at 24 fps. Reopening the
packed scene reports no missing files. The case demonstrates a sustained
3D-authoring workflow in which geometric construction, material
revision, and camera control contribute to one reusable animated asset.

### G.6  Zuma Gameplay

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/nexcua_case_zuma.png)

Figure 41: Screenshot-driven Zuma gameplay. Panels follow row order. \#
denotes zero-based after-action IDs. Editorial aim guides precede
actions 0, 191, and 197. Paired ammunition crops straddle action 144.
L/R denotes mouse buttons. Intervening actions are omitted. The game
pauses between actions.

Turns and tunnels change the accessible targets in this six-color
Jaguar-level task.
Figure [41](#A7.F41 "Figure 41 ‣ G.6 Zuma Gameplay ‣ Appendix G NorthCUA Case Studies ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
begins with a purple match and progresses to a six-shot chain bonus. At
5,020 points, the progress bar turns green with balls still in play. The
agent swaps blue ammunition for green, clears the exposed pair, and
works through the bend to a short bottom-lane tail. It eliminates the
last purple and red groups, waits for the completion bonus, and reaches
native STATS with 9,070 points after 199 GUI actions.

## Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols

This appendix defines the runtime choices needed to interpret an
interaction record, including the observations presented to the policy,
the tools available to each role, environment ownership, and
demonstration selection. The learning-side segment and update rules are
consolidated in
Appendix [E.5](#A5.SS5 "E.5 Sequence Integrity and Update Boundaries ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

### H.1  Runtime Configuration

A context profile determines which retained interactions enter each
model call. The sliding visual profile counts the latest clean frame
within its image budget and keeps textual action/result records after
image eviction. Other profiles replay visual history within a compaction
epoch. Text windows, image budgets, and semantic compaction are separate
settings. The generic context manager defaults to off. Shadow mode
records decisions without applying them, and enforce mode applies its
configured policy.

Historical overlays mark issued actions on pre-action images, while the
latest clean frame shows the currently observed state. Text-only tools,
including notes, do not add visual frames. A later application change
can require waiting or a fresh observation. After a timeout or failed
refresh, state inspection precedes retry. Web element references belong
to the observed page state, and failed refreshes invalidate affected
aliases.

Compaction retains a structured account of the goal, constraints,
supported facts, completed work, failures, current state, open items,
and artifact references. Summaries and notes carry their temporal
provenance and remain distinguishable from observations. The summary
prompt, protected recent interactions, trigger/target budgets, and
failure policy determine the resulting input. Note categories are fact,
correction, precaution, constraint, decision, and checkpoint.
Compatibility names map mistake to correction and plan/other to
checkpoint. The exposed schema, replacement behavior, and prompt budget
control which notes remain visible.

##### Execution scopes and tool interfaces.

A session binds the agent to its environment and resources. A round
tracks one request and execution budget, and a step records one
policy-decision and action-execution iteration. A step can contain
several tool calls, so model decisions, tool calls, and GUI interactions
are different counts. The dispatcher validates declared arguments before
invoking local handlers or connected MCP tools and returns results to
the requesting step.
Appendix [H.2](#A8.SS2 "H.2 Action Spaces and Parameter Schemas ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
specifies the model-facing interfaces. Runtime probes use a separate
permission profile from actor tools.

##### Evaluator profiles and attribution.

Evaluation binds a task and rubric to an evidence source, inspection
permissions, budget, and report schema. The rubric-generation template
requests three to seven criteria. The Agent Kit desktop entry path can
use a request-level fallback, while the web profile supplies eight fixed
criteria. The selected profile determines which criteria, weights,
applicability rules, and reward conversion apply. Report-format
validation checks the report’s structure.

Artifact tools inspect retained records. Live tools additionally require
authorization and a dispatcher bound to the evaluated workspace.
Artifact-only replay has no live device access. Read-only probes are
preferred. Shell inspection uses an allowlist, and state-changing GUI or
browser inspection requires explicit permission. The workspace remains
reserved through required live checks.

Actor credit depends on the state before evaluator intervention.
Terminal evidence and evaluator actions are therefore recorded
separately. Inspection checks the submitted artifact or page state. A
potentially mutating check needs a procedure that preserves the original
state, such as a prepared evaluation copy. When that basis cannot be
preserved, assessment uses retained evidence or leaves the condition
unresolved. Probe failure, conflicting evidence, and an exhausted
inspection budget are distinct from a supported task-failure verdict.
When evaluator protection is enabled, recognized evaluation failures
remain unresolved without replacing the actor’s terminal status.

### H.2  Action Spaces and Parameter Schemas

The desktop and web instances in
Section [3.1.3](#S3.SS1.SSS3 "3.1.3 Desktop and Web Instantiations ‣ 3.1 Unified Execution Across Desktop and Web ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
expose different tools through the same dispatcher. The tables below
summarize their model-facing contracts and the evaluator interfaces in
Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Agentic Outcome Evaluation ‣ 3NorthCUA Harness: Full-Lifecycle Infrastructure for Computer Use ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
Fields with neither a default nor a question mark are required. A
question mark marks an optional field with no supplied value, and T\[\]
denotes an array. Empty arguments are written as {}. These are tool
signatures, independent of provider-specific call envelopes.
Runtime-only probes and environment-setup commands are outside the
acting agents’ action spaces.

#### H.2.1  Desktop Agent

Desktop actions address the current screen and focused application.
Coordinates follow the run’s declared coordinate policy, which maps the
model’s image coordinates to device coordinates. A drag point has the
schema {x: number, y: number}. The button argument accepts left, right,
or middle.

[TABLE]

Table 21: Desktop agent action space. Defaults and argument constraints
are shown explicitly.

The note categories are fact, mistake, precaution, plan, constraint,
decision, and other. These exposed names map to the working-memory
categories described in
Appendix [H.1](#A8.SS1 "H.1 Runtime Configuration ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
The same note fields apply to web_note below.

#### H.2.2  Web Agent

Web actions can use element references from the latest page observation
or viewport-relative CSS coordinates. In
Table [22](#A8.T22 "Table 22 ‣ H.2.2 Web Agent ‣ H.2 Action Spaces and Parameter Schemas ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
Ref is a string or integer element label. References are refreshed with
page state, while tab_ref identifies a browser tab. Point actions use
nonnegative CSS coordinates measured from the current viewport’s
top-left corner. These differ from full-page or resized-image
coordinates.

[TABLE]

Table 22: Web agent action space. Each row gives one tool’s arguments
and operation.

#### H.2.3  Evaluator

The evaluator retrieves recorded evidence and, when authorized, inspects
the evaluated workspace through live tools.
Table [23](#A8.T23 "Table 23 ‣ H.2.3 Evaluator ‣ H.2 Action Spaces and Parameter Schemas ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
separates these interfaces. The active desktop or web profile selects
the applicable tools. Evidence references identify saved artifacts,
independently of the web agent’s element references. Retrieval tools
with detail_level use standard by default and accept debug for expanded
records.

[TABLE]

Table 23: Evaluator inspection interfaces. Live tools require access to
the evaluated workspace and the corresponding permissions.

Authorized desktop GUI inspection reuses the schemas in
Table [21](#A8.T21 "Table 21 ‣ H.2.1 Desktop Agent ‣ H.2 Action Spaces and Parameter Schemas ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
through client_gui_screenshot, client_gui_click, client_gui_type,
client_gui_keypress, and client_gui_wait. Additional browser operations
use the matching client_browser\_ wrappers and schemas from
Table [22](#A8.T22 "Table 22 ‣ H.2.2 Web Agent ‣ H.2 Action Spaces and Parameter Schemas ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report"),
excluding web_note. State-changing inspection requires explicit
permission and follows the attribution protocol in
Appendix [H.1](#A8.SS1.SSS0.Px2 "Evaluator profiles and attribution. ‣ H.1 Runtime Configuration ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

Evaluation ends with final_report, whose required fields appear in
Table [24](#A8.T24 "Table 24 ‣ H.2.3 Evaluator ‣ H.2 Action Spaces and Parameter Schemas ‣ Appendix H Runtime and Admission Protocols ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
Optional structured fields carry criterion scores, diagnoses, side
effects, safety and efficiency assessments, and evidence-linked
divergence information. The web report additionally records the final
URL and title, verification flags, a blocker category, and critical
evidence references.

|  Field |  Type and allowed values |
|----|----|
|  overall_verdict |  Enum success, partial, failure, or uncertain. |
|  outcome_label |  Enum success, failure, or uncertain. |
|  process_rating |  Integer from 1 to 5 rating execution quality. |
|  completion_rating |  Integer from 1 to 5 rating task completion. |
|  confidence_rating |  Integer from 1 to 5 rating confidence in the assessment. |
|  controllability |  Enum controllable, uncontrollable, mixed, or unknown. |
|  reason |  String giving the evidence-grounded rationale. |

Table 24: Required fields of the evaluator’s final_report argument
object. Platform-specific and diagnostic fields extend this shared core.

### H.3  Batch Collection and Resource Ownership

Task preparation binds CaseSpec to its inputs, initialization procedure,
application/tool profile, budgets, and optional post-run checks.
Placement considers configured evaluator requirements as well as actor
requirements. Resumed work uses the original task and configuration
identity. Supported context-managed settings are retained in batch-local
snapshots, while external dependencies keep their own version records.

Fresh single-use sandboxes isolate new collection attempts.
Task-specific fixtures also cover external accounts and service state.
An optional prepared queue, disabled by library default, performs
initialization ahead of execution. Reservations are
task/attempt-specific and assigned once. On-demand acquisition is a
separate fallback. Prepared queues and warm pools have different
allocation and reuse rules. An externally owned environment retains its
own termination authority.

Creation, preparation, execution, submission, and status-query limits
govern different resources. Assignment checks the remaining provider
lifetime against the task timeout and release margin. Attempt
identifiers link scheduler state to controller state, and reconciliation
checks an existing attempt before starting another. A scheduler claim
and a device execution lease represent distinct ownership decisions.

Live evaluation and environment-resident output capture finish before
owned resources are released. Once a sandbox is destroyed, replay is
limited to retained artifacts. Unconfirmed cleanup remains unresolved
and continues to incur resource cost until destruction is confirmed.
This lifecycle distinguishes transport disconnection from environment
termination.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  Responsibility |  Supervised collection |  Online RL |
|  Acting policy |  Configured demonstration policy |  Current policy being optimized |
|  Scheduling |  Standalone batch service |  Training-side group orchestration |
|  Environment lifetime |  Owned sandboxes released after required checks and capture |  External orchestrator retains release authority |
|  Learning output |  Selected trajectories and role-specific demonstrations |  Attempt outcomes and captured calls for task groups |

Table 25: Shared execution interfaces with different collection and
training lifecycle owners.

### H.4  Cohort Admission

Outcome-verified completion demonstrations require actor-only
extraction, valid action/context alignment, required artifacts,
full-task acceptance, and the cohort’s quality checks. Broad
judge-filtered mid-training uses its separately declared selector, and
evaluator demonstrations are separate judgment-task samples
(Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Supervised Representation and Sampling ‣ Appendix E Model Identities and Learning Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")).
Online RL retains valid negative outcomes within task groups.

|  |  |
|----|----|
|  Attempt evidence |  Completion-demonstration disposition |
|  Verified complete with quality checks |  Eligible. Retain source and outcome evidence. |
|  Supported infeasibility or uncontrollable blocker |  Excluded. Retain separately if used. |
|  Partial or valid failed outcome |  Excluded. Retain as failure/diagnostic experience if declared. |
|  Uncertain verdict or conflicting evidence |  Unresolved. Assessment is required. |
|  Required evaluation error or missing outcome evidence |  Not admitted as a verified completion |

Table 26: Selection distinguishes task outcomes from missing or invalid
evaluation and defines completion-demonstration eligibility.

## Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details

Section [6.1](#S6.SS1 "6.1 Evaluation Setup ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
introduces the six benchmarks. This appendix collects the metric
definitions, task sets, and implementation settings used to interpret
the results, together with the sources of published baseline scores.

##### Comparable tasks and interfaces.

Interactive evaluation uses the benchmark’s scorer independently of the
harness evaluator used for collection. OSWorld-Verified uses the
supported 361-task setting without the eight Google Drive tasks. Our
OSWorld 2.0 binary-completion and partial scores both use the full
108-task set. Grounding records identify the OSWorld-G instruction
variant and absent-target convention, and map crop/resize predictions
back to annotation coordinates.

WebArena-Verified retains the responses and network traces required by
its scorer. Our WebTestBench evaluation supplies the gold checklist
directly, following the benchmark’s oracle setting. Agents return
Pass/Fail judgments, with Fail as the positive class. Precision, recall,
and F1 measure defect detection. The gold checklist fixes the inspection
scope for defect detection. We first compute precision, recall, and F1
from the checklist judgments within each task, then average each metric
over all evaluated tasks. For task-level scores
$`P_{t},R_{t},F_{1,t}\in[0,1]`$ across $`N`$ tasks, the reported
percentages are

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\overline{P}=\frac{100}{N}\sum_{t=1}^{N}P_{t},\qquad\overline{R}=\frac{100}{N}\sum_{t=1}^{N}R_{t},\qquad\overline{F}_{1}=\frac{100}{N}\sum_{t=1}^{N}F_{1,t}.
``` |  | (6) |

This task-level macro average gives equal weight to tasks with different
checklist lengths. It differs from pooling all checklist judgments into
one confusion matrix. In particular, $`\overline{F}_{1}`$ averages the
per-task F1 scores and generally differs from the harmonic mean of
$`\overline{P}`$ and $`\overline{R}`$.
Table [11](#S6.T11 "Table 11 ‣ Web testing. ‣ 6.4 Web Agent Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
reports all three metrics for each included model and omits models
without measured results. The NorthCUA Nano, Mini, and Pro results all
use final checkpoints after joint desktop–web RL.

The source notes below record the task scope, tool permissions, budgets,
and scoring conventions of published baselines. Parameter columns
distinguish total from activated language-model counts. Within each
NorthCUA tier, stage comparisons use the declared benchmark tasks,
tools, observations, and execution budgets.

##### Repeated predictions.

Table [29](#A9.T29 "Table 29 ‣ I.1 Grounding Scores and Repeated Sampling ‣ Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
summarizes repeated predictions on the same grounding examples,
including run-to-run variation and pass@3 ([Dror et al.,
2018](#bib.bib18)). The accompanying task-level breakdowns explain
coverage and execution behavior. Showcase demonstrations are described
separately from model checkpoint evaluation.

screenshot.

Pro. This makes the coverage differences visible without changing the
task distribution between models.

The paired Nano–Mini predictions show 1,086 items correct for both
models, 375 correct only for Mini, 66 correct only for Nano, and 54
missed by both. Mini locates 375 of Nano’s 429 misses, but each model
also has unique successes. For Mini and Pro, 1,373 items are correct for
both, 102 only for Pro, 88 only for Mini, and 18 for neither. Pro’s net
gain of 14 correct items therefore coexists with distinct successes from
each model. The paired counts distinguish shared errors from
checkpoint-specific successes.

Pro’s remaining 106 errors all produce valid coordinates outside the
target box. Icons account for 59 errors, including 46 in the 16–$`<32`$
pixel band. That band contains 418 icon targets, of which 11.0% are
missed, and contributes 43.4% of Pro’s errors overall. On icons smaller
than 16 pixels, Pro reaches 93.0% accuracy. Across application groups,
Pro gains most over Mini in CAD (3.1 points) and scientific software
(2.4 points), while development software is 1.0 point lower.

OSWorld-G Refined uses temperature 0.7, top-$`p`$ 0.95, top-$`k`$ 40,
and a 1,024-token output limit.

On SSP, Pro raises single-run accuracy by 0.9 percentage points over
Mini and reaches 95.6% pass@3, 2.3 points above its single-run result.

The Refined records contain 564 unique item IDs in each run, with
matched annotations across all three models and all three draws. Of
these, 510 have visible targets, comprising 470 rectangular and 40
polygonal reference regions. The other 54 require an absent-target
refusal. Visible-target accuracy uses the corresponding region test, and
refusal accuracy uses the benchmark’s designated no-target output.

All three tiers share the grounding system prompt, sampling settings,
and coordinate scoring. Nano and Mini use a grounding-specific click
schema that describes normalized coordinates and refusal, requires x, y,
and button, and enumerates button choices. Pro uses the frozen NorthCUA
desktop click schema, whose descriptions refer to desktop interaction
and whose button argument is optional. The reported tier differences
therefore describe these evaluated model and interface configurations.

Table [30](#A9.T30 "Table 30 ‣ I.1 Grounding Scores and Repeated Sampling ‣ Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")

467, 460, and 468 correct predictions, while Mini’s contain 488, 496,
and 492, and Pro’s contain 504, 509, and 513. All runs have zero request
errors and unparsed predictions. Pro’s second run includes one
separately completed missing item, yielding the same 564 unique IDs as
the other runs. This completion contributes one prediction for that item
to run 2 and adds no extra draw to pass@3.

Pro’s 34 persistently missed items contribute 102 of its 166 incorrect
predictions (61.5%). Its remaining 41 items vary across draws, while 489
succeed in all three. Mini and Pro share 17 persistent misses, and 16
items remain persistently incorrect across all three model tiers. These
counts show that Pro reduces the recurring error set while leaving a
smaller common subset unresolved. The 1,692 predictions are repeated
measurements of 564 items across 251 screenshots.

groups use the task’s site set, pooling multi-site combinations into one
group. Outcome types follow the evaluator’s expected category. Each
grouping therefore partitions the same task set and reveals a different
aspect of task completion.

|  Task group |  Tasks |       NorthCUA-Nano  passes |       NorthCUA-Mini  passes |       NorthCUA-Pro  passes |
| Website scope |  |  |  |  |
|  GitLab only |  180 |  115 |  126 |  131 |
|  Map only |  109 |  67 |  71 |  77 |
|  Forum only |  106 |  87 |  85 |  89 |
|  Shopping only |  187 |  123 |  123 |  135 |
|  Store administration only |  182 |  122 |  135 |  151 |
|  Cross-site |  48 |  7 |  12 |  17 |
| Expected task type |  |  |  |  |
````
