````math
Table 32: WebArena-Verified task counts for Nano SFT, Mini SFT, and Pro
RL. Website scope and expected task type each partition the same 812
tasks. Forum denotes Reddit, and state change denotes the evaluator’s
MUTATE category.

Mini’s additional completions over Nano concentrate in state-changing
and retrieval tasks, with 13 and 19 more successes, respectively, while
their navigation counts are nearly identical. Pro reaches 75.4% on
retrieval, 73.3% on state change, and 71.7% on navigation. Cross-site
work is more demanding for all three checkpoints, with 7, 12, and 17
successes out of 48 tasks. In comparison, Pro completes 76.3% of the 764
single-site tasks.

Mini’s records show how this difference relates to execution length
within one checkpoint. It completes 540 of 764 single-site tasks (70.7%)
and 12 of 48 cross-site tasks (25.0%). Their median lengths are 10 and
27.5 decisions, respectively. Even among successful tasks, the
cross-site median is 17 decisions, compared with nine for single-site
work. These tasks combine lower completion with longer interactions,
making them a useful focus for experience that trains the agent to carry
constraints and intermediate results between websites.

Table [33](#A9.T33 "Table 33 ‣ I.2 Interactive Results and Trajectory Breakdowns ‣ Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
summarizes decision counts by evaluator outcome. Each record contributes
its current assistant response once, including the final response. Nano
has 811 traced tasks and one untraced failure, while Mini and Pro each
have 812. All 812 tasks enter each model’s success-rate denominator.
Quartiles use linear interpolation at $`(n-1)p`$ for percentile fraction
$`p`$.

Successful tasks have similar median lengths of 9, 10, and 10 decisions
for Nano, Mini, and Pro. Failed tasks have longer medians of 19, 18, and
15.5 decisions and contribute 58.5%, 50.6%, and 38.4% of their recorded
decisions, respectively. For Mini, this is 7,698 of 15,224 decisions
across 260 failures. Separating outcomes thus exposes the
disproportionate execution devoted to unsuccessful tasks. At the same
time, Pro completes 24 tasks with at least 50 decisions, including a
93-decision success, showing that extended execution can also support
successful completion.

|                 |           |          |        |          |           |
|-----------------|-----------|----------|--------|----------|-----------|
|  Model          |  Outcome  |  Traces  |  Mean  |  Median  |  Q25–Q75  |
|  NorthCUA-Nano  |  Passed   |  521     |  13.4  |  9       |  7–16     |
|                 |  Failed   |  290     |  33.9  |  19      |  10–44    |
|  NorthCUA-Mini  |  Passed   |  552     |  13.6  |  10      |  7–16     |
|                 |  Failed   |  260     |  29.6  |  18      |  10–36.3  |
|  NorthCUA-Pro   |  Passed   |  600     |  15.0  |  10      |  7–17.3   |
|                 |  Failed   |  212     |  26.5  |  15.5    |  10–33.3  |

Table 33: Decision records by task outcome for Nano SFT, Mini SFT, and
Pro RL. Counts include terminal responses. Nano uses the archived record
boundary, which includes interrupted execution, while Mini and Pro use
one retained attempt per task.

Mini likewise completes 13 tasks with at least 50 decisions, with its
longest success taking 75. Across all three checkpoints, successful-task
means exceed their medians, reflecting a tail of extended but productive
execution. This profile favors training examples that retain successful
long trajectories alongside concise workflows. In our learning pipeline,
outcome-based selection retains successful long workflows for
supervision, while RL also uses unsuccessful attempts to provide
negative feedback.

The browser case in
Figure [31](#S6.F31 "Figure 31 ‣ 6.7 An Evaluated Browser Recovery Case ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
is task 1 in the Pro export. Its 13 decisions include an
unavailable-option error, inspection of the actual dropdown choices, and
successful completion after revising the report settings. The
screenshots show how inspecting the available choices resolves the error
and lets the agent finish the report query.

The desktop checkpoint summaries cover 361 OSWorld-Verified tasks with a
100-step limit and the full 108-task OSWorld 2.0 set with a 500-step
limit. The OSWorld-Verified main table uses the RL checkpoints at 78.9%,
85.3%, and 88.6% for Nano, Mini, and Pro.
Tables [12](#S6.T12 "Table 12 ‣ 6.5 Progress Across Training Stages ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
and [13](#S6.T13 "Table 13 ‣ Task completion across platforms. ‣ 6.6 Joint Desktop–Web Reinforcement Learning ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
retain the earlier checkpoints for stage comparisons. On OSWorld 2.0,
the final RL checkpoints have Binary/Partial scores of 13.0/45.6 for
Nano, 18.5/50.1 for Mini, and 30.6/66.8 for Pro. The Mid pairs are
0.9/24.2, 2.8/25.6, and 9.3/30.6, respectively. The long-horizon SFT
checkpoints score 12.0/40.9, 18.5/46.3, and 27.3/63.6 in the same tier
order. These SFT checkpoints serve as the Before-RL baselines in
Table [13](#S6.T13 "Table 13 ‣ Task completion across platforms. ‣ 6.6 Joint Desktop–Web Reinforcement Learning ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").
The binary and partial scores describe full completion and intermediate
progress on the same tasks.

### I.3  External Comparison Settings

Table [34](#A9.T34 "Table 34 ‣ Grounding and browser references. ‣ I.3 External Comparison Settings ‣ Appendix I Benchmark Evaluation Details ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
summarizes the evaluation settings of published specialist and
general-purpose models. Scores retain their reported task scopes,
metrics, and aggregation rules.

##### Additional OSWorld-Verified references.

Table [8](#S6.T8 "Table 8 ‣ 6.3 Desktop Agent Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
extends the comparison across model generations and specialist families.
OpenCUA-72B’s 45.0% is the 100-step, three-run mean in its final
conference paper ([Wang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib83)). GUI-Owl-1.5 uses
the 8B and 32B Instruct checkpoints ([Xu et al., 2026b](#bib.bib93)),
while UI-Venus-2 contributes its 9B and 27B computer-use models ([Venus
Team, 2026b](#bib.bib76)). These are distinct from UI-Venus-1.5 in the
grounding comparison. MiniMax M3’s 75.2% and Seed 2.1 Pro’s 78.8% follow
the Qwen-UI-Agent comparison. Qwen3.8-Max’s 86.1% and GPT-5.6 Sol’s
83.2% follow the Qwen3.8 release ([Zhou et al., 2026b](#bib.bib110);
[Qwen Team, 2026c](#bib.bib62)). The Nex-N2.5 release adds 76.7% for
DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Vision on OSWorld-Verified. It also reports
WebArena-Verified success of 71.6% for Kimi-K3 and 62.3% for
DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Vision ([Nex-AGI, 2026b](#bib.bib51)). We retain the
release’s DeepSeek model label.

The benchmark authors separately report 86.0% for Claude Fable 5 \[1m\]
on 358 scored tasks and 83.4% for Claude Opus 5 \[1m\] on 360, both with
100 steps ([XLANG Lab, 2026b](#bib.bib91)). Google’s Gemini 3.6 Flash
card reports 83.0%, using a five-run mean with 100 steps ([Google
DeepMind, 2026b](#bib.bib25)).

GLM-5.3-Flash’s 84.7% is an independent public evaluation by [hojuna
(2026)](#bib.bib30), with released code and trajectories. It reports
305.0 earned credits over 360 tasks after excluding one grader failure
from the non-Google-Drive set. The run uses screenshot observations,
PyAutoGUI, a 100-step budget, and no task retries. The main-table
citation identifies the model, while the source above identifies this
independent evaluation.

Our DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash evaluation yields 71.7% on the 361-task
OSWorld-Verified setting with a 100-step limit. Its starred entry in
Table [8](#S6.T8 "Table 8 ‣ 6.3 Desktop Agent Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
is separate from the published DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Vision result.

##### OSWorld 2.0 task scopes.

Our GLM-5.3-Flash evaluation uses the full 108-task set and a 500-step
budget, yielding 14.8% binary completion and 48.7% partial credit. The
star in
Table [9](#S6.T9 "Table 9 ‣ 6.3 Desktop Agent Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
identifies this measurement as our evaluation, with the model citation
identifying the tested system. For GPT-5.6 Sol and Claude Opus 5,
Table [9](#S6.T9 "Table 9 ‣ 6.3 Desktop Agent Results ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
uses the official full 108-task v2026.08.08 results, evaluated with
batched actions, maximum effort, and 500 steps ([XLANG Lab,
2026a](#bib.bib90)). GPT-5.6 Sol averages 27.3% binary and 62.7% partial
over two runs. Claude Opus 5 averages 31.4% and 68.3% over seven runs.
These references match our full-set task scope, while their execution
settings and aggregation remain source-specific. OpenAI’s 65.7% GPT-5.6
Sol result uses the 82-task offline subset ([OpenAI,
2026d](#bib.bib55)). MiniMax’s release comparison reports 70.6% for
Claude Opus 5 without identifying its task subset ([MiniMax,
2026a](#bib.bib45)). Both alternative scores are omitted from the main
table in favor of the full-set pairs. The earlier GPT-5.5 and Claude
Opus 4.8 pairs use the official June full-set release, also at 500 steps
with batched actions. UI-Venus-2 uses 108 tasks with a shorter 150-step
budget ([Venus Team, 2026b](#bib.bib76)).

Claude Fable 5 and Fable 5.1 report binary/partial pairs of 36.1%/72.9%
and 41.7%/77.9%, respectively, on 108 tasks ([Anthropic,
2026a](#bib.bib3)). Binary corresponds to the source’s strict pass rate.
Both are five-run means at maximum effort with a 500-step budget and
1080p observations. They use the August 2026 task release with
subsequent task, setup, and grading fixes, so their scores differ in
protocol from earlier releases. Safeguard interventions count as
failures.

MiniMax’s comparison also reports partial scores of 61.7% for MiniMax
M3.1 Flash, 54.3% for DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, and 58.3% for Kimi-K3
([MiniMax, 2026a](#bib.bib45)). These remain additional published
references, with task versions and subsets unspecified. Corresponding
binary results are unavailable for these reported settings and remain
dashes. We retain DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash without transferring its score to
the later 1017 variant. DeepSeek’s size entry uses 552B backbone
parameters and 16B activated during decoding. Its model card separately
reports 8B activated during prefill and 196B of additional Engram memory
([DeepSeek-AI, 2026a](#bib.bib15)).

Other reports provide both metrics without an explicit task count.
Qwen3.8-Max reports 19.4% binary and 46.7% partial, while Qwen-CUA,
Qwen-CUA-Max, and Qwen-UI-Agent retain their published pairs ([Qwen
Team, 2026c](#bib.bib62); [Lu et al., 2026](#bib.bib41); [Zhou et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib110)). Gemini 3.8 Flash’s 59.0% partial score is the
maximum of three 500-step benchmark runs on the pre-August-patch setting
([Google DeepMind, 2026c](#bib.bib26)).

The later Opus 5.5 system card reports 48.7% binary and 81.8% partial
for Opus 5.5, alongside 42.8%/80.7% for Fable 5.1 ([Anthropic,
2026e](#bib.bib7)). These five-run means use 108 tasks and a 500-step
budget, but update the task assets to September 10, 2026 and retain
screenshots until server-side context compaction. The release
announcement labels this revision OSWorld 2.1 ([Anthropic,
2026d](#bib.bib6)). We keep these later-revision results separate from
the OSWorld 2.0 main table because both the task files and harness
configuration changed.

The other starred rows in

GLM-5.3-Flash, and Gemini 4 Argon. Their model citations identify the

For browser specialists, Qwen-UI-Agent reports original-WebArena success

These results use the original WebArena setting. Likewise, OpenGCUA

In

3.1 Pro, and GPT-5.6 Sol scores come from the August Qwen3.8-Max release

that release, including Claude Fable 5 and GPT-5.6 Sol. Qwen3.8-Max’s
2.4T total and 95B active parameters describe its language-model
backbone ([Qwen Team, 2026d](#bib.bib63)). The evaluated hosted model
supports vision, while the downloadable Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B checkpoint is

The starred GPT-6 Astra, GPT-6.1 Sol, Claude Opus 5.5, Gemini 4 Argon,
GLM-5.3-Flash, and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash entries are our WebArena-Verified

stars distinguish these measurements from the published reference
scores. The main table reports task success, with stage-specific trace
accounting above. The Mid-to-SFT comparison appears in
Section [6.5](#S6.SS5 "6.5 Progress Across Training Stages ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report").

### I.4  Web Repair Evaluation Protocol

The recorded case in
Figure [18](#S4.F18 "Figure 18 ‣ Case study of a responsive homepage repair. ‣ 4.2.2 Scaling Environments ‣ 4.2 Scaling Intents, Environments, and Verifiers ‣ 4Experience Flywheel: Scaling Grounding and Agentic Experience ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
illustrates browser feedback and code repair. A controlled repair
comparison additionally fixes the issue set, starting patch, Coding
Agent, test generator, adjudicator, budgets, and protected acceptance
conditions. Role-specific information remains separate even when roles
share model weights.

Each assigned issue begins with the nominated defect reproduced. Before
a new condition or patch is tested, browser storage, account/backend
data, service-worker state, and external fixtures are restored.
Intentional stateful test dependencies remain explicit. Reports bind the
build, reset state, test item, and attempt. Only evidence-supported
product discrepancies justify a repair.

Acceptance uses the common assigned issue set as its denominator,
retaining tester noncompletion. Infrastructure-invalid attempts follow
the same bounded rerun rule, and unresolved issues remain in the
results. Final acceptance runs the submitted patch from a protected
clean state outside the repair loop, with its outputs withheld from
further revision.

## Appendix J OSWorld 2.0 Execution Cases

These cases follow NorthCUA through selected decisions in complete
desktop tasks. Counts refer to model decisions, each of which can issue
multiple tools. Agent callouts summarize recorded decisions for
readability.

The pair in
Figure [28](#S6.F28 "Figure 28 ‣ 6.5 Progress Across Training Stages ‣ 6Experiments and Analysis ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
has identical requests but different dataset revisions. The earlier run
records unavailable OCR, while the later run uses it. The case follows
source selection and verification in each execution.

### J.1  Preserving a Delivery Constraint Across Long Contexts

The photo-delivery task in
Figure [42](#A10.F42 "Figure 42 ‣ J.1 Preserving a Delivery Constraint Across Long Contexts ‣ Appendix J OSWorld 2.0 Execution Cases ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
requires the agent to reconcile invoices against bank payments, identify
two clients’ photos, and send different deliveries. Sarah has paid her
\$800 invoice in full and should receive her complete gallery. Emily has
paid \$250 of a \$500 invoice and should receive a watermarked
solo-indoor preview with a balance reminder. The difficulty is carrying
this distinction through image preparation and email composition after
the original banking interaction leaves recent context.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/osworld2_context_case.png)

Figure 42: A payment constraint retained through a long-context task.
Stages follow note 46, compacted input 150, the three image inputs at
decision 233, and delivery checks. Historical action marks are
preserved. Text artifacts are abridged and the agent callout summarizes
a recorded decision. Screenshot crops precede the indexed decisions,
with enlarged details.

After exact-name payment matching, the agent records Emily’s outstanding
\$250 and preview-only rule at decision 46. The compacted input at
decision 150 retains both and the unfinished delivery checks, carrying
the payment constraint beyond the earlier interaction history.

At decision 233, two action-marked historical screenshots identify the
attachment click and selected PNG, while the clean current frame shows
the file attached to Emily’s draft. The agent sends the message and
checks its sent copy at decision 236, confirming the preview and \$250
reminder. A final image inspection confirms the watermark, and the
243-decision attempt receives full credit. Notes and compaction preserve
the constraint, while the bounded image window exposes the current
attachment state.

### J.2  Using a Terminal Through the GUI

Figure [43](#A10.F43 "Figure 43 ‣ J.2 Using a Terminal Through the GUI ‣ Appendix J OSWorld 2.0 Execution Cases ‣ NorthCUA Technical Report")
follows a 3D course-logo task requiring Bauhaus 93 text, a 120-frame
rotation, and a 960 $`\times`$ 540 WebM video at 24 fps inside Impress.
To control scene settings and automate repeated rendering, the agent
opens a terminal through GUI keypresses and types commands. Its exposed
tools are desktop actions and notes, and the task permits terminal and
application-console use.

![Refer to caption](2611.02102v1/osworld2_terminal_case.png)

Figure 43: GUI-mediated scripting and recovery in an animation task.
Each numbered phase reads top to bottom. Callouts paraphrase recorded
decisions. Screenshots show cropped pre-decision observations with
enlarged diagnostic details. Intermediate interactions are omitted.

After opening a terminal at decision 3 to identify fonts, the agent
builds the scene in Blender’s scripting workspace. When direct video
export rejects WEBM and FFMPEG settings, it renders 120 PNG frames and
encodes them with terminal-based ffmpeg using VP9 at 24 fps, preserving
the 960 $`\times`$ 540 resolution. Separating rendering from encoding
lets it complete the repeated frame processing programmatically.

After checking video properties, the agent positions the video in
Impress and starts the slideshow. Observations 143 and 144 show changing
logo orientations. The task receives full credit after 147 decisions and
253 tool calls, including four notes. GUI-accessible scripting handles
the repetitive work, and GUI playback verifies the result.
````
